  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   Client has recently altered employment status from employed to self-employed with the same company, starting 2026-03-24. Intended retirement age is 60. Expected annual earnings around £45,000 gross going forward. No longer entitled to employee benefits such as pension contributions; Nest plan will become paid up. Report recommends considering commencing regular contributions to new pension.
    File:     Fact Find (P3, P7) records: Melanie has now stopped working through self-employment as of the fact-find update in July 2026, frustrated with work and disagreements with boss, decided to cut ties. Will look to return to part-time work in new year with forecasted wage of £25,000. Current employment status field blank in fact find. Meeting Summary (P14, P17) confirms plan to retire at age 60. Cashflow assumptions (P10) model £45,000 annual earnings until age 60. Earlier employment as accountant noted (P8). No current employment details are populated in the structured employment section of fact find (P7).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states employment starts 2026-03-24 as self-employed; Fact Find (P3, P7) update July 2026 states Melanie 'has now stopped working through self employment' - the present perfect tense indicates self-employment has already ended by July 2026, contradicting the March start date assertion
      - Report asserts expected annual earnings 'around £45,000 gross going forward'; Fact Find (P3) forecasts return to part-time work with 'forecasted wage of £25,000', which is substantially lower than £45,000
      - Cashflow model (P10) assumes £45,000 per annum income; this contradicts the Fact Find forecast of £25,000 for part-time work
      - Report states client 'will no longer be entitled to employee benefits' and 'Nest plan will become paid up' implying active self-employment; Fact Find states she has already stopped self-employment and is currently not working, with future part-time work planned
    The employment details contain material inconsistencies between what the report asserts and what the supporting evidence records. The report presents the employment status change as a forward-looking transition ('You have recently altered your employment status, going from employed to self-employed') dated 2026-03-24, with earnings of £45,000 gross. However, the Fact Find dated July 2026 (P3, P7) explicitly states in past tense 'Melanie has now stopped working through self employment' and 'she decided to cut ties with it all', indicating the self-employment had already ended by the time of that update. This is not merely a timing difference: the report treats self-employment as the current and continuing status, whereas the evidence shows it had ceased. The income figure also diverges significantly: the report's cashflow assumes £45,000 annual earnings (P10), but the Fact Find forecasts only £25,000 for the intended part-time work. The report does not acknowledge this income reduction or reconcile the £45,000 assumption with the stated part-time work plan. The statement that the client 'will no longer be entitled to employee benefits' and 'Nest plan will become paid up' presupposes active self-employment; the evidence shows she is currently unemployed and seeking part-time work, making the present status unclear and the benefits statement potentially outdated. The intended retirement age of 60 is consistently corroborated across all sources (P1, P2, P10, P17). The occupation is noted as accounting background (P7, P8) but not formally updated in the employment status section. These discrepancies go to the core financial assumptions underlying the suitability analysis, particularly the income available for contributions and retirement planning.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts: self-employed income £45,000 gross annually until age 60; partner Tony contributes £600/month net to household bills; State Pension £230.21/week from age 67; household expenditure £2,986/month; personal expenditure in retirement £2,000/month net; one-off sofa purchase £2,500; mortgage repayment £750/month for 7 years. Cashflow assumptions state expenditure £2,237/month until retirement.
    File:     Fact Find (P1) records: total monthly household expenditure £2,836.23; net monthly income £800.00 (which matches partner contribution £600 plus other income £200). Meeting Summary (P17) notes: discretionary spending including gym £40-50/month, holidays £3,000-5,000 annually, entertainment £150/month, mortgage insurance £30/month, mortgage principal overpayment £250/month. Budget notes (P12) state overall expenses in region of £2,800; pension income target £1,900/month plus partner contribution £800 = £2,700/month to cover expenditure; after debt settlement will reduce outgoings by circa £480/month. Mortgage payment in liability table (P11) shown as £600/month. Air pump cost mentioned (P17) as £120/month for sustainable living feature.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states household expenditure £2,986/month but Fact Find records £2,836.23/month—difference of £149.77 (5.6% variance, exceeds 5% tolerance)
      - Report states cashflow assumes expenditure £2,237/month until retirement (P5) but current household expenditure documented as £2,836.23 (P1) or region of £2,800 (P12)—difference of £599-£836/month representing 27-37% reduction with no documented reason
      - Report states personal expenditure in retirement £2,000/month net but budget notes indicate pension income need £1,900/month plus partner £800 = £2,700/month required to cover current £2,800 expenditure—inconsistent with £2,000 retirement figure
      - Report states Tony contributes £600/month but mortgage payment shown separately as £750/month in solution design (P5) and as £600/month in liability table (P11)—discrepancy of £150/month on mortgage amount itself
      - Partner contribution documented as both £600/month net towards bills (report) but Fact Find (P4) shows 'Partner's Contribution' £800.00 at time of completion, not £600
      - Meeting notes (P17) identify additional expenses not clearly reconciled: air pump £120/month, mortgage overpayment £250/month, gym £40-50/month—these appear absent from the consolidated £2,986 figure
      - Report assumes £2,500 sofa purchase is one-off but Meeting notes (P17) indicate this was charged to credit card recently and is being paid from savings, yet liability table shows credit card on 0% (P11)—timing and treatment unclear
    Multiple expenditure figures appear throughout the file at different bases and with different scopes, creating reconciliation difficulties. The Fact Find (P1) records total monthly household expenditure as £2,836.23, which the report rounds or restates as £2,986 (£149.77 or 5.6% higher). The extraction notes identify an ambiguity: the report later assumes £2,237/month expenditure until retirement in cashflow assumptions (P5), a figure not found in any evidence document and representing 21-27% reduction from documented expenditure without stated justification. The extraction also flags that the retirement expenditure figure of £2,000 net may be personal only (excluding Tony's £600/month contribution), whereas current £2,986 is stated as household. The budget notes (P12) indicate current expenses 'in the region of £2,800' and model retirement pension income of £1,900 plus partner contribution £800 totalling £2,700/month, which would not cover £2,986 household expenditure but would cover £2,000 personal plus £600 partner = £2,600. The mortgage payment shows two values: £600/month in liability section (P11) and £750/month in cashflow assumptions (P5); P11 also shows total liability payments of £600 mortgage + £175 credit card + £308 personal loan = £1,083, yet the solution design states £750 mortgage for next 7 years. The partner contribution appears as £600/month net in income section but as £800/month in Fact Find (P4) and Meeting notes refer to no change in this figure. The report does not reconcile these or explain the basis differences. On the gross/net guard: the £2,000 retirement expenditure is explicitly stated as net, while the £2,986 current expenditure basis is unspecified (could be gross household or net). This is a material basis difference that should be declared. The value match check found £2,986 not matched exactly (nearest £3,000 in P17 is 0.5% away but that is the holiday spend, not monthly expenditure).

  [G1.5] Assets and liabilities are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts main residence £430,000, cash £17,000, inheritance £350,000, mortgage £70,000, mortgage monthly payment £750 over 7 years ending 2033-03-24. Total assets £447,000 (residence + cash), total liabilities £70,000, net asset value £377,000.
    File:     Fact Find records main residence £430,000 as at 20/02/2026, cash £7,000 as at 21/07/2026, inheritance £150,000 as at 21/07/2026 (pending legal proceedings, 2-3 years, maybe sooner), total assets £587,000. Mortgage outstanding £68,000 at 5.25%, monthly payment £600, loan term to age 65. Credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000. Emergency fund required £24,000, current £7,000. Total liabilities table shows mortgage £68,000, credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000 (total £88,000).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states cash £17,000; Fact Find states £7,000 (P14, P11 both dated after report date 2026-03-24)
      - Report states inheritance £350,000; Fact Find states £150,000 (P14)
      - Report states mortgage £70,000; Fact Find states £68,000 (P3, P20)
      - Report states mortgage payment £750 per month; Fact Find states £600 per month (P3, P12)
      - Report states mortgage ending 2033-03-24 (7 years); Fact Find states mortgage term to age 65 with no specific end date in liabilities table (P3)
      - Report calculates net asset value as £377,000 (£447,000 assets minus £70,000 liabilities); this excludes inheritance entirely and other liabilities (credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000 totalling £88,000 in Fact Find)
      - Report includes only mortgage in liabilities; Fact Find shows three liabilities totalling £88,000 (mortgage £68,000, credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000)
    Multiple material discrepancies exist between report assertions and Fact Find evidence. Cash holdings differ by £10,000 (report £17,000 vs Fact Find £7,000). The Fact Find clearly shows £7,000 cash at P11 (emergency fund section dated 21/07/2026) and £7,000 at P14 (assets table). Inheritance differs significantly: report asserts £350,000 but Fact Find states £150,000 with note 'to be split between her and her sister' (P14), suggesting Melanie receives only half. Mortgage principal differs: report £70,000 vs Fact Find £68,000 (P3, P20). Monthly payment differs: report £750 vs Fact Find £600 (P3, P12). Report's mortgage end date of 2033-03-24 (7 years from report date) does not align with Fact Find term 'to age 65' with no explicit maturity date. Most significantly, report's net asset value calculation of £377,000 (£447,000 minus £70,000) excludes: (1) the inheritance entirely (£350,000 or £150,000), (2) credit card liability (£7,000), and (3) personal loan (£13,000). Fact Find shows total liabilities of £88,000 (mortgage £68,000 + credit card £7,000 + personal loan £13,000), not the £70,000 mortgage-only figure the report uses. The extraction notes identify this ambiguity: 'Net asset value calculated as £377,000 (excluding inheritance)'. For net asset value, the Fact Find shows total assets £587,000 and liabilities table identifying three debt items. The report's selective inclusion of liabilities and omission of inheritance creates material inconsistency. Cash discrepancy of £10,000 is material to affordability analysis given emergency fund shortfall of £17,000 stated in Fact Find (P11). Inheritance amount affects capacity-for-loss and retirement income adequacy. The Fact Find and report appear to derive from different time periods (February 2026 vs July 2026) but the inheritance and liability figures suggest fundamental data mismatches rather than temporal evolution.

    The report asserts the client will receive Full State Pension of £230.21 per week from age 67. The suitability report [P1] repeats the £230.21 figure on page 3.     NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quilter provider statement showing fund value £260,000 and pension sharing order award details, True Potential provider statement showing current fund value £46,672.80 and individual fund holdings and values, Nest provider statement showing current fund value £21,272.64, Contemporaneous valuation dates for Quilter and True Potential holdings to establish whether as-at dates explain value differences, Fact Find entries or amendment showing why Quilter, True Potential and Nest appear in the suitability report but not in the Fact Find pension schedule completed 16/01/2026, Clarification of the relationship between the Aviva pension recorded in Fact Find (£294,867.94) and the three arrangements listed in the report
    The report asserts three existing pension arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest) with specific current values and, for two of them, transfer values. No passages in the retrieved evidence independently corroborate these figures with provider statements. The Fact Find completed 16/01/2026 does not record Quilter, True Potential or Nest in its Personal Pensions section; instead it records a single Aviva pension with substantially different values (current £294,867.94 vs the three reported arrangements totalling £327,945.44). The Quilter pension is central to the case narrative (discussed extensively in the Meeting Summary as the subject of divorce proceedings and pension sharing order), yet the only documentary support is the Certified PSA which references a pension sharing order but does not provide Quilter's own valuations. The report's claim that Quilter fund value is 'c. £260,000' has no corroborating provider evidence in the pack. The True Potential holdings are listed with granular detail (six funds, each with specific values summing to £46,672.80) but the True Potential Defaqto Existing Holdings document provides minimal supporting detail. The Nest pension is supported by fund documentation (Nest Higher Risk Fund factsheets) confirming the fund exists and its risk level, but no statement confirms the specific current value of £21,272.64. The arithmetic guard for fund values vs transfer values does not explain this gap: the report correctly states that Quilter has current value but no transfer value (it is the subject of a pending pension sharing order), and True Potential and Nest have matching current and transfer values. However, the core issue is not arithmetic but membership: the three arrangements in the report do not appear in the Fact Find's pension schedule, creating a material inconsistency about which pension arrangements the client held at the fact find date (16/01/2026). The Aviva pension in the Fact Find is not mentioned anywhere in the suitability report, raising the question of whether this was a reporting error, an extraction error, or a material omission. The report's suitability report date is marked 2026 but no day/month is given; True Potential holdings are dated 20/03/2026, Nest documentation is undated, and the Aviva Fact Find is dated 20/07/2026, creating temporal ambiguity. Without independent provider statements for Quilter, True Potential and Nest, and without explanation of why the Aviva pension recorded in the Fact Find is absent from the report's arrangement list, the consistency of existing product information cannot be established.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report contains five internal inconsistencies: (1) True Potential platform charges stated as 0.40% in existing arrangements but 0.98% in charge comparison (including 0.50% advice charge and 0.58% fund charges); (2) Nest plan risk level 8 versus agreed risk profile 7, with recommendation for risk 7 funds, yet Nest stated as misaligned and report claims Nest Higher Risk Fund has higher equity content achieving greater growth; (3) Recommended portfolio allocation of HSBC 50% and Vanguard 50% presented ambiguously as 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity - 50%' potentially indicating nested allocations; (4) Initial advice fee stated as 3% equalling £9,838.36 on transfer values of £67,945.44, but 3% of £67,945.44 equals £2,038.36, meaning £9,838.36 is actually 14.49% of transfer value; (5) Net asset value calculated as £377,000 (assets £447,000 minus liabilities £70,000) excludes £350,000 pending inheritance expected within one year, creating ambiguity whether the £377,000 is pre- or post-inheritance.
    File:     This requirement assesses the report's internal consistency against itself. No supporting documents are used to validate or corroborate these figures, as the comparison is within the report alone. The inconsistencies identified are derived from the report's own extracted values and stated figures appearing in different sections.
    Discrepancies:
      - True Potential charges: 0.40% (existing arrangements table) vs 0.98% (charge comparison including advice and fund charges)
      - Nest risk level 8 stated as misaligned with agreed risk profile 7, yet recommendation is for risk 7 funds - the logic of misalignment is consistent, but the characterisation of Nest Higher Risk Fund as achieving 'greater growth' than recommended funds appears to contradict the switch rationale
      - Initial advice fee percentage-to-amount mismatch: stated as 3% but £9,838.36 divided by £67,945.44 equals 14.49%, not 3%
      - Net asset value £377,000 excludes £350,000 pending inheritance discussed throughout objectives and cashflow, creating uncertainty about whether figure is pre- or post-inheritance basis
      - Fund allocation presentation ambiguity: HSBC 50% and Vanguard 50% shown as 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity - 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic - 50%' unclear whether nested or flat allocations
    The report contains genuine internal contradictions and ambiguities on material figures. First, the True Potential charges discrepancy reflects a difference in scope: the 0.40% is platform charge alone, while 0.98% includes ongoing advice charge of 0.50% and fund charges of 0.58%. These are not the same thing - the report mixes platform charges with total recurring charges without clarity on whether the 0.98% figure should apply to existing or proposed arrangements. Second, the Nest risk level inconsistency is structural: the report correctly identifies Nest (risk 8) as misaligned with agreed risk 7 profile, recommending switch to risk 7 funds. However, the extract notes state Nest Higher Risk Fund achieved 'higher equity content' and 'greater growth' (45.91% over 5 years) compared to the recommended funds (47.84%), which appears to undermine the risk-based rationale for switching. This is internal logical inconsistency, not merely a factual disagreement. Third, the advice fee arithmetic fails: £9,838.36 is claimed as 3% of £67,945.44 but is actually 14.49% - this is a clear calculation error. Fourth, the net asset value of £377,000 excludes an expected £350,000 inheritance, which is discussed extensively in objectives and cashflow modelling. The report does not clarify whether £377,000 is meant to be current position excluding the inheritance or if the inheritance is simply treated separately. This creates material ambiguity about the client's actual financial position on which the recommendations depend. Fifth, the fund allocation presentation is ambiguous but less material - the stated 50%/50% split appears correct but the rendering 'Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity - 50%' could be misread as the internal allocation of that fund (80% equity) multiplied by its 50% weighting, creating confusion about overall portfolio construction.

  [G1.9] Emergency fund figures stated in the report are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an emergency fund held amount of £17,000 as at 2026-03-24. The report also states the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds', indicating the emergency fund is confirmed sufficient.
    File:     The Fact Find (P4) records: 'Amount put aside for Emergency Fund: £7,000.00' and 'Amount of Emergency Fund Required: £24,000.00' with an 'Emergency Fund Shortfall: £17,000.00'. Budget notes in P4 state 'Currently living off savings which is now reduced to £7,000 and this also acts as an emergency pot. She prefers to have 12 months of expenditure as an emergency reserve so close to £24,000. The £7,000 will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up and this is making her feel uncomfortable.' Additional notes record 'Total Lump Sum available for this Advice Session (including any emergency funds): £0.00' and mention a planned lump sum withdrawal of £20,000 to replace emergency funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts £17,000 as the held emergency fund amount, but the Fact Find records £7,000 as the actual amount currently held for emergency purposes.
      - The report states the emergency fund is confirmed sufficient, but the Fact Find identifies a £17,000 shortfall (required £24,000 minus held £7,000).
      - The report's £17,000 figure matches the identified shortfall in the Fact Find, not the actual held amount.
      - The Fact Find notes the held amount will reduce to approximately £4,000 before pension income is set up, making the client uncomfortable, which contradicts the report's statement that the client is comfortable with available liquid cash access.
    The report's assertion that an emergency fund of £17,000 is held appears to conflate two separate figures from the Fact Find. The evidence shows: (1) amount currently held = £7,000; (2) amount required = £24,000; (3) shortfall = £17,000. The report states £17,000 as 'heldAmount' when this is actually the shortfall or required reserve, not what is currently held. The report further asserts the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds', but the Fact Find budget notes explicitly state 'The £7,000 will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up and this is making her feel uncomfortable.' This directly contradicts the comfort statement in the report. The Fact Find also notes the total lump sum available for investment is £0.00 (as funds are depleting), though £20,000 of the recommended lump sum withdrawal from tax-free pension cash is intended to 'replace emergency funds', suggesting the current £7,000 is insufficient and requires supplementation. The report's £17,000 figure is materially inconsistent with the evidenced held amount of £7,000.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states annual earnings of £45,000 gross until age 60, then retirement. Monthly expenditure is £2,237 until retirement and £2,000 net per month in retirement. The client spends £2,500 on a new sofa immediately, pays £750 per month on mortgage for 7 years, receives £350,000 inheritance next year, receives full State Pension from age 67. Pension investment growth is 6.00% per annum before charges. Inflation assumptions: general expenditure 2%, savings 2%, State Pension 2.5%, property 4.7%, lending 4%. Cashflow modelled to age 100. Assets include main residence £430,000, cash £17,000. Baseline projection shows no shortfall to age 100. High inflation stress test shows shortfall at age 81. Market crash stress test shows no shortfall. Life expectancy adequacy shows funds would last to age 87 (ONS female life expectancy).
    File:     Supporting passages corroborate: £45,000 annual earnings [P19], £2,237 monthly expenditure [P19], £2,000 net monthly in retirement [P19], £2,500 sofa spend [P19], £750 mortgage payment [P19], £350,000 inheritance [P19], age 67 State Pension [P19], 6.00% growth rate [P1], 2% general expenditure inflation [P1], 2% savings inflation [P1], 2.5% State Pension inflation [P1], 4.7% property inflation [P1], 4% lending inflation [P1], main residence £430,000 [P20], cash £17,000 [P20]. Cashflow analysis documented [P12]. Life expectancy from ONS: female age 55 has life expectancy of 87 years [P23]. However, a material ambiguity exists: the extraction notes 'Monthly expenditure before retirement: £2,237' but 'Monthly expenditure in retirement: £2,000 net per month' with unclear basis. Report also states 'personal expenditure' of £2,000 in retirement but earlier financial position analysis refers to £2,237 household expenditure before Tony's £600 contribution, creating ambiguity about whether £2,237 is gross household or personal net. The inheritance is stated as pending legal proceedings and expected within one year but excluded from current net asset value of £377,000 (£447,000 assets minus £70,000 liabilities), though included in cashflow modelling assumptions.
    Discrepancies:
      - Retirement expenditure basis unclear: report states £2,000 'net per month' in retirement as 'personal expenditure' but earlier states £2,237 monthly expenditure 'until retirement' with no explicit statement that this is gross household or personal net. The ambiguity is whether £2,237 continues into retirement or is replaced by £2,000 net, and whether these figures are comparable on the same basis (gross vs net, household vs personal).
      - Inheritance treatment: the report lists inheritance as an asset but excludes it from the net asset value calculation of £377,000 (assets £447,000 minus liabilities £70,000), stating it is 'currently pending legal proceedings'. However, the cashflow assumptions include it as 'received next year', creating a discrepancy between the static balance sheet (which excludes pending inheritance) and the dynamic cashflow model (which includes it). The report states 'your assets are sufficient' in both contexts but the underlying figures differ.
      - Inheritance timing ambiguity: the report states in assumptions 'You receive an inheritance of £350,000 next year' but also states in the assets section it is 'currently pending legal proceedings' without specifying the expected receipt date precisely. One source says 'next year', another says 'within one year' — these may be the same but the precision differs.
    The core assumption values used in cashflow modelling are corroborated by the supporting passages. The earnings, expenditure until retirement, one-off expenditure, mortgage payment, inheritance amount, State Pension age, pension growth rate, and all inflation assumptions match exactly between the report narrative [P19], the Cashflow Assumptions section [P1], and the Assets & Liabilities table [P20]. The life expectancy figure cited (87 years for a 55-year-old female) is directly supported by ONS data [P23]. Figures checked in code confirm: £45,000 corroborated, £2,237 corroborated, £2,000 corroborated, £2,500 corroborated, £750 corroborated, £350,000 corroborated, age 67 corroborated, 6.00% corroborated, 2% corroborated, 2.5% corroborated, 4.7% corroborated, 4% corroborated, main residence £430,000 corroborated, cash £17,000 corroborated, age 87 corroborated. However, the extraction's own 'ambiguities' field flags a material inconsistency: 'Report states monthly expenditure as £2,986 but later states personal expenditure in retirement as £2,000 net per month. Unclear if retirement figure is net of State Pension or represents different basis.' The passages provided do not contain £2,986, and the canonical model extracts expenditure as £2,237 (not £2,986). This suggests either the extraction encountered a different figure not visible in the current passages or there is an internal ambiguity within the report itself about household vs personal expenditure. The passages [P12], [P18], [P19] discuss the cashflow analysis and state 'Your expenditure is £2,237 per month until retirement' and 'In retirement, your personal expenditure is £2,000 net each month' without explicitly reconciling whether these are on the same basis or whether the £2,237 figure includes a partner's contribution. The term 'personal expenditure' applied to the retirement figure suggests it is client-only, while the pre-retirement £2,237 may be household. The inheritance is stated in the Assets section [P20] as pending and excluded from net asset value, but the cashflow assumptions [P19] include it as a received asset in year two, creating a temporal and accounting inconsistency between the snapshot net asset value and the dynamic cashflow projection. The extraction notes flag this: 'Net asset value calculated as £377,000... This excludes the £350,000 inheritance that is stated as "currently pending legal proceedings" and expected within one year of the report date. The inheritance is discussed extensively in objectives and cashflow but not included in current net asset value, creating ambiguity about whether the £377,000 is pre- or post-inheritance.' This is not a contradiction of the facts themselves but a gap in disclosure of how the model handles a pending asset. The guard 'A cashflow that models property for a client the fact find records as renting is using an asset the client does not have' does not apply here; the client owns the residence and the inheritance is explicitly stated as assumed. The ambiguity about expenditure basis and the treatment of the pending inheritance in the net asset value do not prevent the cashflow from being internally coherent if the assumptions are read as stated, but they create potential for misinterpretation. The figures themselves are consistent across documents where they appear; the issue is one of clarity about basis and timing, not arithmetic error.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Six objectives stated with priority ranks: (1) Receive and consolidate Pension Sharing Order from ex-husband's Quilter pension into new arrangement [priority 1]; (2) Review and consolidate dormant True Potential and Nest pensions [priority 2]; (3) Ensure pension funds perform to best ability to build funds for retirement [priority 3]; (4) Retire or reduce hours at age 60 with annual income £25,000-£30,000 [priority 1]; (5) Achieve tax-efficient, flexible retirement income rather than guaranteed income [priority 2]; (6) Manage £350,000 inheritance from mother's property sale and mitigate Inheritance Tax liability [priority 2]. However, three material discrepancies arise: First, the inheritance amount contradicts the evidence substantially - report claims £350,000 as a stated objective, but P11 explicitly records 'approximately £150,000' and describes a complex legal situation with potential contribution requirement, materially different circumstances. Second, retirement age 60 is asserted as an objective but not evidenced; P8 records Melanie as currently 55 'considering accessing her pension benefits' and P11 shows plans for January return to part-time work continuing 'until retirement' without specifying age 60. Third, the stated retirement income target of £25,000-£30,000 appears only in the report's extracted objectives field; no passage records the client stating this specific figure. The evidence (P8, P11) discusses 'replacing' employment income of approximately £45,000 gross (£2,900 net monthly from P8) and references pension drawdown discussions but no stated target range. Priority ranking also requires scrutiny: Report assigns priority 1 to both objective 1 (Pension Sharing Order) and objective 4 (retirement age 60 income), priority 3 to performance objective. Meeting evidence (P3, P5, P9, P15) emphasises consolidation and tax planning as primary stated concerns, with income planning emerging later in timeline (P8, P11 from July 2026 meeting). The report does not identify which objectives, if any, conflict or compete - 'priority rank' alone is ambiguous where two items share rank 1. The estate planning goals section of the fact find (P1, P16) is blank, yet inheritance management is listed as objective 6 with priority 2. Estate Planning area appears listed in advice areas (P6) but substantive objectives are not recorded there. The guard on paraphrase applies to objectives 1-2 and 5, which are reasonably paraphrased from the evidence, but does not cover the factual discrepancy on inheritance amount, the unsupported specificity of retirement age 60, or the absence of written client statement for the income target.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a retirement target date of 2026-03-24, a retirement target age of 60, a term of 40 years, and time horizons stated as 'ShortTerm', 'ShortTerm', 'LongTerm', 'LongTerm', 'LongTerm', 'MediumTerm'. The intended retirement age is stated as 60. Life expectancy is stated as age 87 based on Office of National Statistics data for a 55-year-old female. The report states the client 'plan to retire at age 60' with annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until State Pension at age 67. The investment horizon is described as '5 to 10 years' in the risk profiling context.
    File:     The Fact Find [P21] records 'You plan to retire at age 60' and 'You intend to draw an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension starts at age 67'. The Meeting Summary [P24] states 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years'. The ONS life expectancy data [P9] and [P13] confirm life expectancy for a female aged 55 is 87 years. The pension switch analysis [P18], [P20] projects fund values 'Paid Up at age 87' and states 'Term of Pension: Proposed Age 87'. The report date is 24 March 2026 [P7]. The client's date of birth is 15/12/1970 [P7], making her 55 years old at report date. Retirement at age 60 would occur in March 2026 (target date 2026-03-24 in report).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states retirement target date of 2026-03-24, but the client's date of birth is 15/12/1970, meaning age 60 would be reached on 15/12/2030, not March 2026. The report date is 24/03/2026, only 9 days after the stated retirement target date, which is inconsistent with a planned retirement 4+ years in the future.
      - Report asserts 40-year term (age 60 to 100), but life expectancy basis uses age 87, implying a 27-year planning horizon (age 60 to 87), not 40 years.
      - Report states 'investment horizon is five to ten years' [P24] in meeting summary, but the pension switch analysis projects to age 87, which is 32 years from age 55 or 27 years from age 60.
      - Multiple time horizons stated in report ('ShortTerm', 'ShortTerm', 'LongTerm', 'LongTerm', 'LongTerm', 'MediumTerm') with no clear mapping to objectives or explanation of why different horizons apply to different objectives.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Clarity on whether retirement target date 2026-03-24 is intended (would require retirement 4+ years early, contradicting age 60 target) or is an extraction error., Explicit statement of whether the 40-year term (to age 100) or 27-year term (to life expectancy age 87) is the planning basis., Documentation of which objective each stated time horizon applies to, and why multiple horizons are used., Confirmation of whether the '5 to 10 years' investment horizon refers to the time before retirement (incorrect: retirement is ~4 years away) or to the time before income drawdown begins.
    The requirement tests consistency between stated retirement objectives and horizons in the report and supporting evidence. A critical inconsistency exists around the retirement target date. The report extracts a targetDate of '2026-03-24', which is only 9 days after the report print date of 24/03/2026. The client's date of birth (15/12/1970) means age 60 is reached on 15/12/2030, over 4 years after the stated target date. The Fact Find [P21] and report both clearly state the client intends to retire at age 60. The targetDate extraction therefore appears to be an error, possibly confusing the report date with the retirement date. However, this error appears in the canonical model itself, not merely as an inconsistency with evidence — the evidence (client's DOB, explicit age 60 goal) contradicts the report's stated targetDate. The 40-year term contradicts the life expectancy basis of age 87. A 55-year-old retiring at 60 and living to 87 implies a 27-year planning horizon, not 40 years. The guards note that selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date, and that the switch analysis states its own retirement age and term, routinely different from the report. The switch analysis [P18], [P20] projects to 'Proposed Age 87' and shows 'Paid Up at age 87'. This supports a 27-year horizon from age 60, not 40 years. The '5 to 10 years' investment horizon stated in [P24] is ambiguous: it may refer to the time before benefits are drawn (which would be ~4 years, not 5-10), or to the recommended holding period of the funds. The report lists six different time horizons without explaining which objective each serves. This creates material ambiguity about whether the time horizons are specific to objectives or generic. The evidence (Fact Find, Meeting Summary, switch analysis) supports age 60 retirement, age 87 life expectancy, and a 27-year planning term, but does not support a 40-year term or a 2026-03-24 retirement date. The multiple time horizons are not explained in the passages provided. The vulnerability overlay does not apply: no actual or potential vulnerability drivers affecting objectives are noted in the evidence.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an emergency fund of £17,000 held in liquid cash, stating the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report specifies planned access timing for pension funds as '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete.' The report identifies no ad-hoc access requirements beyond this standard planned access window.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] records an Emergency Fund amount put aside of £7,000, with an Emergency Fund Required of £24,000, creating a shortfall of £17,000. The notes in [P1] state: 'Currently living off savings which is now reduced to £7,000 and this also acts as an emergency pot. She prefers to have 12 months of expenditure as an emergency reserve so close to £24,000. The £7,000 will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up and this is making her feel uncomfortable.' The Meeting Summary [P5] notes: 'Morgan emphasised the importance of having a buffer of three to six months of expenses, though Melanie preferred a larger buffer, ideally covering 12 months.' The Meeting Summary [P7] confirms: 'An additional £20,000 is intended to create a cash savings buffer, providing financial security during this transitional period.' Notes in [P1] indicate the client plans to withdraw £40,000 as a tax-free lump sum, of which '£20,000 to replace emergency funds and also to cover some legal fees for the ongoing issue she has regarding the inheritance.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states emergency fund held is £17,000; Fact Find [P1] records only £7,000 currently held, with a required amount of £24,000 and a shortfall of £17,000. The £17,000 in the report appears to be the shortfall amount, not the held amount.
      - Report does not disclose that the held emergency fund is currently £7,000 and expected to fall to £4,000, creating discomfort.
      - Report does not disclose the client's explicit preference for 12 months of expenditure as emergency reserve (approximately £24,000) versus the 3-6 month standard discussed.
      - Report does not reference the planned £20,000 withdrawal from pension tax-free cash to replenish emergency funds, which is a material ad-hoc access requirement tied to a specific objective.
      - Report does not address the timing of emergency fund restoration: the client expects the £7,000 to 'likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up,' creating a transitional vulnerability.
    The report's assertion that the client 'has liquid cash' of £17,000 as an emergency fund is materially misleading. The evidence shows the client currently holds £7,000, which will likely decline to £4,000, creating a situation the client finds uncomfortable. The £17,000 figure in the report is the identified shortfall, not the held amount. This is a fundamental mischaracterisation of the client's current emergency liquidity position. The report correctly identifies the 10-15 working day access window for planned pension access, which is consistent with standard pension administration timelines and is adequately disclosed [P14]. However, the report fails to address a material ad-hoc access requirement: the client's stated plan to withdraw £20,000 as tax-free cash to 'replace emergency funds' [P1, P7]. This withdrawal serves a specific objective — rebuilding the emergency buffer from £4,000 to £24,000 — and is time-sensitive (intended before pension income setup). The report does not link this withdrawal to access timing or acknowledge it as an access requirement the recommendation must accommodate. The suitability statement that the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash' [P14] is not supported by the evidence. The client expresses discomfort about the declining emergency pot, explicitly states a preference for 12 months of reserves, and has identified a specific need to restore emergency funds through a planned lump sum withdrawal. The report's characterisation of comfort appears to be based on the adviser's assessment rather than client confirmation. No vulnerability overlay is identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment; the client's transitional employment status and pending inheritance create the access need, but these are disclosed in the objectives section [P12] without specific accommodation in the access requirements section.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five foreseeable changes: (1) Cessation of employee pension contributions and Nest plan becomes paid up on 2026-03-24, with £0 financial impact stated; (2) Retirement or reduction in working hours on 2031-03-24, with inferred income reduction of -£45,000 gross; (3) Inheritance receipt of £350,000 from mother's property sale on 2027-03-24, currently pending legal proceedings; (4) Mortgage repayment completion on 2033-03-24, with -£750 monthly financial impact; (5) State Pension entitlement begins on 2034-03-24, with derived annual amount of £11,970.92 (£230.21 per week × 52). The report models these changes in cashflow analysis showing assets lasting to age 100, with inheritance assumed received in two years, mortgage at £750/month for 7 years, retirement expenditure of £2,000 net monthly from age 60, and full state pension from age 67.
    File:     The evidence corroborates most foreseeable changes: (1) Employment status change to self-employed confirmed with cessation of employer pension contributions [P2], Nest plan becoming paid up stated [P2]. (2) Retirement goal at age 60 confirmed [P2, P3]; target retirement income of £25,000-£30,000 annually stated [P2, P3]; income reduction from employment cessation referenced [P2]. (3) Inheritance of £350,000 from late mother's property pending legal proceedings confirmed [P2]. (4) Mortgage of £70,000 liability confirmed [P1], mortgage payment of £750/month for 7 years stated [P12]. (5) Full State Pension of £230.21 per week from age 67 confirmed [P1, P12]. Cashflow modeller assumptions documented: income £45,000 until age 60 [P12], expenditure £2,237/month until retirement and £2,000 net monthly in retirement [P12], £2,500 sofa spend [P12], inheritance received in two years [P12], funds consolidated with 6% growth assumption [P12], cashflow runs to age 100 [P12]. Partner's contribution of £600/month noted [P2, P9].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states cessation of employee pension contributions has £0 financial impact, but this omits the loss of £600/month partner's contribution (Tony's contribution ceases when employment ends) which represents £7,200 annually or £86,400 over 12 years to retirement—material to cashflow. The report acknowledges Tony contributes £600/month [P2, P9] but does not quantify this loss as a foreseeable change's financial impact.
      - Report states retirement income objective as £25,000-£30,000 annually [P2, P3], but cashflow modeller uses different basis: personal net expenditure of £2,000/month (£24,000 annually) [P12] differs from stated gross objective range. Ambiguity noted in extraction—unclear if £25,000-£30,000 is household (including Tony's contribution cessation), personal only, or gross vs net.
      - Report models monthly expenditure as £2,237 until retirement [P12] but earlier states household expenditure totals £2,986 with Tony contributing £600 [P2, P9]. The £2,237 appears to be personal expenditure only (£2,986 - £600 - £150 discretionary), but this basis is not explicitly stated for the cashflow model.
      - Report states State Pension entitlement at age 67 [P1, P12] with £230.21/week [P1], but retirement target is age 60 [P2, P3], creating a 7-year gap between retirement and State Pension onset. Cashflow shows income shortfall in this period addressed by pension drawdown [P11], but the sequencing and adequacy of access is not explicitly reconciled against the retirement objective.
    The report documents foreseeable life changes, and most are supported by evidence in the file. However, three material issues arise: (1) Incompleteness: The loss of Tony's £600/month contribution (£7,200 annually) on cessation of employment is not listed as a foreseeable change with financial impact, despite being material to retirement income adequacy. The £600/month contribution is stated in the evidence [P2, P9] and must cease when client transitions to self-employed status, yet the canonical model shows the first foreseeableChange's financial impact as £0. This omission means a material income reduction is undisclosed. (2) Basis inconsistency: The report's stated retirement income objective of £25,000-£30,000 [P2, P3] is not reconciled with the cashflow model's personal expenditure assumption of £2,000/month net (£24,000 annually) [P12]. The extraction highlights this ambiguity: unclear whether the stated objective is household income (would be £24,000-£30,000 less State Pension post-age 67), personal only, or gross vs net. The cashflow shows income drawn from pensions to cover shortfall until State Pension [P11], but the link between objective and model assumption is not explicit. (3) Time-horizon clarity: The report states retirement age 60 but State Pension begins age 67, creating a 7-year drawdown period from pension funds. The cashflow addresses this [P11], but the compatibility between the retirement objective (age 60, income £25,000-£30,000) and the 7-year gap before State Pension is not explicitly reconciled in the foreseeable changes statement. The inheritance receipt in 2027 (year 2) is properly sequenced relative to retirement in 2031 (year 5), and stress tests show funds lasting to age 87 [P10], so the overall plan viability is addressed. However, the stated objectives and the modelled assumptions do not align on a single stated basis. The report does not state whether the £25,000-£30,000 target assumes Tony's contribution continues (it does not; it ceases in 2026), nor whether it is net of investment growth assumptions or the £2,000/month personal spend excludes household joint expenses. No vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence affecting this comparison.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report asserts six objectives with priority ranks: (1) Consolidate pension pots (priority 1); (2) Tax-efficient retirement income strategy (priority 2); (3) Achieve retirement income of £25,000–£30,000 annually (priority 3); (4) Manage inheritance strategically (priority 1); (5) Estate planning including will update and power of attorney (priority 2); (6) Access flexibility for ad-hoc needs such as holiday to New Zealand (priority 2). Target date stated as 2026-03-24 for one objective. Target amounts: £260,000 (Quilter fund value), £67,945.44 (transfer value), £350,000 (expected inheritance pending legal proceedings). Report states client is currently 55 years old, considering transitional phase after leaving employment at £45,000 annual salary, with plans to potentially return to work in January. Retirement personal expenditure stated as £2,000 net per month in one passage; household expenditure £2,986 in another. Retirement income objective of £25,000–£30,000 stated but basis (gross vs net, household vs personal) is ambiguous per extraction note.
    File:     Evidence shows client Melanie Anness is managing finances post-divorce (amicable with ex-husband Paul Annis). Meeting summaries confirm objectives: consolidate pensions (P5), optimize retirement income (P5), explore drawdown and annuities (P14), understand flexibility for ad-hoc withdrawals including planned New Zealand holiday (P14). Estate planning discussed: updating will, appointing daughters as power of attorney (P6), reviewing in light of potential £1.4 million inheritance from late mother's property valued at approximately £1.4 million, with timeline uncertain due to legal proceedings (P6). Risk profile agreed as 7/10 (adventurous, growth-oriented) confirmed across multiple meetings (P3, P7). Client background: accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited earning £42,000 annually (P2); former currency trader with investment experience; opted for higher risk in Nest (risk 8), medium risk in True Potential (P14). Ad-hoc meeting 21/07/2026 (P4) discusses client at age 55 in transitional phase after leaving employment (~£45,000 income, £2,900 net monthly), considering pension access. Client willing to work beyond 60 or part-time if necessary (P3). No explicit statement in evidence of retirement income target of £25,000–£30,000 or specific priority ranking of the six objectives.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states retirement income objective as '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' but evidence does not record this figure; extraction note flags ambiguity whether this is household (including partner's contribution), personal income, or gross vs net basis
      - Report states target date 2026-03-24 for an objective; no matching target date appears in evidence
      - Report lists six objectives with stated priority ranks (1,2,3,1,2,2); evidence discusses objectives (consolidation, tax efficiency, flexibility, estate planning, inheritance strategy) but does not state explicit priority ranking or confirm that six distinct prioritized objectives were agreed with client
      - Report identifies inheritance of £350,000 as objective with priority 1; evidence records £1.4 million potential inheritance (P6) with uncertain timeline due to legal proceedings, creating ambiguity whether £350,000 is client's share or a different figure
      - Retirement expenditure stated in report as £2,000 net per month (per extraction ambiguity); evidence mentions £2,900 net per month as current income after employment departure (P4) and £2,986 household expenditure (extraction note), creating unclear basis for retirement planning figure
      - Report salary figure: extraction from suitability report context suggests £42,000; evidence (P2) states £42,000 annual salary at Bridgeport Interiors; consistent but evidence also mentions ~£45,000 income from recent employment (P4), creating uncertainty about current vs historical salary
      - Report goal of 'access flexibility for ad-hoc needs such as holiday to New Zealand' is supported by evidence (P14 mentions keen interest in drawdown flexibility, planned holiday to New Zealand), but priority rank (2) is not explicitly confirmed in evidence
    The check requires evidence that each objective in the report is supported by categories B, C or F (client-facing documents, meeting notes, fact finds), that terms and access needs reconcile, and that recommendations link to specific objectives. Comparison can be performed on high-level alignment: evidence clearly records the broad categories of objectives (pension consolidation, tax-efficient income, flexibility, estate planning, inheritance strategy) consistent with report. However, material gaps prevent full consistency assessment. First, the report's six numbered objectives with explicit priority ranks (1,2,3,1,2,2) have no matched recording in evidence. Meeting summaries discuss objectives qualitatively but do not state that client prioritized consolidation as rank 1, inheritance management as rank 1, or ranked tax strategy as priority 2. This is material because conflicting objectives (two rank-1 priorities competing for same capital or time) are the trigger for this check: if consolidation and inheritance management both claim priority 1, they may conflict. Evidence does not show this conflict resolved or even that these priorities were explicitly agreed. Second, the retirement income target of £25,000–£30,000 lacks a recorded basis in evidence. The extraction note flags ambiguity: is this household income (including partner's contribution that ceases post-divorce), personal income only, gross or net? P3 states client's capacity for loss is moderate and 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market,' but does not state the £25,000–£30,000 figure itself. P4 records current net income as approximately £2,900 per month (~£34,800 annual); if retirement target is £25,000–£30,000, this is below current income, yet no strategic explanation for this reduction is recorded in evidence. Third, the target date 2026-03-24 appears only in the canonical model extraction and cannot be matched to evidence. Client's retirement age is not explicitly stated as 2026 or age date; evidence references age 55 (P4, 2026), willingness to work beyond 60 (P3), and potential return to work January (P4). No document states 2026-03-24 as a goal date. Fourth, the inheritance is recorded in evidence as £1.4 million potential estate (P6) with uncertain timeline due to legal proceedings; the report's figure of £350,000 may be client's share, but this is inferred, not stated. The extraction note identifies this as a significant ambiguity. Fifth, retirement expenditure figures are not reconciled: report (via extraction) states £2,000 net per month in retirement; evidence records £2,900 net current monthly income and £2,986 household expenditure. The basis of each is unclear. None of these gaps represents a structural conflict visible in the objectives themselves; both sides agree consolidation, tax efficiency, flexibility, and inheritance are objectives. However, the absence of explicit priority ranking, stated retirement income basis, confirmed target dates, and reconciled expenditure figures means the comparison cannot establish whether objectives are mutually achievable or whether term misalignment exists (e.g., capital locked until age 87 but access needed at age 60). Guidance states recommendations should name specific objectives they serve; P3 (suitability report section on multi-asset funds) states funds 'align with your financial goals and risk profile' and 'provide simplicity, cost-efficiency, and flexibility as your circumstances evolve,' but does not link recommendations to specific ranked objectives or explain how flexibility serves the New Zealand holiday access need or how consolidation and inheritance strategies are sequenced. The meeting notes (P5, P14) show client interest in drawdown for flexibility and holiday spending, but no advice record states the recommendation achieves these alongside consolidation and estate planning without conflict. Vulnerability overlay: Evidence (P4, P8) notes client experienced recent divorce, loss of mother (with Parkinson's and dementia), job departure at 55, and desire to 'enjoy life' post-loss. These support needs (estate planning, flexibility, confidence in advice) are addressed by documentation (estate planning meetings, drawdown discussion) but no record states whether these characteristics required adjustment to objective prioritization or term assumptions. Not a separate concern on this check (CHK-010 covers vulnerability) but noted as context to why explicit agreement is important: client has experienced significant life change and stated desire for flexibility and enjoyment, yet retirement spending target (£25,000–£30,000) is not explained in relation to these needs.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies six objectives across three time horizons: OBJ1-OBJ2 (ShortTerm), OBJ3-OBJ5 (LongTerm), OBJ6 (MediumTerm). The recommendation links three objectives to the Aviva Pension transfer (OBJ1-OBJ3), two further objectives to ongoing management (OBJ3-OBJ5), and one to a third recommendation (OBJ6). The client is age 55, retiring at age 60 (5-year horizon), seeking £25,000-£30,000 annual retirement income. Stated time horizon in meeting notes is 'five to ten years'. The recommendation transfers £67,945.44 into an Aviva Personal Pension with nil regular contributions, projected to £106,000 at age 89 (real terms, mid growth 2.94%). Access is via Flexi-access Drawdown from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). The client has £350,000 inheritance pending within one year, £2,237 monthly personal expenditure until retirement, £2,000 net personal expenditure in retirement, and mortgage of £750/month ceasing in 7 years. The report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension'.
    File:     Meeting notes confirm client plan to retire at age 60 and draw £25,000-£30,000 annual pension until state pension at 67. Notes record investment horizon as 'five to ten years' and risk profile as 'level seven'. Client background as accountant and former currency trader noted. The Aviva product literature confirms access available from age 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028) via drawdown or annuity, with flexible access permitted and 24-hour online access via MyAviva. Capped Drawdown can be recalculated after three years and at end of each subsequent three-year period. Death benefits are tax-free before age 75, taxed at beneficiary's rate after. The product supports variable contributions and ad-hoc withdrawals. No evidence in the file specifically names the six objectives (OBJ1-OBJ6), their nature, or whether they are documented. The canonical model extraction notes that '/needsAnalysis/objectives' is ambiguous: retirement income stated as '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' but unclear if household (including Tony's £600/month contribution), personal only, or gross vs net. Expenditure figures are also ambiguous: £2,986 household monthly (with Tony's £600 contribution) versus £2,000 net personal in retirement.

    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the evidence pack does not contain the objective definitions (OBJ1-OBJ6) that the report asserts it is linking the recommendation to. The extraction metadata notes that '/needsAnalysis/objectives' is absent from the canonical model, and while the suitability report mentions client goals in narrative form (retirement at 60, income of £25,000-£30,000), it does not present these as formally numbered objectives with stated time horizons, priorities, or links to specific recommendations. The report does name OBJ1-OBJ6 but does not define them in the file evidence. A link without an objective definition is an unsupported claim. The access mechanism (Flexi-access Drawdown from age 55) does match the stated need (access and flexibility), and the product literature supports this; however, the absence of an evidenced needs analysis, the unresolved ambiguities about the income target basis and expenditure assumptions, and the gap between the stated 5-year horizon and the 34-year projection horizon all prevent a conclusion that the recommendation is demonstrably appropriate for the stated goals as evidenced.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a target retirement income of £27,500 annually (inferred midpoint of the range £25,000 to £30,000 stated on page 1). The report states: 'You wish to achieve an annual income of £25,000 to £30,000 in retirement.' This is modelled on assumptions including: earnings of £45,000 gross until age 60, monthly expenditure of £2,237 before retirement and £2,000 net per month in retirement, an inheritance of £350,000, State Pension from age 67, and 6% investment growth before charges.
    File:     The Fact Find (P5) records in Retirement Goals & Objectives Notes: 'You intend to draw an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension starts at age 67.' Meeting summaries (P15, P17) confirm the same range: 'Your goal of drawing an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension begins at age 67' and 'You aim to draw an annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until your state pension begins at 67.' The Suitability Report itself (P20, P23) repeats: 'You wish to achieve an annual income of £25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' and lists cashflow assumptions including the £2,237 monthly expenditure pre-retirement, £2,000 net monthly in retirement, £350,000 inheritance next year, State Pension from age 67, and 6% growth before charges. All key inputs are consistently recorded across the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The extracted canonical model states target income of £27,500 (midpoint) marked as 'Inferred' with modality 'Intended', while the report itself only states a range of £25,000 to £30,000 without specifying a single point figure as the modelled target. The basis is stated as 'Unspecified' in the extraction. No document in the file states £27,500 as a discrete target; this appears to be a computational inference not asserted by the report itself.
      - The report does not explicitly state whether the £25,000 to £30,000 target is gross or net income, or whether it includes or excludes State Pension contributions. The retirement expenditure is stated as '£2,000 net per month' but the income target basis is not clarified in the same terms.
      - The cashflow analysis shows monthly expenditure in retirement as £2,000 net per month (£24,000 per annum net), which aligns with the lower end of the target range if the range is interpreted as net figures; however, the report does not explicitly reconcile the stated income target with the modelled expenditure, leaving the relationship between the target and the need implicit rather than explicit.
    The report asserts a retirement income objective stated as a range (£25,000 to £30,000 annually) and this range is consistently recorded across multiple documents in the file: the Fact Find, meeting summaries, and the Suitability Report itself. The range is supported by evidence in the form of the client's own stated preferences in contemporaneous advice meetings (P15, P17) and the Fact Find. The cashflow assumptions underlying the model are all present and consistent: £45,000 earnings to age 60, £2,237 monthly expenditure pre-retirement, £2,000 net per month in retirement, £350,000 inheritance, State Pension from age 67, and 6% growth. However, there is material ambiguity about the basis of the income target. The report states retirement expenditure is '£2,000 net per month' but does not state whether the target income is gross or net, or whether it includes State Pension. The extracted model infers a point figure of £27,500 (midpoint of the range) but no document in the file asserts this as the specific modelled target; the range is all that is stated. The cashflow section (P23) lists assumptions but does not show the derivation linking the stated £25,000-£30,000 target to the projected fund requirement or the annuity value that would be purchased. The ambiguity about whether figures are personal net (£2,000 pm) or household is noted in the extraction's own report of ambiguities. This creates a situation where the target is stated and supported by client evidence, but the basis on which the plan is modelled to deliver it is not transparently explained. The fact that £2,000 net monthly (£24,000 per annum) sits below the stated range suggests either the range includes other income sources (State Pension, partner contributions) or the basis differs from that stated.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10). The report recommends a strategy mapped to risk level 7, but the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) are not explicitly assigned a risk rating of 7 in the supporting documents. The HSBC fund is described in a technical analysis [P10] as 'Volatility Managed' in IMA Sector with 60-90% equity allocation; Vanguard LS 80% is named for its equity allocation but its assigned risk level is not stated. The suitability report [P3] states regarding Nest: 'current fund is not in line with the agreed risk level' (implying Nest risk 8 exceeds agreed risk 7), yet the performance table [P11] shows Nest Higher Risk (B) outperformed the recommended risk 7 portfolio (C: HSBC/Vanguard) over 5 years (47.84% vs 45.91%), and Nest achieved greater growth, which the report itself acknowledges: 'the Nest funds with a higher equity content achieving greater growth.'. The risk profile documentation [P1], [P5] does not explicitly state what risk level (on the 1-10 scale) each recommended fund has been assigned. The report claims the recommended funds are 'specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' [P6] but this claim is not corroborated by explicit risk level assignments in the fund literature or risk assessment files. 10 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an agreed risk profile of 7 on a scale of 1-10, labelled 'Growth'. The canonical model extracts: attitudeToRisk.assessedCode = 7, attitudeToRisk.agreedCode = 7, attitudeToRisk.label = 'Growth', attitudeToRisk.scaleName = '1-10'. The suitability report states [P3]: 'Your risk profile was agreed as 7 on a scale of 1 - 10' and 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat.'
    File:     Evidence from the Risk Profile Report [P1], [P5], [P8] confirms that an ATR questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 and a risk level was agreed following interactive discussion considering natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return. [P8] states 'Following an interactive discussion, and considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal, we agreed a risk level.' The Meeting Summary [P18] corroborates: 'Your attitude towards investment risk is characterised as adventurous, with a risk profile score of seven on a scale of one to ten.' The Fact Find [B15] records 'Risk Profile' but the field value is not legible in the passage. The KIID documents [P19] describe funds in risk category 5 (Vanguard/HSBC), with asset allocation 60-90% equities. The recommended portfolio combines HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic (risk profile not explicitly stated in passage) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity. The Nest fund in existing arrangements is documented as 'Nest Higher Risk Fund Pension (GBP)' with risk level 8 [P9]. True Potential existing holding risk level is not explicitly stated.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report recommends a strategy mapped to risk level 7, but the recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) are not explicitly assigned a risk rating of 7 in the supporting documents. The HSBC fund is described in a technical analysis [P10] as 'Volatility Managed' in IMA Sector with 60-90% equity allocation; Vanguard LS 80% is named for its equity allocation but its assigned risk level is not stated.
      - The suitability report [P3] states regarding Nest: 'current fund is not in line with the agreed risk level' (implying Nest risk 8 exceeds agreed risk 7), yet the performance table [P11] shows Nest Higher Risk (B) outperformed the recommended risk 7 portfolio (C: HSBC/Vanguard) over 5 years (47.84% vs 45.91%), and Nest achieved greater growth, which the report itself acknowledges: 'the Nest funds with a higher equity content achieving greater growth.'
      - The risk profile documentation [P1], [P5] does not explicitly state what risk level (on the 1-10 scale) each recommended fund has been assigned. The report claims the recommended funds are 'specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' [P6] but this claim is not corroborated by explicit risk level assignments in the fund literature or risk assessment files.
    The report's asserted risk profile of 7/Growth/1-10 scale is corroborated by the Risk Profile Report evidence [P1], [P5], [P8] and the Meeting Summary [P18]. The questionnaire completion date (16/01/2026) and interactive agreement process are documented. No override, amendment or upward adjustment is visible — the assessed and agreed codes are both 7. However, a material gap exists between the risk profile assertion and fund implementation: the report claims the recommended funds are risk-profiled solutions 'specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' [P6], yet neither the HSBC nor the Vanguard fund has an explicit risk level assignment documented in the supporting passages. The fund literature provided (KIID, technical sheets) describes asset allocation ranges and Morningstar categories but does not state the funds are 'Risk 7' products. The Nest fund, by contrast, is explicitly documented as 'Nest Higher Risk Fund' with risk level 8. The performance comparison [P11] notes that Nest underperformed the recommended portfolio, but this is attributed by the report to Nest having 'higher equity content achieving greater growth' — the very characteristic the client's growth objective and (implicitly) high risk tolerance would favour. The claim that Nest is 'not in line with the agreed risk level' [P3] appears to rest on a literal interpretation (8 > 7) rather than on capacity for loss or suitability reasoning, which is not explained. The recommendation rationale [P6] emphasises that multi-asset funds 'aligns with your financial goals and risk profile' but does not cite a specific risk level mapping or confirm that 7/Growth on the 1-10 scale corresponds to the 60-90% equity allocation range shown in the KIID. The Capacity for Loss assessment [P3] is stated as 'moderate' based on cashflow analysis and retirement sufficiency, but the method and any quantified loss scenarios are not detailed in the passages (NotPresentInDocument per extraction metadata). Without explicit risk level assignments in the fund documentation or a clear mapping from the 1-10 scale to the asset allocation targets, the claim that the recommended funds achieve the agreed risk level remains asserted rather than evidenced. This is not a contradiction—the funds may indeed be suitable—but it is an unsupported claim that the evidence cannot corroborate.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Capacity for loss rating: Medium. Assessment basis: (1) 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market.' (2) 'Additionally you have the option to work beyond age 60 or to continue to work part time if necessary.' The report states capacity for loss is assessed separately from attitude to risk (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr: true). ATR is stated as 7 on a scale of 1-10 (Growth profile).
    File:     Risk profile report [P7] confirms 'Following an interactive discussion, and considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal, we agreed a risk level.' [P1] contains section headings for 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' and 'Capacity for loss for the goal' but no details are recorded — both sections state 'The notes recorded following the discussion were:' with no content following. Suitability report [P5] states CFL is 'moderate' (matching report's 'Medium') and provides the same two bases: sufficient assets including inheritance, and option to work longer. Fact Find [P9] records budget notes confirming pension funds plus inheritance will cover retirement expenditure. [P11] contains stress tests: market crash scenario (2007/8 equivalent) shows 'your assets would still last your lifetime'; high inflation stress test (6%) shows assets could deplete at age 81, but Office of National Statistics life expectancy for 55-year-old female is 87. No capacity for loss questionnaire responses are recorded in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts CFL rating is 'Medium' based on cashflow and inheritance support, but no capacity for loss questionnaire document or questionnaire responses appear in the evidence, despite [P1] containing a section heading for 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' with no recorded notes.
      - Report provides two bases for Medium CFL (sufficient assets + option to work longer) but these are restated from the report's own cashflow analysis and objectives, not corroborated by a separate capacity for loss assessment method on file.
      - [P1] Risk profile report contains empty sections for both 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' and 'Capacity for loss for the goal' with no substantive content recorded, suggesting the CFL assessment process may not have been fully documented.
    The report asserts a Medium capacity for loss rating based on two factors: (1) pension funds plus inheritance will be sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after a market fall, and (2) the client can work beyond age 60 if necessary. These factors are internally consistent with the cashflow analysis in [P5] and [P9], which confirm that projected retirement income from pensions plus the expected inheritance will cover expenditure, leaving a buffer. Stress testing in [P11] corroborates this: a 2007/8-equivalent market crash scenario shows assets lasting the client's lifetime, and the 6% high-inflation scenario projects asset depletion at age 81 against a life expectancy of 87, suggesting modest but identifiable loss capacity. However, a material gap exists: the report's CFL rating is not traced to a formal capacity for loss questionnaire or assessment. [P1] contains section headings 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' and 'Capacity for loss for the goal' but records no notes, responses or scoring. The report states in the canonical model that CFL is 'separately assessed from ATR' (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr: true), implying a distinct assessment method, but that method's output or documentary evidence does not appear in the file. The CFL rating is thus supported by financial projections (cashflow, stress testing) but unsupported by the formal capacity for loss assessment process that the report implies was conducted. This is a weak assessment under the guidance: a CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning statement on a questionnaire or assessment form, where a separate assessment method was claimed. The two bases cited (sufficient assets, option to work longer) are derived from objectives and cashflow, not from a CFL questionnaire. Additionally, the report describes CFL as 'moderate' in [P5] but the canonical model returns it as 'Medium' — these terms are synonymous and internally consistent within the report, but the distinction between CFL rating and ATR rating (7 on 1-10 scale) is not clearly explained: the report does not state what scale the 'Medium' CFL is measured on or whether it maps to a numerical equivalent. The ATR scale is explicit (1-10, with 7 assigned); the CFL scale is not defined.
