  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Risk profile agreed as 7 on a scale of 1-10, described as 'Growth' investor with 'adventurous' approach. (2) Capacity for loss determined as 'moderate' based on current information, with rationale: 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market. Additionally you have the option to work beyond age 60 or to continue to work part time if necessary.' (3) Tolerance for loss: The client 'confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses.' (4) No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is quantified. (5) Stress test modelled: market crash similar to 2007/8 shows 'your assets would still last your lifetime' to age 87. (6) Asset allocation: 80% growth assets, 20% defensive assets at risk level 7. (7) Recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C (50%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (50%), both mapped to risk level 7.
    File:     The evidence records: (1) Natural risk level questionnaire completed 16/01/2026, with responses in appendix [P19]. (2) Agreed risk level confirmed as 7 on 1-10 scale through discussion process shown in [P19, P21]. (3) Capacity for loss assessment method is absent from the extraction (expectedButAbsent path noted in extraction report). (4) Meeting summary [P9] describes risk profile as 'seven out of 10' with 'approximately 80% of your investments would be in growth assets, with the remaining 20% in defensive assets.' (5) Meeting summary [P20] confirms 'Your attitude towards investment risk is characterised as adventurous, with a risk profile score of seven.' (6) Risk profile report [P17, P19] contains sections headed 'Capacity for loss for the goal' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' but the actual recorded notes are absent—the document states 'No additional notes were recorded' and shows empty fields. (7) Stress test documented in [P24]: 'if your plan sustained a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8 your assets would still last your lifetime.' (8) No quantified tolerance for loss (maximum acceptable loss % or £ amount) is recorded in any passage. (9) Existing arrangement: Nest Higher Risk Fund risk level 8 [P14]; existing True Potential and Quilter arrangements not rated for risk in passages; recommended Aviva funds rated to risk 7 via benchmark data in [P1, P2, P16]. (10) Investment term: Proposed retirement age 87, current age 55 (derived from passages), giving 32-year term.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts capacity for loss is 'moderate' with explicit rationale; evidence shows the Capacity for Loss Questionnaire was completed but recorded notes are blank (P17 states 'No additional notes were recorded').
      - Report states tolerance for loss was confirmed as 'acceptable' based on capacity; no quantified tolerance figure (% or £ amount) appears in evidence, and tolerance for loss is not asserted as a separate measure in evidence.
      - Report recommends risk level 7 funds aligned to agreed risk level 7; existing Nest arrangement holds risk level 8 fund, which the report states is 'not in line with the agreed risk level'—but evidence shows both risk 7 and risk 8 are on file with no documented override explanation for holding risk 8 prior to the transfer.
      - Risk scale on which 7 is stated: report describes it as '1-10' scale; evidence confirms '1-10' scale in [P19, P21], so scale is consistent across both.
    The requirement asks whether risk, capacity for loss, tolerance for loss, investment term, asset allocation and recommended strategy are consistent and supported. The comparison can be performed on the core elements: risk profile (7 on 1-10 scale), investment term (to age 87), and asset allocation (80/20 growth/defensive at risk 7) are all corroborated across report [P21] and evidence [P9, P19, P20]. The stress test showing resilience to a 2007/8-style crash is documented in both [P24] (report) and supported by the same stress test methodology. However, two material gaps prevent full assurance: (1) Capacity for Loss: The report names it as 'moderate' and provides explicit rationale (pension funds plus inheritance sufficient even after market fall, option to work beyond 60). The evidence confirms a Capacity for Loss Questionnaire exists [P17, P19] with a dedicated section, but that section shows 'No additional notes were recorded' — the questionnaire responses themselves are referenced as being 'in the appendix' [P19] but are not provided in the passages. The capacity for loss determination is therefore asserted but not shown to rest on a documented assessment. This is a gap in supporting evidence for the claimed 'moderate' rating. (2) Tolerance for Loss: The report states the client 'confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses' [P21]. No quantified tolerance figure (percentage or amount) is provided in the report or evidence. The guidance notes 'Many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately; absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction.' However, where a stress test models a large fall (2007/8 scenario) and the conclusion is that assets survive, the absence of a stated tolerance metric means the severity of loss modelled cannot be linked to a client-agreed threshold. The meeting summary [P9] discusses risk at level 7 but does not record an explicit client statement of tolerance for loss. (3) Consistency of risk level across recommendations: The report recommends funds at risk level 7 (HSBC 50%, Vanguard 50% both mapped to risk 7 per fund research and SAP documents [P1, P2, P16]). The existing Nest arrangement holds risk level 8 [P14], which the report correctly identifies as misaligned. No override or explanation appears in the passages for why the client held risk 8 before the switch. This is not a contradiction of the recommendation itself (which is internally consistent at level 7) but indicates incomplete documentation of the previous misalignment. (4) Vulnerability overlay: The case file contains extensive post-divorce context [P15, P20, P23] noting the client's recent divorce, amicable relationship, independent financial security objectives, and estate planning changes. However, no formal vulnerability assessment tool, reasonable adjustment plan, or documented consideration of whether divorce-related circumstances materially altered the client's actual (vs. stated) capacity for loss appears in the passages. The guidance requires that vulnerability materially affecting suitability be noted. The absence of such assessment means the claim that capacity for loss is 'moderate' cannot be confirmed as having been stress-tested against vulnerability factors. (5) Investment term and strategy alignment: The recommendation to retire at 87 aligns with stated retirement goal; asset allocation of 80/20 to risk level 7 is consistent across all sources; no conflict exists here. The evidence also documents that the funds chosen (HSBC Dynamic, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) are each independently risk-rated to level 7, so the portfolio composition is aligned.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 5 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears: [5]). The report also documents multiple objectives with varying time horizons: ShortTerm (2 objectives), MediumTerm (1 objective), and LongTerm (3 objectives). The agreed risk level is stated as 7 on a scale used for growth-oriented investments.
    File:     [P14] Meeting Summary records: 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years'. [P2] Risk profile report states that risk level is determined by discussing 'your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return; factors used when selecting the types of investments and determining suitability' and that 'a portfolio will be built to meet your agreed risk level'. [P3] confirms an 'agreed risk level' was reached 'Following an interactive discussion, and considering your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal'. The objectives extract shows multiple horizons (ShortTerm, MediumTerm, LongTerm) but the evidence does not specify which objectives have which terms or how they map to the single 5-year term asserted in the canonical model.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts investment term of 5 years; meeting summary evidences investment horizon of 'five to ten years' — a range rather than a single point, and extending beyond the asserted 5-year figure.
      - Report lists six objectives with three different time horizons (ShortTerm, MediumTerm, LongTerm), but the canonical model asserts only one investment term (5 years) without explaining how a multi-objective portfolio with mixed horizons aligns to a single 5-year term.
      - The 5-year figure is not matched by any passage in the evidence. The closest figure retrieved is 4.42 in [P4] (Nest fund performance), which is unrelated to time horizon (11.6% away from 5).
    The report asserts a single investment term of 5 years for the risk assessment. The meeting summary [P14] evidences a broader horizon of 'five to ten years', which is consistent in that it includes five years as its lower bound but extends the upper horizon to ten years. This suggests the client is comfortable with horizons spanning the five-year term. However, the report's objectives extract lists six objectives split across three time horizons (ShortTerm, MediumTerm, LongTerm) without assigning specific terms to each or explaining how a single risk level (7) and asset allocation (HSBC 50% / Vanguard 50%) serves all horizons simultaneously. The guard states that 'money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon than the retirement date itself — but the report must say so.' The report does not explicitly address this; it presents a single 5-year term for the risk assessment without documenting how it handles the LongTerm objectives (which would typically extend beyond five years). The extraction metadata identifies an ambiguity at /needsAnalysis/objectives: 'Report lists retirement income objective as £25,000 to £30,000 in retirement but does not clarify if this is household income (including Tony's contribution), personal income only, or gross vs net.' This ambiguity extends to time horizon — retirement objectives typically have horizons to retirement age, not five years, yet this is not reconciled in the report. The meeting summary's 'five to ten years' could reflect a longer objective horizon not captured in the canonical 5-year term, or it could represent a range around an agreed five-year point for one subset of objectives. Without explicit mapping of objectives to horizons in the evidence, and without the report's own explanation of how it applied the five-year term to six objectives with three different horizons, the consistency cannot be fully established. The evidence supports that time horizon was discussed and a range including five to ten years was documented, but does not support the claim that five years is the sole or primary term across all objectives and risk assessment.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a target risk rating of 7 on an unspecified scale. It recommends a bespoke portfolio of two funds: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc at 50% allocation with risk rating 7 and 0.19% ongoing charge, and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity at 50% allocation with risk rating 7 and 0.20% ongoing charge. The report states these funds are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' and that they provide 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution that aligns with your financial goals and risk profile'.
    File:     The evidence contains fund factsheets and risk data. The HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is documented in [P14] as having an internal risk rating of 4 on its own 1-5 scale ('rated as 4, where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk'), and its KIIDS in [P8] and [P14] show it carries a Risk and Reward Indicator of 5. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is documented in [P9] as having a Risk and Reward Indicator (SRRI) of 4. A scatter chart in [P11] labels the recommended portfolio combination as 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR' and plots it against a benchmark labeled 'UT Mixed Investment 40-85% Shares Retail TR' which is labeled 'D' with risk profile characteristics. The evidence in [P17] states the Vanguard fund 'seeks to hold investments that will pay out money and increase in value through a portfolio comprising approximately 80% shares and 20% bonds.' The extraction metadata notes an ambiguity: 'Risk profile stated as 7 - Growth on a scale of 1-10. True Potential plan has risk level 6, Nest has risk level 8. Report recommends funds with risk rating 7 but states existing Nest fund (risk 8) is not in line with the agreed risk level.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts both recommended funds carry a risk rating of 7, but the HSBC fund's own factsheet [P14] states it is 'rated as 4' on its internal 1-5 scale and its KIIDS shows Risk and Reward Indicator 5; the Vanguard fund's KIIDS [P9] shows Risk and Reward Indicator 4, not 7.
      - The report does not state the scale on which risk rating 7 is measured; the funds' own disclosures use different scales (HSBC's internal 1-5 scale and regulatory SRRI categories).
      - The HSBC fund's ongoing charge figure is stated as 0.19% in the report but the nearest comparable figure in the evidence [P20] is 0.23%, a discrepancy of 21.1%.
      - The Vanguard fund's ongoing charge figure is stated as 0.20% in the report but no exact match appears in the evidence; the nearest comparable figure is 0.23% in [P20], a discrepancy of 15%.
    The comparison can be performed on allocations (which total 100% across the two funds) but cannot be completed on risk ratings without establishing the scale. The report asserts risk rating 7 for both funds without stating whether this is on a 1-10 scale, the firm's own scale, or another metric. The individual funds' own factsheets disclose different risk measures: HSBC is internally rated 4 on a 1-5 scale and shows SRRI 5; Vanguard shows SRRI 4. Neither fund individually carries a rating that would naturally be called 7 on any standard scale. The guard states that 'fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale' and recommends checking for a stated combined rating before flagging. No combined rating for the 50/50 blend is stated in the report. The scatter chart [P11] labels the combination as 'Risk 7' suggesting the firm does use a 1-10 scale internally, but this is the report itself repeating its own assertion, not independent corroboration. The evidence shows the funds carry regulatory risk indicators (SRRI) of 5 and 4 respectively, neither of which equals 7. The ongoing charge figures stated (0.19% and 0.20%) do not match exact figures in the evidence for these specific funds; the evidence does not confirm these charges are accurate to the precision claimed. The fund allocations of 50% and 50% total 100% and are internally consistent. The absence of the client's risk questionnaire result and the scale on which the agreed rating of 7 was derived prevents verification that the recommended risk rating equals the client's capacity for loss and attitude to risk. The extraction metadata flags this as an ambiguity: the report states the existing Nest fund (risk 8) 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' yet if the client's agreed level is 7, recommending funds at exactly 7 is appropriate, but no evidence of the assessment method for capacity for loss appears in the evidence pack. The required minimum of 1 corroborating passage (factsheet or research) for fund risk ratings is satisfied by the presence of [P14] for HSBC and [P9] for Vanguard, but these passages show ratings on different scales than the 7 asserted by the report.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the risk profile is 7 on a scale of 1-10 (Growth), and states hasOverride=false with direction='Unchanged', meaning the assessed and agreed ratings are the same. The reconciliation shows fromCode='7' and toCode='7' with no justification required because there is no override. The report includes client acknowledgement text: 'You completed a risk profiling questionnaire, and this was discussed and agreed.' The report's own assertion in the narrative confirms: 'Your risk profile was agreed as 7 on a scale of 1 - 10.'
    File:     The Risk profile report (P2, P3) documents the process by which a natural risk level (from the questionnaire completed 16/01/2026) is used as a starting point, then discussed and adjusted if needed until agreement is reached on the agreed risk level. P2 states: 'The natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point to discuss the actual level of risk you are willing and able to take to meet your specific investment objective.' However, the passages provided do not explicitly state what the natural risk level score was, nor do they explicitly state that the agreed level is 7. P4 describes risk profile as 'seven on a scale of one to ten' and notes it as 'adventurous'. P7 confirms 'Your risk profile assessed as level seven, indicating a preference for growth-oriented investments'. P9 confirms the same: 'At your current risk level of seven out of 10, approximately 80% of your investments would be allocated to growth assets'. P11 (from the report itself) states 'Your risk profile was agreed as 7 on a scale of 1 - 10.' The evidence confirms agreement at level 7 but does not show the natural (questionnaire) score separately or demonstrate that they differ.
    The comparison can be conducted on the basis of what is asserted: the report states hasOverride=false with fromCode='7' and toCode='7', meaning no override occurred. This claim is internally consistent—if both codes are 7, no change has been made and no override justification is needed. The evidence corroborates that the agreed risk level is 7 (confirmed across P4, P7, P9, and P11). However, the evidence does not separately disclose the natural risk level from the questionnaire itself. The passages describe the methodology by which the natural level is taken as a starting point and adjusted through discussion, but they do not state what the natural score was. Consequently, it is not possible to independently verify from the case file whether the natural and agreed levels truly match, or whether they differed and an override occurred that the report failed to flag. The risk profile report (P2, P3) indicates sections for 'Natural risk level' and 'Agreed risk level' but the extracted passages do not include the numerical values for either. Without the natural level figure, the claim of no override (hasOverride=false) cannot be corroborated by independent evidence. This represents a missing component: the questionnaire score itself is not in the file, making it impossible to verify the reconciliation. The report's assertion that the risk profile 'was discussed and agreed' (P11) is consistent with the methodology described, but does not address whether the starting point and end point were the same.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Client's agreed risk profile is 7 on a scale of 1-10. Capacity for loss is rated as 'Medium'. The suitability report recommends an investment strategy with two funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity), each allocated 50%, both with a risk rating of 7. The recommended strategy is stated to align with the agreed risk level of 7. The report states the Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk level 8) 'is not in line with the agreed risk level'.
    File:     The risk profile report [P1] [P2] and suitability report [P3] confirm an agreed risk level of 7 on a scale of 1-10 following interactive discussion. Capacity for loss is documented as 'moderate' [P3]. The two recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity [P5], both rated risk 7 [P5]. Performance comparison [P19] shows 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR' with performance 59.29 and volatility 8.63, versus 'NEST - Higher Risk Pn GTR' with performance 59.28 and volatility 8.50. Both funds' KIIDs [P9] [P17] classify them in risk category 5 (medium to high fluctuations). The Nest Higher Risk Fund is rated 8 on the provider's scale [P6], not on the same 1-10 scale used for the client's agreed profile. Vanguard's KIID [P17] identifies annual volatility range -4.0% to -8.8% over historical periods. Fund fact sheets [P11] [P12] [P13] show asset breakdowns for comparable risk levels. The suitability report's discussion of capacity for loss states: 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' [P3].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Nest fund (risk 8) is 'not in line with the agreed risk level' (7), but does not explicitly state whether the Nest rating of 8 is on the same 1-10 scale or on a different provider scale, creating ambiguity about comparability
      - Report recommends funds with risk rating 7 and states this aligns with agreed profile of 7, but performance data [P19] shows the recommended portfolio (59.29 performance, 8.63 volatility) and the existing Nest fund (59.28 performance, 8.50 volatility) have virtually identical performance and only marginally different volatility, raising a question about the material distinction claimed
      - KIID for Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity [P17] categorises it as risk category 5, not risk 7 in absolute terms, suggesting the risk rating of 7 may be relative to a specific framework rather than standalone
      - Capacity for loss is asserted as 'Medium' [P3] in the suitability report, but no capacity for loss questionnaire result, assessment methodology, or quantified loss scenario is evidenced in the file to support this rating beyond the statement that 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market'
      - The report does not disclose the scale on which the Nest Higher Risk Fund's rating of 8 is based, preventing direct comparison with the client's 1-10 agreed profile of 7
    The report asserts an agreed risk profile of 7 on a 1-10 scale [P3], supported by the risk profile report [P1] [P2] [P14] documenting the agreed level following interactive discussion. The recommended strategy comprises two funds each rated 7 on the same 1-10 scale [P5], creating apparent consistency. However, the supporting evidence reveals material gaps. First, the capacity for loss rating of 'Medium' is asserted in the suitability report [P3] but no underlying assessment, questionnaire, or methodology is produced in the file to evidence how this rating was determined. The statement that 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' [P3] is a conclusion about retirement adequacy, not a quantified capacity for loss assessment or loss tolerance. Second, the report criticises the existing Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk 8) as misaligned with the agreed profile (7), but the Nest rating appears to be on the provider's own scale ('Nest Higher Risk Fund 8' [P6]), not necessarily the same 1-10 scale. The extract states the Nest fund has 'risk level 8' but does not state the scale name; the extraction metadata marks it 'isComparableToClientScale: false', signalling a scale mismatch. Performance data [P19] shows the recommended Risk 7 portfolio and the existing Nest fund have virtually identical performance (59.29 vs 59.28) and similar volatility (8.63 vs 8.50), yet the report asserts the Nest fund 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' without quantifying or explaining why the 1-point difference in provider-specific scales justifies replacement when risk-adjusted returns are comparable. Third, the KIID documents [P9] [P17] classify both recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio) as risk category 5 on the risk-reward indicator scale (1-7), introducing a third scale not reconciled in the report. The report does not explain how risk category 5 on the KIID scale maps to risk 7 on the client's 1-10 scale, leaving the investor's ability to understand actual risk exposure unclear. The comparison of strategy to risk tolerance cannot be fully performed because: (1) the capacity for loss rating lacks supporting evidence of assessment methodology; (2) existing arrangement scales are not mapped to the client scale; (3) no quantified loss scenario is modelled to test whether Medium capacity for loss can absorb the volatility of the recommended strategy (8.63% volatility per [P19] suggests moderate-to-high fluctuation, typical of growth portfolios, but no stress test or drawdown analysis is evidenced). The requirement asks whether the recommended strategy exceeds risk tolerance or capacity for loss. The report asserts alignment without providing the supporting assessment of capacity for loss or evidence that the strategy's volatility aligns with a quantified loss tolerance. The absence of a capacity for loss assessment method or questionnaire result in the file (noted in expectedButAbsent in the extraction metadata) is itself a finding: a claim of Medium capacity for loss is unsupported by documented assessment.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a single agreed risk profile of 7 on a scale of 1-10, labeled 'Growth'. The report states 'Your risk profile was agreed as 7 on a scale of 1 - 10' and describes this as: 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat.' The report indicates no override from assessed to agreed level (hasOverride = false, direction = Unchanged, fromCode = 7, toCode = 7). The methodology is stated as Risk profiling questionnaire, assessed on 16/01/2026 and discussed/agreed with the client. The report's rationale for recommendation is to switch from existing Nest (Higher Risk Fund, risk level 8) to recommended funds that align with the agreed risk level 7.
    File:     The risk profile evidence on file is fragmented and contains multiple labels and scores. [P2] describes the process of deriving an agreed risk level from a natural risk level via interactive discussion considering attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return, mapped to asset class breakdowns. [P1] references projections based on 'agreed risk level' but provides no numerical risk score in the visible text. [P11] (Meeting Summary) describes the client's attitude as 'adventurous, with a risk profile score of seven on a scale of one to ten' and explicitly states this indicates 'a willingness to accept a higher level of risk in pursuit of potentially greater r[eturns]' — consistent with the 'Growth' label. [P13] (Meeting notes) records 'Your risk profile assessed as level seven' and references 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years'. [P5] and [P9] both identify the client as Melanie Anness with an agreed risk level but do not expose the numerical score in the truncated passages. The existing arrangements show: Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk level 8) per the ambiguities note, and True Potential plan (risk level 6 per ambiguities). No conflicting numerical scores for the client's own agreed profile appear, but the file does not corroborate the full risk assessment methodology (capacity for loss assessment method is noted as NotPresentInDocument per extraction metadata). The recommended funds are described as 'risk-profiled solutions' designed to remain within the agreed risk level [P19].
    Discrepancies:
      - The extraction metadata notes that /riskAssessment/capacityForLossAssessmentMethod is NotPresentInDocument, indicating the capacity for loss assessment rationale is not on file despite the report referencing it as part of the agreed risk level process.
      - The report states existing Nest fund (risk level 8) 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' (implying agreed = 7), but no explicit statement in the file rejects or overrides the client's natural risk level upward; the reconciliation shows no override, yet the suitability report asserts moving away from 8 to align with 7.

The client's risk profile on file (score 7, scale 1-10, 'adventurous' and 'Growth') is corroborated across three independent sources: the Risk Profile Report [P2], the Meeting Summary [P11], and the Meeting notes [P13]. However, a material gap exists in the capacity for loss component: the extraction metadata explicitly marks /riskAssessment/capacityForLossAssessmentMethod as NotPresentInDocument, yet [P1] refers to a 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section with the note 'No additional notes were recorded'. This is a document present but silent, not an absent document. The absence of the assessment method creates ambiguity about whether the agreed risk level of 7 was derived from questionnaire score, professional override, or discussion-led adjustment, though the reconciliation (hasOverride = false) suggests no override occurred. No numerical conflict exists for the client's own risk profile across scales. The existing arrangements (Nest 8, True Potential 6) are on different bases (fund risk levels, not client scales) and are not claimed to be on the same scale as the client's agreed 7. The recommended funds' internal categorizations (HSBC category 4, Vanguard 60-80% equity) are presented without explicit reconciliation to the client's 1-10 scale, but the report states they are 'risk-profiled solutions' 'designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' [P19]. The gap is not in the risk score itself but in the evidential trail showing capacity for loss was assessed and how it influenced the final agreed rating.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts an agreed risk profile of 7 on a 1-10 scale ('Growth' label). Capacity for loss is rated 'Medium' (moderate). Investment term is 5 years. The recommended portfolio comprises two funds at 50% each: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 7, active, 0.19% OCF) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (risk rating 7, passive, 0.20% OCF). The report states both funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and that the portfolio provides 'exposure to a range of asset classes and market sectors resulting in greater diversification and reduced risk'. The strategy is described as a 'Blend' of active and passive management on the 'Aviva Platform Personal Pension - Growth Portfolio' with target risk rating 7 on a 1-10 scale and rebalancing based on 'portfolio drift monitoring with regular reviews'.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund documentation (KIID) states it is in risk and reward category 5 (on a 1-7 scale), with historical volatility of 8.63% and asset allocation of approximately 80% equities and 20% bonds/cash. Its objective is to 'provide a return on your investment...within a predefined asset allocation over the long term (5 years or more)'. (2) HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio documentation states it is rated as risk level 4 within the HSBC range (where 1=lower risk, 5=higher risk), and describes itself as a 'Global Strategy Portfolio' aimed at 'provide[ing] growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of 5 years or more'. HSBC's own risk indicator differs from the report's claim. (3) Performance comparison data [P1] shows the combined 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80' portfolio with performance 59.29% and volatility 8.63%, compared against 'NEST - Higher Risk Pn GTR' (59.28% performance, 8.50% volatility). (4) True Potential existing holdings show 'Growth-Aligned Aggressive' fund with OCF 0.54% and 'TrinityBridge Balanced' with OCF 0.69%. (5) Nest Higher Risk Fund is documented with asset allocation showing 40.21% North America equity, 10.64% Global property, and other diversified holdings. (6) No formal capacity for loss assessment method is documented in the file (extraction noted this as absent). (7) Both funds are documented as suitable for 5-year+ holding periods matching the stated term.
    Discrepancies:
      - Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is rated risk category 5 on the SRRI/risk-and-reward scale (1-7 range) per fund KIID, but the report assigns it risk rating 7 on its stated 1-10 scale
      - HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is internally rated as level 4 on HSBC's own 1-5 risk scale per fund documentation [P11], but the report assigns it risk rating 7 on a 1-10 scale
      - The report does not disclose which risk scale or methodology was used to assign risk ratings of 7 to both recommended funds
      - No documented assessment of the client's capacity for loss is presented beyond the rating label 'moderate' — the basis and methodology for this rating is stated as absent from the report ('NotPresentInDocument')
      - The report asserts funds reflect the agreed risk profile 'both individually and combined' but provides no explicit showing of how two funds rated 7 on a 1-10 scale combine to remain at risk 7 when one (Vanguard) is documented at SRRI category 5
    The report recommends two funds, each explicitly rated 7 on a stated 1-10 scale. Fund documentation shows: Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is categorised as SRRI risk 5 (on the regulatory 1-7 scale); HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is rated level 4 on HSBC's proprietary 1-5 scale. A guard in the comparison method states: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' This guard permits the report's use of a distinct 1-10 scale for profiling, separate from funds' own published indicators. However, a second guard requires that 'A multi-asset fund's name ('Balanced', 'Cautious Managed') is marketing, not a risk rating; judge on the allocation and the indicator.' Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity allocation is documented at approximately 80% equities and 20% bonds/cash, consistent with a mid-to-growth risk profile. HSBC's allocation is not fully disclosed in the evidence, but its internal 4/5 rating suggests it too is positioned at a growth-oriented level. Both have minimum 5-year holding periods matching the client's stated 5-year term. The report's claim that funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' rests on the internal consistency of the 1-10 scale within the report itself, not on the funds' native risk indicators. No evidence shows the report has justified this internal rating to either fund manager's documentation or to a standard benchmark. The capacity for loss assessment is stated as 'moderate' based on sufficient assets and alternative income options, but no formal quantified assessment method is documented in the file. The extraction notes the 'capacityForLossAssessmentMethod' is 'NotPresentInDocument'. This is a disclosure gap: a capacity for loss label without disclosed methodology leaves it unsupported. Additionally, the 5-year projection shown (£106,000 at age 89 in real terms at 2.94% mid growth) is not linked to the capacity for loss rating or to a stress test showing the portfolio can withstand material loss while still meeting the stated objective. The term (5 years) is consistent with both fund minimum holding periods and the report's stated investment term.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk: agreed risk profile of 7 on a 1-10 scale, label 'Growth', described as not cautious and viewing risk as opportunity. Methodology: Risk profiling questionnaire. (2) Capacity for Loss: rated 'Medium'/'moderate', based on sufficient pension funds plus inheritance to cover retirement expenditure even after market fall, and option to work beyond age 60 or part-time. (3) Tolerance for Loss: client confirmed acceptance within tolerance based on capacity to suffer losses; no maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount stated. (4) Investment Strategy: two funds each 50% allocation (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc risk 7, and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity risk 7), combined targeting risk rating 7 on 1-10 scale. (5) Reconciliation: no override applied; questionnaire completed, discussed and agreed; risk profile unchanged at 7.
    File:     Evidence from case file: (1) Risk profile report [P1][P2][P3] confirms risk profiling questionnaire methodology, agreement process, and discussion of attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and required return. Report states 'natural risk level' ascertained from psychometric questionnaire used as starting point, then altered until agreement reached. [P3] explains risk levels mapped to asset class collections designed to expose appropriate level of risk. (2) Meeting notes [P4] describe client's attitude as 'adventurous, with a risk profile score of seven on a scale of one to ten', with willingness to accept higher risk for greater returns. [P7] confirms risk profile assessed as level seven with five to ten year investment horizon and client confirmed comfort with risk level. (3) Meeting notes [P8] state 'You have expressed a preference for medium-risk investments' and note at risk level 7 approximately 80% in growth assets, 20% in defensive assets. (4) Risk profile report [P20] contains description '[4] As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat', matching report's language. (5) Nest fund documentation [P21] shows existing Nest Higher Risk Fund rated as 'Very Adventurous' (risk 9 descriptor) with volatility 17.68 and 10-year volatility 9. (6) Fund performance data [P13][P15] shows recommended HSBC and Vanguard funds with 5-year performance of 59.52% and 59.04% respectively; Nest Higher Risk Fund [P18] shows 5-year performance of 59.28%. (7) SAP analysis [P12] projects fund value at retirement age 87 under Aviva Pension Portfolio at £146,000 (at 2.94% growth) versus existing arrangements £134,612.81, marginal difference. (8) Risk comparison [P10] shows 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR' with performance 59.29 and volatility 8.63; Nest Higher Risk has performance 59.28 and volatility 8.50 — virtually identical risk-return profile.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states agreed risk profile is 7; Nest fund documentation [P21] describes existing Nest holding as 'Very Adventurous' with risk descriptor for scale level 9, not 7.
      - Report states capacity for loss is 'Medium'/'moderate'; meeting notes [P8] describe client preference for 'medium-risk investments' which conflates attitude to risk with capacity for loss.
      - Report asserts tolerance for loss confirmed by client as 'within acceptable tolerance'; no quantified loss scenario, maximum acceptable loss percentage, or loss tolerance assessment document appears in file.
      - Report recommends switch from existing Nest (risk 8 per canonical model) to recommended portfolio (risk 7); however [P10] comparative analysis shows Nest Higher Risk Fund performance 59.28% and volatility 8.50 vs recommended Risk 7 portfolio performance 59.29% and volatility 8.63 — performance and risk virtually identical, suggesting no material risk misalignment between existing and recommended.
      - Report states capacity for loss based on 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient'; canonical model notes inheritance of £350,000 is 'pending legal proceedings' and not included in current net asset value of £377,000, creating basis ambiguity in CFL assessment.
    The requirement is to identify unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy. (1) ATTITUDE TO RISK CONSISTENCY: Report asserts agreed risk 7 on 1-10 scale with 'Growth' label and specific description. This is corroborated by [P1][P2][P3] confirming risk profiling questionnaire methodology and agreement process, and [P4][P8] confirming client characterization as 'adventurous' with risk profile 7 and 80/20 growth/defensive split at this level. No contradiction on ATR. (2) CAPACITY FOR LOSS LABELLING: Report rates CFL as 'Medium' and 'moderate'. [P8] references 'medium-risk investments' but this describes attitude to risk, not capacity for loss. The report's two assessment bases ('sufficient assets' and 'alternative income') are stated without reference to any CFL scale, methodology or rating framework. The canonical model flags capacityForLossAssessmentMethod as 'NotPresentInDocument', meaning no formal CFL process document is in the file. The 'Medium' rating is asserted but its basis on any defined scale or questionnaire is not evidenced. This is a weak justification: the label exists, the bases are stated, but the connection to a 'Medium' conclusion is unexplained. (3) TOLERANCE FOR LOSS: Report states 'you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance' without quantifying what 'acceptable' means or what loss scenarios were modelled. No tolerance for loss percentage, amount, or scenario-tested outcome document is in the file. This is unsupported: the report asserts client acknowledgement of tolerance but provides no evidence of what tolerance was discussed or accepted. (4) INVESTMENT STRATEGY RISK RATING ALIGNMENT: Report states recommended funds HSBC and Vanguard each carry risk rating 7 and combined target risk 7. Both funds are documented [P13][P15] with specified performance and allocation. However, comparative data [P10] shows the recommended Risk 7 portfolio (performance 59.29, volatility 8.63) is effectively identical in risk-return terms to existing Nest Higher Risk Fund (performance 59.28, volatility 8.50). The canonical model states existing Nest fund is risk 8; report recommends switching to risk 7 on grounds that 'current fund is not in line with the agreed risk level'. Yet the evidence shows no material risk difference — both are 'Very Adventurous' tier on performance/volatility metrics [P21]. This is internally inconsistent: the report justifies switch by risk misalignment where the evidence shows none. (5) CAPACITY FOR LOSS ASSESSMENT BASIS: Report states CFL conclusion based on 'pension funds along with the inheritance'. However, canonical model notes inheritance of £350,000 is pending legal proceedings; SAP projections [P12] include existing arrangements value £53,497.38 vs proposed £58,200 at 0% growth, a minimal difference. The CFL assessment does not explain whether the £350,000 inheritance is included in 'pension funds' or treated separately, or whether the CFL is assessed on current £377,000 or projected £727,000. This is unexplained: the assessment references an asset whose inclusion in the calculation is ambiguous. (6) INVESTMENT TERM: Report does not explicitly state investment term/time horizon in the canonical extraction under riskAssessment. Meeting notes [P7] state 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years', but this is not cross-referenced in the suitability report risk or strategy sections. No contradiction but a gap in evidence for term. (7) RECONCILIATION OF EXISTING TO RECOMMENDED: Report states no override (reconciliation direction 'Unchanged', from 7 to 7). Risk profile not altered. However, the report also states existing Nest fund 'is not in line with agreed risk level' [per canonical model statement in internalInconsistencies]. If agreed risk is 7 and Nest is 8, that is a mismatch. Yet [P10] shows performance and volatility of both are indistinguishable. The explanation for switching (risk misalignment) is not supported by the evidence of identical risk-return profiles. This is a weak justification: a recommendation is made on a basis (risk misalignment) that the comparative data does not support. (8) GUARD APPLICATION: Standard boilerplate risk warnings are not assessed. The description of 'Growth' investor [P20] is boilerplate risk language, not unsupported as to this client. The CFL statement that 'pension funds along with inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' is client-specific and outcome-dependent; it is stated but not quantified or loss-scenario-modelled. (9) VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: No vulnerability indicators are recorded in the evidence relating to the client's ability to understand or accept the risk profile, capacity for loss, or investment strategy. Meeting notes confirm client understanding and agreement. No accessibility, communication or cognitive support needs are noted that would affect the risk assessment. Proceeding without overlay effect.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  N/A
    Report:   The report states that all three existing arrangements are being fully transferred (adviceAction = 'FullTransfer' for all arrangements). Therefore, no arrangements are being retained. The report asserts an agreed risk level of 7 on a 1-10 scale. The existing arrangements contain funds with stated risk levels: Quilter arrangement (Risk Level 6, comparable to client scale); True Potential arrangement (no specific risk level stated in extraction, but holdings include Growth Aligned Aggressive and Aggressive funds); Nest arrangement (Risk Level 8, stated as 'Nest Higher Risk Fund 8', marked as not comparable to client scale).
    File:     The evidence confirms the existence of three existing arrangements. Passage [P3] explains the risk profiling methodology, stating that an agreed risk level is reached through discussion using projections based on attitude to risk questionnaire, investment amount, target and time horizon, with risk levels mapped to asset class breakdowns. Passages [P1], [P7], [P8] describe individual fund factsheets for True Potential funds (Growth Aligned Aggressive, SEI Aggressive, UBS Aggressive) showing them as higher-risk, equity-heavy strategies. Passage [P2] shows Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is rated 4 on its risk/reward profile scale (not on a 1-10 scale). Passage [P9] is a Fact Find showing existing Aviva wrap arrangement. The evidence does not provide explicit risk ratings aligned to a comparable 1-10 scale for the funds held in the True Potential arrangement, nor does it map the 'Nest Higher Risk Fund 8' label to the agreed client risk scale of 7.
    Discrepancies:
      - The requirement asks whether funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating. However, the report states all three arrangements are subject to 'FullTransfer' advice, meaning no arrangements are retained. This creates a logical mismatch: the requirement is inapplicable if no funds are being retained in any arrangement.
      - The Nest arrangement is stated to have risk level 8, the True Potential arrangement holds funds described as Aggressive (higher-risk), while the agreed client risk profile is 7. If any of these were retained, there would be a misalignment between holdings and agreed profile.
      - The report's extraction shows True Potential holdings include 'Growth Aligned Aggressive' (23.06%), 'SEI Aggressive' (15.54%), and 'UBS Aggressive' (21.64%), constituting approximately 60% in explicitly aggressive funds, yet no aggregate or composite risk rating is stated for the True Potential arrangement as a whole to compare against the agreed level 7.
      - Nest Higher Risk Fund is marked as risk level 8 on a provider-specific scale ('Nest Higher Risk Fund' scale name), not on the 1-10 scale. The extraction notes it is not comparable to client scale (isComparableToClientScale = false), creating a comparison barrier.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The requirement presupposes retention of one or more arrangements. The report recommends full transfer of all three arrangements, so no comparison of retained fund risk against agreed client profile can be performed. No arrangements remain to assess., Even if any arrangement were retained, the True Potential arrangement risk rating is absent: no single risk level is stated for the portfolio of six funds held within it, only individual fund holdings listed. A composite risk assessment for True Potential holdings is not on file., The Nest fund risk level (8) is stated on a provider-specific scale not confirmed as compatible with the 1-10 client scale, and no mapping or conversion is provided. Comparability cannot be established., Capacity for loss assessment method is recorded as 'NotPresentInDocument' (per expectedButAbsent), so no capacity-for-loss basis exists to cross-check whether agreed level 7 itself remains supported.
    The requirement [G3.11] is triggered only where funds are held inside a retained arrangement and must align with the agreed risk rating. The suitability report recommends full transfer of all three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest). No arrangements are retained under the advice. Consequently, the comparison cannot be performed because the trigger condition — funds inside a retained arrangement — is absent. The extraction explicitly states all adviceAction values are 'FullTransfer'. This is not a weakness in evidence; it is a logical inapplicability. The guard on this requirement states: 'This group is about a plan the advice keeps, not one it moves.' Where the advice is to move all plans, this requirement does not apply. However, the check instructions note that where trigger is satisfied at the case level (hasInvestmentOrPensionRecommendation = true), the check must still be assessed. The trigger is the presence of a recommendation, not the presence of retention. A suitability report containing only transfer recommendations can still trigger G3.11 if the canonical model extraction erroneously flags arrangements as retained. Examining the canonical model: all three arrangements show adviceAction = 'FullTransfer'. Under a strict reading, if no arrangement is retained, comparisonPerformed should be false and outcome should be NotApplicable. However, the decision logic states: 'N/A: Trigger absent.' The trigger at case level is satisfied (investment recommendation exists). The sub-trigger (arrangement retained) is not satisfied in the data. The requirement cannot be assessed because its precondition is not met: there are no retained funds to evaluate. This is a genuine N/A state, not a Potential Concern. No evidence gap exists; the advice structure itself displaces the requirement.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report asserts knowledge level as 'Good' but the canonical model field `/knowledgeAndExperience/perClient[]/statedLevel` records this as 'Good' — this internal extraction appears consistent. However, there is no independent assessment of knowledge grading in the fact find documents themselves [P16] or [P11] which show blank fields for knowledge assessment. The report narrative supports the 'Good' grading through reference to accounting background, currency trading experience, and self-reported comfort checking fund values, but no contemporaneous fact find document records knowledge as graded or assessed to that level prior to the suitability opinion. Report states client 'invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' — fact find records three existing pensions but does not confirm whether advice was taken on them or when they were established or reviewed. Report states client 'regularly checks the value of funds' — fact find and risk profile report contain no record of this behaviour or frequency. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client's knowledge level as 'Good'. The report narrative states: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds. You feel you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to [investments].' The report characterises the client as having 'reasonable understanding of non-complex investments' and notes 'past experience as a currency trader, which has given you a foundational understanding of market dynamics and risk management.' The client is described as a 'seasoned professional' with 'solid understanding of financial matters' and the report states she 'find[s] financial matters fairly easy to understand'.
    File:     The fact find and risk assessment documents evidence a client with professional accounting background and previous currency trading experience. [P11] describes the client as 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading' and notes 'Your career as an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited' with earning history. [P2] characterises the investment experience as 'medium, noting your reasonable understanding of non-complex investments' and states this 'level of experience supports your adventurous risk profile'. [P22], drawn from the suitability report itself [I], reiterates: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds.' The risk profiling and meeting summaries consistently refer to the client's professional background and market awareness without contradiction. No fact find entry records knowledge assessment in a standardised scale or grade.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts knowledge level as 'Good' but the canonical model field `/knowledgeAndExperience/perClient[]/statedLevel` records this as 'Good' — this internal extraction appears consistent. However, there is no independent assessment of knowledge grading in the fact find documents themselves [P16] or [P11] which show blank fields for knowledge assessment. The report narrative supports the 'Good' grading through reference to accounting background, currency trading experience, and self-reported comfort checking fund values, but no contemporaneous fact find document records knowledge as graded or assessed to that level prior to the suitability opinion.
    The report asserts the client's knowledge level as 'Good' and this is supported by multiple corroborating passages. The backing evidence comes from: (1) professional background in accounting [P11]; (2) prior currency trading experience [P2, P11, P22]; (3) demonstrated pension investment behaviour without advice [P22]; (4) regular monitoring of fund values [P22]; and (5) the client's own statement of 'fair degree of knowledge' [P22]. These facts appear consistently across the meeting summaries [P2, P11], the risk profile discussion [P22], and the suitability report itself. The complexity of recommended products (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio, both actively managed multi-asset funds) is proportionate to a client assessed as having medium-to-good investment knowledge, a risk profile of 7/10 described as 'Growth' investor, and an understanding of market dynamics from prior currency trading. The report's rationale adequately justifies the knowledge level by grounding it in specific, evidenced prior experience. No fact find document independently rates knowledge on a numerical or categorical scale [P16, P11], which represents a potential documentation gap — the knowledge level stated in the report is supported by narrative evidence but not by a completed knowledge assessment questionnaire or form. However, this is an absence of a form, not a contradiction. The report does not overstate the client's knowledge relative to the supporting facts; rather, it characterises as 'Good' a client whose documented background and behaviour reasonably support that characterisation. The vulnerability overlay is not engaged: no vulnerability drivers or support needs are identified in the evidence that would materially affect the assessment of knowledge appropriateness.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states: 'You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds.' The report also notes: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters.' The report records pension product experience as held=true, with no years of experience quantified. The report characterises the client as having 'a fair degree of knowledge and understanding' and describes prior self-directed investing in pensions.
    File:     The fact find (P7, P14) records three existing pension arrangements: Aviva Personal Pension (transferred value £351,493.34, current value £294,867.94, policy start 13/04/2026), True Potential pension (current value £46,672.80), and Nest Higher Risk Fund (current value £21,272.64). The meeting summary (P22) confirms: 'You have taken a proactive approach to managing your pension investments, reflecting your background as a former currency trader. You have opted for a higher risk strategy within your NEST pension...For your True Potential pension, you have chosen a medium-risk strategy...This strategic allocation demonstrates your understanding of risk management.' The risk profile report (P8, P11, P13) records that discussions took place on attitude to risk, investor experience, capacity for loss, and investment target, but the specific notes are not disclosed — it states 'No additional notes were recorded' and sections are blank. The fact find shows the client confirmed pension scheme membership but does not record the claim of 'investing without advice' or systematic fund checking behaviour. The existing holdings (P1, P2, P3, P4) show the Nest fund holds 100% Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk level 9, described as 'Very Adventurous'), and True Potential holds a Balanced Growth fund (risk level 6, described as 'Growth'). The Aviva arrangement is listed in the fact find but the fund composition is not detailed in the evidence pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client 'invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' — fact find records three existing pensions but does not confirm whether advice was taken on them or when they were established or reviewed.
      - Report states client 'regularly checks the value of funds' — fact find and risk profile report contain no record of this behaviour or frequency.
      - Report characterises client as having 'a fair degree of knowledge and understanding' — this is consistent with existing holdings showing proactive self-directed choice of risk profiles (Nest risk 9, True Potential risk 6) but the basis for grading is not documented in the risk profile notes, which are blank (P8, P11).
      - Existing holdings show asymmetric risk positioning: Nest Higher Risk Fund at risk level 9 (Very Adventurous per P4) and True Potential at risk level 6 (Balanced Growth per P1), which the meeting summary (P22) characterises as deliberate 'strategic allocation' reflecting understanding of risk management, yet the report does not explain how a client holding risk-9 funds can be recommended risk-7 funds without discussing the apparent downgrade.
    The report's assertion that the client 'invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' cannot be corroborated or contradicted by the evidence. The fact find records three existing pension arrangements with current values and one transfer value, but does not record whether advice was taken on their selection, transfer, or ongoing management. The meeting summary (P22) and report narrative describe the client as having made deliberate risk choices (higher risk in Nest, medium risk in True Potential), which is consistent with active self-directed management, but this does not explicitly confirm the absence of prior advice. The report's characterisation of the client's background in accounting and currency trading is stated but not independently verified in the fact find. The fact find does not record the client's profession or trading background as information provided by the client during fact-finding. The meeting summary (P22) confirms the background: 'reflecting your background as a former currency trader,' suggesting this was discussed and is on file, but the primary fact-find document does not contain this detail. The report's statement that the client 'regularly checks the value of your funds' is not recorded anywhere in the evidence pack — the risk profile notes are blank (P8, P11), and the fact find contains no note of this behaviour. This represents an unsupported assertion about client conduct. The report grades the client's knowledge as 'fair' and suitable for growth/complex investing. The basis for this grading is not documented. The risk profile questionnaire is referred to (P11: 'Your natural risk level is based on the answers given in the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire which was completed on 16/01/2026'), but the actual questionnaire responses, psychometric score, or adviser assessment notes are not in the pack. The risk profile report (P8, P11, P13) explicitly states 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Investor Experience,' which is the section that would document prior investment experience and understanding. This is a material omission: the suitability report relies on the client's knowledge to justify complex pension fund recommendations, yet the evidence of how that knowledge was assessed is absent from the file. The existing holdings do provide some corroboration: the client has held three pension schemes (Quilter, True Potential, Nest) with different risk profiles (6, 8, 9 depending on fund choice within each), suggesting some engagement with risk selection. The meeting summary (P22) describes the client as having 'taken a proactive approach' and made deliberate choices reflecting 'understanding of risk management.' This is consistent with the report's narrative but does not quantify or formally assess the level of knowledge or experience. The guard requiring the assessor to distinguish between holding a workplace pension (passive) and self-directed investing applies here: the client appears to have actively chosen risk profiles within pension schemes, which is self-directed; however, the fact that the client holds pensions does not itself prove understanding of pension complexity or investment fundamentals. The report does not distinguish between the client's familiarity with pension administration (e.g., holding an account, reviewing statements) and understanding of investment principles, fund selection, or the complexity of the recommended products. The guard that questionnaires ask separately about shares, unit trusts, tax-advantaged schemes, and pensions, and that understanding of one does not prove understanding of another, applies: the file does not provide the investor-experience questionnaire responses, so it is not possible to verify what the client stated about understanding of pensions specifically versus other asset classes. In summary: (1) The claim of 'investing without advice' is neither supported nor contradicted but remains unverified. (2) The claim of 'regularly checking funds' is unsupported and absent from the file. (3) The grading of knowledge as 'fair' relies on discussions not documented in the risk profile notes (which are blank), creating a gap between assertion and evidence. (4) The existing holdings provide circumstantial evidence of proactive engagement but do not formally document the knowledge assessment. (5) The vulnerability overlay: No evidence of vulnerability, support needs, communication requirements or reasonable adjustments is present in the pack, so the overlay does not materially affect this finding.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension is 'NonComplex', characterised as a personal pension with standard pension freedoms, straightforward platform access to named funds, and flexi-access drawdown via standard mechanism. The report states the recommended funds are multi-asset funds that are 'risk-profiled solutions' designed to remain within the client's agreed risk level. The recommendation rationale emphasises that these funds provide 'professionally managed, diversified investment solution', combining 'growth potential of equities with potentially uncorrelated returns of other asset classes, such as bonds, property and absolute return'. The report does not explicitly record the client's stated knowledge level, investment experience, or existing holdings that would evidence the client's capability to understand the product.
    File:     The case file contains Aviva Pension Key Features Document (P1), Terms and Conditions (P2, P3, P4, P5, P8, P10, P11, P12, P13, P14, P15, P16, P20), and fund literature for the recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio - P9, P18, P22, P24). The Aviva T&Cs detail complex operational features including Smooth Managed Funds with smoothed pricing mechanics, Commercial Property investment options, Structured Products, discretionary investment models, phased drawdown, capped drawdown, flexi-access drawdown mechanics with tax consequences post-age-75, and fund price adjustments. The fund KIIDs (P18, P24) classify the funds at risk levels 5 and 4 respectively, note use of derivatives for efficient portfolio management, and contain material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, and derivatives risk. The suitability report (P6, P17, P21) contains general descriptions of personal pensions and flexi-access drawdown but does not record any fact-find data on the client's prior investment knowledge, experience, or existing holdings that would evidence understanding of the recommended solution.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report classifies the Aviva Platform Personal Pension as 'NonComplex' but the Terms and Conditions (P2-P16, P20) reveal multiple complex operational features: Smooth Managed Funds with complex smoothed pricing mechanics (Band of 5%-10% smoothing, reference to Bank of England Base Rate), discretionary investment manager model portfolios, structured products with provider-specific terms, commercial property with separate T&Cs and mortgage risks, multiple drawdown options (capped, phased, flexi-access) with different tax consequences depending on age at death and timing, and fund price adjustment mechanics.
      - The report does not provide any recorded evidence of the client's prior investment knowledge, experience or existing holdings to support the assertion that complexity is proportionate to the client's demonstrated capability.
      - The recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic at risk 4 and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% at implied risk level based on 80% equity content) are multi-asset solutions with active derivatives usage (P18, P24), material risks including counterparty, credit, default and derivatives risk, yet no evidence is provided that the client has been tested on or demonstrated understanding of derivatives, counterparty risk, or active management mechanics.
      - The suitability report describes the funds as 'straightforward' and 'simplified' multi-asset solutions (P17) but the KIIDs (P18, P24) indicate these are actively managed funds that may diverge from benchmarks and use derivatives; the report provides no explanation bridging this characterisation gap for a client with no recorded investment experience.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Client's stated investment knowledge level or rating, Client's prior investment experience (length, types of investments, frequency of trading), Details of client's existing holdings (evidence of past investment decisions and experience), Record of client questions, concerns or comprehension checks on product complexity, Any vulnerability assessment or support needs that might affect the client's ability to understand complex features, Documentation of client acknowledgement or sign-off on product complexity or feature explanations
    The report's assertion that the recommended product is 'NonComplex' cannot be adequately compared against evidence of the client's knowledge and experience because no fact-find record of the client's investment knowledge, prior experience, or existing holdings appears in the case file. This is not a case where the comparison shows misalignment; it is a case where one side of the comparison is entirely absent from the available evidence. The Aviva Platform Terms and Conditions (P2-P16, P20) and the fund KIIDs (P18, P24) demonstrate that the product wrapper contains material complexity: smoothed pricing mechanics for certain funds, structured product capabilities with provider-specific terms, commercial property with separate governance, multiple drawdown options with age-dependent tax consequences, derivatives usage, and various material risks. While the mainstream guard ('a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex') would normally apply, it applies only where the client has been assessed and recorded as capable of understanding such a product. Here, no such assessment exists. The report's characterisation of the funds as providing 'simplicity' (P17) conflicts with the fund literature showing active management, derivatives usage, and material risks not fully captured by the risk-reward indicator (P18, P24). Without evidence of the client's prior investment knowledge or existing holdings, the proportionality of this complexity to the client's demonstrated capability cannot be established. The vulnerability overlay asks whether support needs or characteristics identified affect the assessment; no such assessment appears in the file for this requirement either.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the client's stated knowledge level is 'Good' and that the recommended products are 'NonComplex'. The report states: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds. You feel you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to [text truncated].' The report characterises the client as a Growth investor with risk profile 7/10, suited to this approach because 'If you have experience of investments and find financial matters fairly easy to understand then you may be suited to a Growth approach.' The report recommends a diversified multi-asset portfolio (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic 50%, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity 50%) as 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution' providing 'simplicity, cost-efficiency, and flexibility.'
    File:     The file consistently supports the report's characterisation of the client's knowledge level as good and experience as substantial. [P5] records the client's investment experience as 'medium, noting your reasonable understanding of non-complex investments,' which aligns with 'Good' knowledge. [P5] confirms the client has 'past experience as a currency trader, which has given you a foundational understanding of market dynamics and risk management.' [P14] states: 'You are a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters.' [P6] documents proactive pension management: 'You have taken a proactive approach to managing your pension investments, reflecting your background as a former currency trader. You have opted for a higher risk strategy within your NEST pension... For your True Potential pension, you have chosen a medium-risk strategy, balancing your desire for growth with a level of caution appropriate for the larger sum involved.' [P6] records the client's engagement with complex pension options: 'You expressed a keen interest in the flexibility offered by the drawdown option for accessing your pension funds... You remain open to exploring other options, such as annuities.' The recommended funds are confirmed as non-complex multi-asset funds. [P4] describes HSBC and Vanguard funds as investing 'in a range of asset types such as shares of companies (equities), bonds and property' with aims to 'track the performance of market indices' and 'focus on lower ongoing charges.' [P5] confirms the recommendation aligns with the client's stated preferences: 'a diversified investment portfolio that includes a mix of growth and defensive assets, tailored to your risk tolerance and financial objectives. At a risk level of seven, approximately 80% of your investments would be allocated to growth assets, such as equities... The remaining 20% would be invested in defensive assets, like bonds.'
    The report's assertion that the client's knowledge level is 'Good' is directly corroborated by multiple passages in the file. The client's background in accounting and currency trading, confirmed in [P14] as providing 'solid understanding of financial matters,' supports the 'Good' characterisation. [P5] explicitly grades the investment experience as 'medium, noting your reasonable understanding of non-complex investments,' which is consistent with 'Good' general knowledge. The evidence of proactive engagement documented in [P6]—managing pension investments across multiple providers, deliberately choosing different risk levels for different pots, and exploring complex options like drawdown versus annuities—demonstrates the knowledge level is accurately reflected. The recommended products are non-complex diversified funds. [P4] confirms both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) are multi-asset index-tracking funds with low charges and broad exposure to standard asset classes (equities, bonds, property). These are straightforward diversified funds, not niche or structured products. The file supports that this complexity level is appropriate. [P5] records the adviser's explanation of the 80/20 equity-to-defensive allocation, and [P5] confirms the client understood and agreed this approach: 'Your investment strategy is informed by your past experience as a currency trader.' The client's risk profile of 7/10 (Growth) is justified in [P8] through documented discussion of natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss, and required return. [P1] states the client 'do[es] not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat' and 'understand[s] that some risk needs to be taken in order to achieve investment growth.' The rationale for recommending non-complex funds rather than investment pathways is provided in [P9]: the client 'require[s] a more tailored solution' and 'a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions,' reflecting the client's specific circumstances post-divorce, inheritance considerations, and staged retirement approach. No material contradiction exists between what the report states about the client's knowledge and what the file evidences. The recommended product complexity is proportionate: non-complex diversified funds suit a client with good knowledge, accounting background, and currency-trading experience who has actively managed multiple pension arrangements and engaged with complex pension options. The supporting rationale is explicit and coherent: the risk profile was agreed through documented discussion, the fund selection is justified by the client's circumstances and preferences, and the rejection of simpler pathways is explained by need for customisation.
