  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's background in accounting and currency trading has equipped her with 'a solid understanding of financial matters'. The report states she 'feel[s] you have a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning' and that she 'regularly check[s] the value of your funds'. The report recommends two complex investment funds: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (50% allocation) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (50% allocation), presented as suitable for a client with stated knowledge.
    File:     The case file contains multiple fact-find and meeting summaries. [P1] confirms the client's background: 'You are a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters' and notes she 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio'. [P3] records the client as 'currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce', with the primary concern being 'transfer of your pension information'. [P18] records a risk profile assessment as level 7 on a scale of 1-10, with the risk level justification noting 'The following areas were discussed as part of the process to agree the risk level', but the specific notes on 'Investor Experience', 'Attitude to Risk', 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', and 'Other notes' are all blank or empty. [P2] records a meeting aimed at presenting recommendations where 'Your risk profile had been assessed as a level seven'. No evidence is recorded in the file of the client asking questions about the specific features of the recommended funds, their complexity, or demonstrating comprehension of their risk characteristics beyond the risk rating number itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has 'a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning', but no specific pension investment experience or existing holdings in pension funds prior to the divorce settlement are documented in the file. The only holdings mentioned are in Quilter, True Potential, and Nest pension schemes inherited from the divorce settlement, not self-directed investments.
      - The report states the client 'regularly check[s] the value of your funds', but the file records she 'do[es] not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio' as of the fact-find meeting on 20/02/2026 ([P1]). No evidence contradicts this between early February and the recommendation date.
      - The risk profile assessment template in [P18] shows blank entries for 'Investor Experience', 'Attitude to Risk', 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', and 'Other notes', despite the requirement to document discussion of these areas. This means the risk level 7 was agreed but the reasoning based on investor experience is not recorded.
      - No record exists in the file of client questions, comprehension checks, or explanations of the specific features of the two recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity), their derivative or hedging strategies, their drawdown characteristics, or their alignment with the client's post-divorce financial objectives beyond the risk rating.
    The requirement asks whether evidence shows the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product based on their questions and comprehension. The check applies because this is an advised case. The guard states: 'An absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand — report it as an evidence gap, weighted by product complexity.' The recommended funds are moderately complex. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity fund ([P21]) explicitly discloses use of derivatives, counterparty risk, liquidity risk, credit risk, and inflation risk — features requiring explanation. The HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is named as 'Dynamic' and includes a stated allocation strategy suggesting active rebalancing. The report's assertion of the client's knowledge level is broadly consistent with [P1], which confirms her professional background in accounting and currency trading. However, the file reveals no material inconsistency with the self-assessed 'fair degree of knowledge' in pension planning, though the statement that she 'regularly check[s] the value of your funds' is not corroborated by evidence she holds any stocks-and-shares investments prior to this advice. The critical gap is the absence of any recorded questions, explanations of the funds' features, or evidence of comprehension documented by the adviser after the initial recommendation presentation. The risk profile assessment form [P18] contains empty fields where discussion of investor experience should have been recorded, suggesting either the discussion did not occur or was not documented. Given the product complexity (two multi-asset funds with derivative and dynamic features), the regulatory basis (COBS 9.2.1R requires knowledge and experience assessment; COBS 9A.2.1R requires suitability determination; PRIN requires fair treatment), and the vulnerability overlay (the client is post-divorce, in a transitional life phase, and [P5] records she left employment at 55), the absence of recorded comprehension evidence is a material gap. The file does not show that the client was walked through the funds' specific risk features, asked questions that demonstrated understanding, or given explanations tailored to her stated (but not independently verified) pension knowledge level. The guard permits reporting this as an evidence gap; it is weighted high by the moderate complexity of the funds and the post-divorce transitional context that may affect the client's capacity to absorb complex information.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states: 'You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years.' If you have experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced Growth approach... you may also be suited to this approach if you make investment decisions reasonably quickly and don't tend to be particularly anxious about those decisions.' The Nest holdings assessment [P17] categorises the Nest Higher Risk Fund as 'Very Adventurous' [4], describing: 'If you have substantial experience of investments and find financial matters easy to understand then you may be suited to a Very Adventurous approach.' The file thus contains categorisations that assume experience, but these are derived from fund risk profiles and portfolio allocation, not independent assessment of the client's knowledge or demonstrated competency. The Fact Find [P2] contains no explicit statement of years of pension experience, prior self-directed investment history, or formal assessment of knowledge level.
    Discrepancies:

The Fact Find appears to record different or incomplete holdings information.

      - The True Potential holdings assessment [P11] assumes 'Balanced Growth' suitability based on risk profiling, but states 'If you have experience of investments and a degree of understanding' — this is a conditional assumption, not a confirmation the client meets this criterion.
    The suitability report asserts the client 'invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years,' a statement that directly contradicts the Fact Find record [P2] showing the Aviva pension held 'Under Agency - Servicing Agent' status, suggesting advisory involvement. The Fact Find header states 'Client did not disclose' regarding Personal Pensions, which contradicts the report's assertion that the client 'confirmed' experience. The holdings themselves (six True Potential funds ranging from Balanced to Aggressive, plus Nest Higher Risk Fund) evidence diversified pension experience, but the file provides no independent assessment confirming the client's knowledge of these products or conscious selection. The Defaqto and Nest categorisations [P11, P17] are derived from fund risk profiles and portfolio composition, not from documented client knowledge assessment. They use conditional language ('If you have experience...') rather than confirming experience exists. The Fact Find contains no graded knowledge assessment, years-of-experience statement, or evidence of client comprehension of complex fund strategies. While the holdings corroborate exposure to multiple fund types, they do not prove the client understood what they held or selected them independently. The report's primary assertion — that the client invested 'without taking any advice' — directly conflicts with the Aviva arrangement's recorded 'Servicing Agent' status. This is a material inconsistency between the report's narrative and the file evidence regarding the basis and nature of prior experience.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommended products are NonComplex, with complexity drivers limited to 'Personal pension with standard pension freedoms', 'Straightforward platform access to named funds', and 'Flexi-access drawdown via standard mechanism'. The report states the client's background: 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters. You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds.' The report provides nine rationale statements supporting the recommendation, including cost savings (0.19% vs 0.27% platform charges), investment flexibility, ongoing oversight, client preference, tax efficiency, knowledge and experience alignment, risk alignment, diversification, and blend of active and passive investments.
    File:     The evidence records the client as 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters. Your career as an accountant at Bridgeport Interiors Limited, where you earn an annual salary of £42,000, reflects your expertise.' Meeting summaries confirm 'You confirmed that you have invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and you regularly check the value of your funds.' The risk profile assessment (P17) records 'Agreed risk level' but the supporting notes sections for 'Attitude to Risk', 'Investor Experience', 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', 'Investment target', and 'Other notes' all show '[blank]' or 'No additional notes were recorded'. Product literature (P9, P13) describes the HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio as 'actively managed' and the Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity as a multi-asset fund. The Aviva Pension KFD (P14) outlines standard pension freedoms including flexible drawdown, disinvestment, and restrictions on certain jurisdictions, confirming these are standard features. No record exists in the file of client questions, expressions of understanding, or explanations given regarding the product features or their suitability to the client's knowledge level.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk profile assessment document (P17) records that 'No additional notes were recorded' in sections addressing Attitude to Risk, Investor Experience, Capacity for Loss, and Investment Target, yet the report relies on these factors to justify the client's knowledge level and experience; the file does not evidence the discussions claimed to have occurred
      - The report provides a rationale emphasizing 'blend of active and passive investments' and 'greater diversification' but the evidence does not record any discussion of these features with the client or confirmation of the client's understanding of active vs passive management
      - The report states platform consolidation 'enables active adviser monitoring and adjustment of investments' but no evidence records client discussion or acknowledgment of this ongoing management model
      - The report cites client knowledge from 'accounting and currency trading' background but does not evidence how this translates to understanding of pension product complexity, multi-asset funds, or drawdown mechanics
    The requirement asks whether the client's knowledge, experience and investment understanding are accurately reflected, supported by evidence, and whether product complexity is consistent with demonstrated knowledge, with adequate rationale. The report classifies the recommended products as NonComplex. The client's knowledge level is drawn from documented background in accounting and currency trading, confirmed investment in pensions without advice within 5 years, and regular checking of fund values—these are corroborated by meeting summaries (P2, P8). However, the evidence supporting the risk profile assessment is incomplete: P17 shows blank sections for attitude to risk, investor experience, capacity for loss questionnaire, investment target, and other notes that would typically record discussion of these factors. The rationale provided spans nine topics, several of which—active vs passive management, diversification benefit, ongoing adjustment capability—do not appear to be grounded in documented client discussion or demonstrated understanding. The Aviva platform, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity, and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio are described in product literature (P9, P13, P14) as standard pension products with straightforward features (pension freedoms, platform access, drawdown), supporting the NonComplex classification. The client's background in accounting and financial markets is relevant to her knowledge level, but the file does not evidence that this background has been connected to discussion of the specific products recommended or that the client has demonstrated understanding of multi-asset fund mechanics or drawdown administration. The absence of recorded discussion points, client questions, or evidence of comprehension is notable given the report emphasizes platform consolidation, active adviser monitoring, and blend of active and passive strategies—features that warrant confirmation of client understanding where the product is presented as having these characteristics. The report's rationale does not always rest on documented client-specific discussion: for example, the rationale on 'Platform consolidation enables active adviser monitoring' is presented as a benefit to the client but no evidence records whether the client values this or understands what it entails. The rationale citing 'Your background in accounting and currency trading' (P2) does support a claim of financial knowledge, but this is general background, not demonstrated understanding of the specific product complexity or drawdown mechanics being recommended.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report assumes retirement expenditure of £2,000 net per month (personal only); Fact Find records current household expenditure of £2,836.23 monthly with no stated reduction. The basis for the £2,000 figure in retirement is not explained and appears to exclude partner's contribution or other household costs. Report states affordability is supported by assets being 'sufficient to make up income shortfall throughout lifetime' but the Fact Find shows current monthly deficit of -£2,036.23 with no current pension income yet being drawn. Report assumes £750 monthly mortgage payment for 7 years but provides no detail on how this interacts with the stated £2,000 retirement expenditure or whether retirement begins before the mortgage ends. 24 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial commitment of £67,945.44 (transfer to Aviva Pension) and an ongoing commitment of £0 (Nil regular contributions). The report states affordability is supported by: (1) 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' and (2) 'your assets are sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime'. The report's cashflow assumptions state: earnings of £45,000 per annum until age 60, personal expenditure of £2,237 per month until retirement, £2,500 sofa purchase, £750 monthly mortgage for 7 years, £350,000 inheritance next year, retirement personal expenditure of £2,000 net per month, and full State Pension from age 67. Current financial position: net assets £377,000 (excluding inheritance). The report indicates the client has liquid cash available for instance access needs.
    File:     The Fact Find records: total net monthly income of £800.00 (partner's contribution), total monthly household expenditure of £2,836.23, resulting in a monthly disposable income of -£2,036.23 (a deficit). The expenditure breakdown shows Council Tax £221, Electricity £120, Water £42, Telephone/Mobile £20, Food & Personal Care £300, Car PCP £440, Car travelling £50, Car tax £1,000 annually, Car fuel £200, plus additional household items. No change in expenditure is expected. Current monthly cash flow shows the household cannot meet current expenditure from current income. The Aviva Pension Terms & Conditions permit flexible contributions and drawdown, with cash account interest at 3.05% AER and charges deducted from available cash. The client agreement confirms initial advice fee of 3% = £9,838.36 (or £9,839.36 stated as alternative). The Aviva pension illustration states ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% per annum, estimated at £265.09 per month (later shown as variable). Platform charges total 0.39% annually on the Aviva plan. The inheritance of £350,000 is noted as 'pending legal proceedings' and expected within one year.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report assumes retirement expenditure of £2,000 net per month (personal only); Fact Find records current household expenditure of £2,836.23 monthly with no stated reduction. The basis for the £2,000 figure in retirement is not explained and appears to exclude partner's contribution or other household costs.
      - Report states affordability is supported by assets being 'sufficient to make up income shortfall throughout lifetime' but the Fact Find shows current monthly deficit of -£2,036.23 with no current pension income yet being drawn.
      - Report assumes £750 monthly mortgage payment for 7 years but provides no detail on how this interacts with the stated £2,000 retirement expenditure or whether retirement begins before the mortgage ends.
      - Report's cashflow model incorporates inheritance of £350,000 'next year' but inheritance is documented as 'pending legal proceedings' — certainty and timing are not established in supporting evidence.
      - Report asserts affordability without quantifying or addressing the current deficit of £2,036.23 per month on income of £800 against expenditure of £2,836.23.
      - Ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% per annum (£265.09 per month estimated) is mentioned in the Aviva illustration but not discussed in the suitability report's affordability assessment as an ongoing commitment against retirement income.
      - Report states client has 'liquid cash should you require instance access funds' but no current liquid cash position is documented in the Fact Find or financial position section.
      - Discrepancy in stated fees: initial advice fee quoted as both £9,838.36 in suitability report and £9,839.36 in client agreement — minor but inconsistent.
      - Report's assumption of 6.00% annual investment growth for pension funds is stated but no basis or justification for this rate is provided against historical performance or fund objectives.
    The report asserts affordability on two grounds: (1) retirement assets will be sufficient, and (2) current deficit can be covered by inheritance. However, the comparison reveals material gaps and inconsistencies. The Fact Find establishes a current household deficit of £2,036.23 per month, which the report does not address or reconcile. The report's cashflow model assumes a retirement expenditure of £2,000 net per month (personal), but the Fact Find records current household expenditure of £2,836.23 with no indication of how or why this would fall to £2,000. The £2,000 figure may exclude the partner's current contribution of £800/month, but this is not clarified. The report states the inheritance (£350,000) will support the cashflow, but the Fact Find documents it as 'pending legal proceedings' — material uncertainty about both timing and receipt. The initial commitment of £67,945.44 is a one-off transfer of existing pension funds and is not itself a new affordability burden (guard: existing pension money transferred is not a new commitment). However, the report does not quantify or address the ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% per annum (c. £265/month stated in the Aviva illustration) as an ongoing cost against retirement income. The report asserts liquid cash is available for emergencies but does not quantify this buffer. The report does not reconcile the current income-expenditure position (£800 income vs £2,836 expenditure) with the retirement affordability claim, leaving unclear how the transition from deficit to surplus is achieved. The inheritance is critical to the affordability case but its status as 'pending' introduces material uncertainty that is not addressed. The report's assumption of 6.00% annual investment growth is not justified against fund objectives or historical performance. Overall, the affordability assessment is asserted without detailed reconciliation of: current cashflow to retirement cashflow, the impact of the pending inheritance on timing and certainty, the treatment of ongoing adviser charges, or quantification of emergency reserves. The comparison can be performed because both income and expenditure figures exist in the file, but the report's affordability conclusion relies on assumptions (inheritance receipt, timing, retirement expenditure reduction, investment returns) that are not fully supported or explained in the evidence. The guards confirm that the £67,945.44 is a one-off transfer and not a new commitment, and that fees are deducted from the fund, not the client's income. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers are noted in the evidence that would materially change this assessment, though the dependence on uncertain inheritance for retirement affordability itself represents a form of financial fragility.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's financial position is: total assets £797,000 (including main residence £430,000, cash £17,000, and expected inheritance £350,000); total liabilities £70,000; net worth £727,000. The report states monthly expenditure is £2,237 until retirement and £2,000 net per month in retirement. Current monthly income is stated as £800 (partner's contribution). Annual earnings are £45,000 gross until age 60. The mortgage payment is £750 per month for 7 years. The report projects via cashflow analysis that 'your assets are sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime' in the baseline scenario to age 100. The recommendation is to transfer £67,945.44 to Aviva with investment growth assumed at 6.00% per annum before charges. The report states an emergency fund exists ('you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds') and that capital will be accessible within 10-15 working days. The client's retirement income objective is stated as '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement'. A stress test for high inflation (6%) shows funds depleted at age 81, but the market crash scenario shows funds lasting a lifetime. Life expectancy basis is age 87 per ONS statistics.
    File:     The Fact Find records total assets of £587,000 (not including the inheritance). Total liabilities are stated as £70,000 (mortgage £68,000, credit cards £7,000, personal loan £13,000 = £88,000 in total, which does not reconcile to the £70,000 stated in the report). Current monthly household expenditure is calculated as £2,836.23 (not £2,237 as the report states for personal pre-retirement expenditure). Current net monthly income is £800. The mortgage payment is £600 per month, not £750. The Fact Find does not detail a specific emergency fund or liquid cash reserve beyond the £17,000 in savings. Projected total disposable income is shown as negative (£-2,036.23 monthly). The client's stated retirement income objective in the Fact Find is not explicitly quantified but the report later asserts '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement'. No cashflow modelling output or detailed affordability assessment is attached to the case file. The partner Tony's £600 monthly contribution is noted in the report but the Fact Find shows this as 'Partner's Contribution' of £800 net monthly. The inheritance of £350,000 is noted in the Fact Find as 'pending legal proceedings' but no cash date or certainty is documented.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total household liabilities as £70,000; Fact Find itemises mortgage £68,000 + credit cards £7,000 + personal loan £13,000 = £88,000. Reconciliation is unclear.
      - Report states monthly expenditure as £2,237 until retirement; Fact Find shows 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £2,836.23. Report later states personal expenditure in retirement as £2,000 net per month. Ambiguity whether £2,237 is personal only (excluding Tony's £600 contribution) and basis differs from household total.
      - Report states mortgage payment as £750 per month for 7 years; Fact Find records £600 per month. Difference of £150.
      - Report asserts cash reserves of £17,000 with comfortable liquidity access; Fact Find confirms £17,000 savings but does not explicitly confirm emergency fund status or accessibility timeline.
      - Report references partner Tony's £600 monthly contribution towards household bills; Fact Find shows 'Partner's Contribution' line item as £800 net monthly under income. Unclear whether these are the same figure or different.
      - Report states current net monthly income as £800 (partner contribution only); Fact Find shows Total Net Monthly Income £800 with same source identified. However, the report will move to self-employment at £45,000 gross annually, which projects to approximately £2,925 net monthly (using typical 35% tax/NI), but this is not detailed in the Fact Find income section.
      - Report projects funds lasting to age 100 and age 87 per ONS; Fact Find contains no life expectancy assessment or comparable projection.
      - Report states total assets £797,000 including £350,000 inheritance; Fact Find records £587,000 total assets excluding inheritance. Inheritance treatment differs: report includes as 'expected' within one year; Fact Find notes as 'pending legal proceedings' with no quantified expectation or date.
      - Report asserts retirement income objective of '£25,000 to £30,000'; Fact Find does not quantify this objective explicitly, though report cites this as client's stated goal.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Complete cashflow modelling output or projection schedule showing year-by-year assets, income, expenditure and surplus/deficit from present to age 100 or life expectancy, Reconciliation of £70,000 total liabilities stated in report against itemised liabilities in Fact Find (mortgage £68,000 + credit cards £7,000 + personal loan £13,000 = £88,000), Clarification of whether £2,237 monthly expenditure is personal only (net of Tony's £600 contribution) or whether £2,836.23 household total is the correct input, Confirmation of mortgage payment amount: £750 per month (as stated in report cashflow assumptions) versus £600 per month (as recorded in Fact Find), Emergency fund quantification: explicit statement of liquid reserves available for immediate need, separate from the £17,000 general savings, Confirmation of partner's contribution: whether £600 monthly (stated in report as household expense) or £800 monthly (shown in Fact Find income line), Defined affordability surplus once self-employment income is earned: calculation of net monthly surplus at age 55-60 during employment phase, Detailed assumptions for cashflow stress tests (high inflation at 6%, market crash at 2007/8 magnitude) including impact on planned contribution pattern and withdrawal sequencing, Inheritance contingency: confirmation of timing, legal status, and treatment in model if legal proceedings delay receipt beyond 'one year' timeline stated, Documented confirmation of the client's ability to meet initial sofa purchase (£2,500) immediately from available liquid resources without impairing emergency reserves
    The comparison cannot be fully performed because the essential cashflow modelling output is not provided in the case file. The report makes strong affordability claims ('your assets are sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime') but the spreadsheet, charts, or detailed year-by-year projections that would allow verification are absent. Without these, it is impossible to verify that the stated assumptions (£45,000 earnings, £2,237 or £2,000 monthly expenditure, 6% investment growth, 2% general inflation, 2.5% pension inflation) were correctly modelled or that the conclusion is arithmetically sound. The report states scenarios but no supporting output is on file. Multiple input discrepancies exist between the report's cashflow assumptions section (P6) and the Fact Find (P4, P20, P24). The report's stated monthly expenditure of £2,237 until retirement differs significantly from the Fact Find's calculated household total of £2,836.23 (difference of £599.23 or 21.2%). The report notes that this figure represents personal expenditure and that Tony contributes £600 per month (£2,237 + £600 = £2,837, which aligns closely with the Fact Find total), suggesting the £2,237 may be net of his contribution. However, this basis is never explicitly stated in the cashflow assumptions section, creating ambiguity about which figure was actually modelled. The mortgage payment discrepancy (£750 stated in assumptions section versus £600 recorded in Fact Find) is material and unexplained. If £750 was used in the model but the actual payment is £600, the model overstated expenditure and understated surplus by £150 monthly. Conversely, if the model used £600 but the report states £750, the affordability conclusion rests on an incorrect input. The liabilities reconciliation issue (£70,000 reported versus £88,000 itemised in Fact Find) is significant but may be explained by the report excluding liabilities to be repaid or consolidated, though this distinction is never made explicit. The inheritance treatment introduces material uncertainty: the report includes it in total assets as 'expected within one year' yet the Fact Find records it as 'pending legal proceedings'. If legal proceedings delay receipt beyond the projected timeline or if the inheritance is not received, the affordability projection collapses. The baseline cashflow conclusion depends heavily on this £350,000 inflow occurring on schedule. The report's statement that the client 'has liquid cash should you require instance access funds' and can access capital 'within 10-15 working days' is supported by the Fact Find's £17,000 cash holding, but there is no explicit emergency fund protocol, quantified adequacy level, or confirmation that this reserve remains intact after the initial £2,500 sofa purchase and ongoing investment of £67,945.44. The projected household disposable income in the Fact Find is negative (£-2,036.23 monthly), reflecting current expenditure of £2,836.23 against net income of £800. The report does not explain how this negative cashflow is reconciled with the self-employment income of £45,000 gross (approximately £2,925 net monthly assuming standard deductions), which would create a material surplus of approximately £90 net monthly until age 60. This calculation is absent from the file. No assessment of affordability of regular contributions to the new Aviva plan is provided. The report recommends the transfer but does not address whether the client can make additional contributions or whether the recommendation assumes contributions only from the transferred capital. The stress test showing funds depleted at age 81 under 6% inflation is concerning in light of the client's stated life expectancy of age 87 (ONS basis) and willingness to project to age 100, yet the report concludes this scenario is acceptable without explaining the plan to address a 6-year shortfall. The baseline scenario is described as reaching age 100, but no asset value at that point is stated, nor is there confirmation that the assets remain positive throughout retirement or whether they reach zero before age 100 (the projection claims they last the lifetime, which could mean zero at age 100 or positive reserves). Overall, the financial resilience conclusion rests on inputs not fully evidenced in the file, assumptions not reconciled between documents, and modelling output not provided for independent verification.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the client holds an emergency fund of £17,000 as at 2026-03-24. It asserts this is confirmed sufficient, with the statement 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report notes that pension fund access will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete. No quantified required amount for the emergency fund is stated in the extracted model.
    File:     The Fact Find records: Amount put aside for Emergency Fund £7,000.00; Amount of Emergency Fund Required £24,000.00; Emergency Fund Shortfall £17,000.00. Budget notes state 'She is also wanting to withdraw a tax free cash lump sum of £40,000. £20,000 to replace emergency funds and also to cover some legal fees for the ongoing issue she has regarding the inheritance.' Further notes: 'Currently living off savings which is now reduced to £7,000 and this also acts as an emergency pot. She prefers to have 12 months of expenditure as an emergency reserve so close to £24,000. The £7,000 will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up and this is making her feel uncomfortable.' The Fact Find also notes planned expenditure of £2,800 overall, with personal expenditure £2,237 per month, and planned retirement expenditure £2,000 net per month.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts emergency fund held is £17,000; Fact Find records emergency fund held as £7,000 with a shortfall of £17,000 (meaning required amount £24,000).
      - Report states sufficiency is confirmed; Fact Find indicates the client 'feels uncomfortable' with current reserves and intends to use £20,000 of a planned lump sum withdrawal specifically to 'replace emergency funds'.
      - Report does not state a required emergency fund amount; Fact Find quantifies required amount as £24,000 (12 months expenditure at ~£2,000/month).
      - Fact Find notes current cash 'will likely be down to circa £4,000 by the time the pension income is set up'; report asserts £17,000 held without caveat.
      - Report's assertion of client comfort with access timing (10-15 working days) is not tested against stated emergency fund insufficiency in Fact Find.
    Critical discrepancy on the base figure: the report's £17,000 emergency fund figure appears to derive from the Fact Find's stated 'Emergency Fund Shortfall' rather than the actual amount held (£7,000). The Fact Find shows: current emergency fund £7,000, required amount £24,000, shortfall £17,000. The report has inverted the meaning, treating the shortfall as the held amount. The Fact Find is explicit: the client currently holds £7,000 and prefers £24,000 (12 months' expenditure). The Budget Notes confirm the client's discomfort and plan to withdraw £20,000 from pension tax-free cash specifically to 'replace emergency funds', indicating active awareness of insufficiency. The notes further warn that the £7,000 'will likely be down to circa £4,000' before pension income commences. The report's claim that sufficiency is 'confirmed' and the client is 'comfortable' directly contradicts the Fact Find's record that the client 'is making her feel uncomfortable'. The report's assertion of adequate liquid cash (sufficient for the 10-15 day access window) cannot be supported when the Fact Find documents that current holdings are inadequate, projected to fall further, and scheduled for partial drawdown to address liabilities rather than reinforce reserves. No evidence shows that the £17,000 asserted in the report corresponds to any actual held sum; it matches only the Fact Find's calculated shortfall. The required emergency reserve (£24,000) is never addressed in the suitability report as a benchmark against which to measure affordability or resilience. The recommendation to transfer pension funds and draw £40,000 tax-free cash is asserted as addressing liabilities and 'creating more disposable income', but the allocation of that lump sum (£20,000 to emergency reserves, £20,000 to liabilities) means post-action reserves would reach only ~£27,000 before further depletion. The suitability report does not quantify or discuss this trajectory or whether the resulting emergency fund position supports the stated recommendation.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Initial commitment: £67,945.44 (transfer to Aviva Pension). Ongoing commitment: £0 (Nil regular contributions). The report asserts affordability is supported by: (1) 'Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' [P7]; (2) 'As you can see your assets are sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime' [P7]; (3) Client has capacity for moderate loss and option to work beyond age 60 or part-time if necessary [P7]. Cashflow model assumptions: earnings £45,000 annually until age 60; expenditure £2,237 per month until retirement and £2,000 net per month in retirement; inheritance £350,000 next year; partner contribution £800 monthly; State Pension from age 67; investment growth 6.00% before charges [P11].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states monthly income from employment £45,000 annually but states net income basis as only £800 monthly (partner contribution) in current position; meeting notes show Melanie earning £42,000 annual salary (~£2,600 net monthly) at time of advice
      - Report cashflow assumes Melanie working until age 60 earning £45,000, but meeting summary indicates she has 'recently departed from employment' and is in 'transitional phase' [P24]
      - Report inheritance figure not specified in suitability report assertions but meeting notes show £150,000 to £1.4 million range with 'uncertain timeline' due to legal proceedings; report model assumes £350,000 'next year' without qualifying uncertainty
      - Report states personal expenditure in retirement £2,000 net monthly but household expenditure before retirement £2,237 or £2,836.23 or £2,986.23 depending on section; basis (gross vs net, individual vs household) inconsistent
      - Report asserts affordability without quantifying monthly surplus or deficit during accumulation phase (pre-retirement) when inheritance timing and amount are uncertain

    The report asserts affordability with two high-level statements focused on retirement adequacy ('funds along with inheritance will be sufficient') and asset sufficiency ('assets are sufficient to make up income shortfall throughout lifetime'). However, the comparison cannot be completed reliably because of material ambiguities in the underlying figures. The extraction metadata flags three candidate readings for expenditure (£2,237 personal net, £2,986.23 household gross, or £2,836.23 calculated total) and two readings for retirement income objective (personal or household, gross or net). Fact Find [P2] records current monthly cash position as -£2,036.23 deficit when household expenditure £2,836.23 is set against stated income £800.00 (partner contribution only). Meeting Summary [P18] provides context: Melanie earned £42,000 annually (net ~£2,600 monthly) from employment but has 'recently departed'; she is in 'transitional phase' considering pension access and planning to 'return to work potentially in January' [P24]. This means the report's core assumption—income £45,000 annually until age 60—does not reflect the client's actual current position. The report assumes this employment income will continue 7 years to retirement, yet meeting notes contemporaneous with advice show employment had ended. If the £45,000 income is not present, the cashflow model's core driver is absent. The inheritance is modelled at £350,000 'next year' in the report's assumptions [P11] but meeting notes indicate £150,000 to £1.4 million share with 'timeline uncertain' due to 'ongoing legal proceedings' [P18]. This is a material range and the timing is not 'next year' but 'uncertain'. Neither the range nor the timing uncertainty is disclosed in the report's affordability assertion. The initial commitment £67,945.44 is a transfer of existing pensions, not a new contribution, so the affordability test is whether ongoing income and assets can sustain this consolidation plus any subsequent drawdown and whether liquid reserves exist for contingencies. The report does not quantify: (1) monthly surplus available during accumulation to retirement (age 55 to 60) when inheritance may not yet have been received; (2) liquid emergency reserves outside pension; (3) access to funds if circumstances change. The guard 'Employment income due to cease within the term should be excluded from the surplus used for a long-term commitment' applies here: if the £45,000 income has already ceased, it cannot support affordability through age 60. The recommendation to transfer £67,945.44 consolidating three pensions and switch to Aviva platforms locks capital into pension until age 55 (currently met, minimum age 55 applies) with flexible drawdown available. Aviva KFD [P15] confirms flexi-access drawdown allows one-off lump sums, series of single payments, or regular basis payments, so access is not restricted. However, the affordability of the transfer and ongoing charges (0.39% platform + 1.00% ongoing advice = 1.39% estimated £1,278.99 + £3,180 annually [P7]) cannot be verified against a reliable surplus figure. The report states 'capacity for moderate loss' and option to 'work beyond 60 or part-time if necessary' [P7], acknowledging contingency, but this is not a substitute for demonstrating current affordability on stated income and expenditure. Vulnerability overlay: No drivers of vulnerability, support needs, communication requirements or reasonable adjustments are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that emergency funds are adequately addressed and liquid capital is retained. Specifically: (1) The report states 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' [P5], indicating the client has liquid reserves; (2) The cashflow analysis shows assets are 'sufficient to make up your income shortfall throughout your lifetime' [P7], with a specific note that 'Where income is insufficient to cover your expenditure, the modeller assumes that you will draw from your liquid assets' and 'Once your savings are depleted, the purple shading shows pension funds being accessed again' [P7]; (3) The report confirms 'your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market' [P2]; (4) The recommendation is a Personal Pension with Flexi Access Drawdown, which provides access to funds from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028) [P5]; (5) The Aviva Platform permits access within 10-15 working days [P5]. The report's liquidity position statement is that liquid assets exist and are adequate; the emergency fund is not quantified but is implicitly asserted as sufficient through the affordability conclusion.
    File:     The evidence contains the following information on liquidity and emergency funds: (1) Fact Find shows 'Total Assets (including all Savings and Investments): £587,000.00' [P1] but provides no breakdown of liquid vs illiquid assets or designation of emergency fund amounts; (2) The Fact Find does not explicitly record any cash holdings or emergency fund amount in the 'Existing Bank Accounts / Existing Cash' section [P10], which shows 'Do you have existing Cash products?: [blank]' with no values entered; (3) Suitability report states 'Current Total Assets' chart shows 'blue being pension funds and green is the inheritance' [P7], but does not quantify non-pension liquid reserves separately; (4) The report references an expected £350,000 inheritance 'currently pending legal proceedings' expected 'within one year' [P2], but the current asset position does not clarify how much of the £587,000 (or the stated £377,000 net assets after liabilities) consists of accessible cash vs illiquid pension funds; (5) Aviva Pension T&Cs confirm a Cash Account exists with interest at 3.05% AER and funds are held in client money bank accounts [P15], but this is prospective (post-transfer) provision, not current emergency liquidity; (6) The transfer value being moved to Aviva is £67,945.44, leaving other assets untouched, but the current split between liquid savings and pension funds in the existing arrangements is not clearly stated in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts 'you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' [P5] but the Fact Find shows the Existing Bank Accounts section with no disclosed cash balances [P10], creating ambiguity about the quantum and location of this liquid reserve.
      - The report's total asset figure of £587,000 in the Fact Find [P1] differs from the Suitability Report's reference to £377,000 net assets [implied by assets £447,000 minus liabilities £70,000], and the relationship between these figures and the expected £350,000 inheritance is unclear from the evidence.
      - The report references 'savings being used to make up the shortfall' in the cashflow chart [P7] but does not quantify how much savings exist outside the pension funds being transferred or state an emergency fund target amount.
      - The Fact Find 'Total Assets' of £587,000 includes all investments but does not distinguish which portion is held as emergency liquidity, which is in pensions, and which may be the pending inheritance; the suitability report does not clarify this breakdown for assessing emergency fund adequacy.
    The requirement tests whether emergency funds are maintained at an adequate level, are not depleted by the recommendation, and remain accessible when needed. The comparison can be performed because both the report and the evidence address liquidity and cashflow. The core issue is quantification and clarity rather than a fundamental contradiction. The report asserts that the client 'has liquid cash' and will retain it, but the evidence does not quantify this amount. The Fact Find records total assets of £587,000 but shows no disclosed cash account balances [P10]. The suitability report references a cashflow that 'assumes you will draw from your liquid assets' before drawing pension income [P7], which indicates emergency funds are part of the modelled affordability, but their current size is not stated. The recommendation itself (transfer of £67,945.44 to Aviva Personal Pension) does not deplete liquid cash—it is a transfer of existing pension benefits, not a drawing of savings. The Aviva Platform will provide a Cash Account [P15] for post-transfer liquidity management. The report's stress tests show the plan survives a 2007/8-style market crash and 6% inflation [P3], which addresses resilience but does not address emergency fund quantum. The ambiguity in the extraction report about whether retirement expenditure is £2,986 gross or £2,000 net [noted in expectedButAbsent] affects the cashflow but does not directly determine whether an emergency fund exists or is adequate. The report does not state an emergency fund target, does not quantify existing liquid reserves outside pensions, and relies on an implicit assertion that sufficient liquid assets exist. The evidence base does not contradict this—it simply does not provide the quantification needed to verify it. Given that pension transfers do not inherently deplete emergency funds, and the report's access to the recommended product is via Flexi Access from age 55, the recommendation itself does not create a liquidity trap. However, the report's affordability assertion rests partly on the assumption that liquid reserves will top up shortfalls; without quantifying these reserves, the assertion is not fully supported. A vulnerability overlay check is not indicated in the evidence (no support needs, communication requirements or vulnerability drivers are recorded in the passages).

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a Personal Pension (SIPP) with a selected retirement age of 60. The report states the client plans to start taking income from the pension of £1,900 per month, and to withdraw a tax-free lump sum of £40,000 (£20,000 for emergency funds and legal fees related to inheritance, £20,000 to pay off liabilities). The report discusses retirement income objective as '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' and states personal expenditure in retirement as £2,000 net per month. The report notes the client currently has £7,000 emergency funds against a required £24,000, leaving a £17,000 shortfall. The recommendation is structured to allow the client to take initial capital (tax-free lump sum and liabilities clearance) and then ongoing income via drawdown.
    File:     The Fact Find records current emergency funds of £7,000 against a required amount of £24,000, creating a £17,000 shortfall [P21]. Monthly liabilities total £1,083 (mortgage £600, credit card £175, personal loan £308) [P17]. Overall household expenses are stated as approximately £2,800, with personal expenditure around £2,000-£2,237 net per month once partner's £800 contribution to bills is accounted for [P21]. The client has stopped working and seeks to replace employment income with £1,900 per month from the pension, supplemented by partner's £800 contribution to bills [P21]. The client prefers 12 months of expenditure (circa £24,000) as emergency reserve [P21]. The Aviva Pension Terms permit access from age 55 currently (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028) [P15, P19], allowing the client's chosen retirement age of 60 to be implemented. Income drawdown permits regular payments, lump sums, or phased drawdown [P15]. The client plans to use pension to clear £33,000 in unsecured liabilities (credit card £7,000 and personal loan £13,000) [P17, P21].
    Discrepancies:
      -  Report asserts retirement income objective of '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' but Fact Find budget notes describe personal expenditure of £2,000 net per month (£24,000 annually), not £25,000-£30,000. Unclear whether the stated retirement income is personal gross, household income including partner contribution, or net.
      - Report states personal expenditure in retirement as £2,000 net per month, but separately records overall household expenses as £2,800 before accounting for partner's £800 contribution, creating ambiguity about which figure represents the basis for affordability.
      - Report plans to withdraw £40,000 tax-free lump sum comprising £20,000 for emergency funds and legal fees and £20,000 to pay off liabilities, but the total liabilities recorded are £88,000 (mortgage £68,000, credit card £7,000, personal loan £13,000), with only the unsecured portion (£20,000) being addressed by the recommendation. Mortgage remains outstanding for 14 years until age 65 at £600 monthly.
      - Client's emergency fund shortfall is £17,000 (current £7,000 vs required £24,000). The recommendation proposes £20,000 from pension as emergency fund replacement, but this does not clearly restore the fund to the required £24,000 if any portion is depleted before pension drawdown stabilises.
      - Report discusses inheritance of £350,000 pending legal proceedings, expected within one year, which materially affects financial resilience post-recommendation, but this expectation is not integrated into the baseline affordability assessment or emergency fund adequacy assessment.
    The recommended wrapper (Personal Pension / SIPP) permits access from age 55 (currently) and the client's chosen retirement age is 60, which is above the minimum pension age, so no liquidity mismatch from access restrictions applies [P15, P19]. Income drawdown via Aviva Pension Portfolio is flexible, permitting regular payments, one-off lump sums, or phased drawdown [P15], which aligns with the client's stated need to take £40,000 initial tax-free cash and then £1,900 monthly income. However, three material inconsistencies affect the affordability assessment. First, the report's retirement income target of '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' is not clearly reconciled with the stated personal expenditure of £2,000 net per month (£24,000 annually) or the household baseline of £2,800 before partner contribution. This ambiguity means the affordability case cannot be fully verified: if the target is household income including partner's £800 contribution, the required pension income would be £25,000 - £9,600 = £15,400 annually (£1,283 monthly), not the £1,900 stated; if it is personal net, the discrepancy to £25,000-£30,000 is unexplained. Second, the emergency fund issue: the client requires £24,000 but currently holds £7,000, a shortfall of £17,000 [P21]. The recommendation allocates £20,000 from the tax-free lump sum to 'replace emergency funds', but the Fact Find notes indicate the fund will likely be down to £4,000 by implementation date [P21], meaning the £20,000 would barely restore the fund to target. No assessment is provided of whether £24,000 is adequate once income drawdown begins, given the 12-month coverage ratio the client expressed and the fact that income from pension (£1,900) plus partner contribution (£800) equals £2,700 against baseline expenditure, leaving only £437 monthly surplus before considering liabilities (mortgage £600, card £175, loan £308 = £1,083, which exceeds the stated personal expenditure). Third, the liabilities position: the client carries £88,000 in total liabilities (mortgage £68,000, card £7,000, loan £13,000) [P17]. The recommendation clears only the unsecured debt (£20,000 from the lump sum), leaving the mortgage at £600 monthly for 14 years until age 65, meaning the client will still have a £68,000 liability when she reaches age 60. This is material to cashflow: if personal expenditure is £2,000 and mortgage continues at £600, the true baseline is £2,600, not £2,000, and the surplus from pension income (£1,900) and partner (£800) becomes £100 monthly before any discretionary spending. The inheritance expectation (£350,000, pending legal proceedings, expected within one year) is discussed in objectives but not integrated into the affordability baseline, creating ambiguity about whether the current emergency fund target and liquidity assessment remain valid if the inheritance arrives as expected. The report does not clearly separate pre-inheritance and post-inheritance scenarios. No evidence is presented that the client's support needs, communication requirements or vulnerability characteristics (if any) have been considered in relation to the complexity of managing ongoing drawdown income and the risk of insufficient cashflow if income expectations (partner's £800 contribution, own employment income of £20,000 from 2027) fail to materialise.

  [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three income sources: (1) Self-Employment income of £45,000 gross per annum as at 2026-03-24, with the quote 'You expect annual earnings to be around £45,000 gross going forward.' (2) State Pension of £230.21 per week (unspecified basis, treated as weekly) commencing at age 67, with the quote 'You expect to receive the Full State Pension of £230.21 per week from age 67.' The report provides an asAt date of 2026-03-24 for this assertion. (3) State Pension commencement date of 2034-03-24 (age 67, calculated from birth year 1967). (4) An 'Other' income category is listed but no figure or description is provided in the canonical extraction.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] records State Pension Retirement Age as 67 with a note 'expecting full state pension', but provides no weekly or annual figure. The Fact Find [P2] shows Total Net Monthly Income of £800.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £2,836.23. The Budget Notes in [P9] state 'she would like to replace with income from the pension of £1,900 per month' and 'With the additional partner contribution of £800 towards bills' (conflicting with the £600 stated in [P14]). The Suitability Report [P12] itself asserts 'You expect to receive the Full State Pension of £230.21 per week from age 67' but does not provide independent corroboration of this figure from a State Pension forecast or DWP documentation. No passage provides a State Pension forecast or official statement of entitlement. [P13] records an action item: 'You will send the state pension forecast screenshot', indicating a forecast was expected but not yet provided at the time this summary was recorded.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts gross self-employment income of £45,000 per annum; [P14] documents £42,000 gross annual salary as an accountant. Difference of £3,000 or 6.7%.
      - The report lists 'Other' income category with no figure; the evidence shows Tony's contribution of £600 per month (£7,200 per annum per [P14]) or £800 per month (£9,600 per annum per [P9] Budget Notes), creating internal inconsistency in the evidence itself regarding what 'Other' income represents.
      - The report provides £230.21 per week for State Pension; no independent State Pension forecast, DWP statement, or BR19 projection appears in the evidence to corroborate this specific figure. [P13] indicates a State Pension forecast screenshot was promised but not yet attached to the file at the time of the meeting summary.
      - The State Pension commencement date in the report is stated as 2034-03-24 (at age 67). [P1] confirms State Pension Retirement Age of 67, but no independent age verification document is present to confirm the client's date of birth or that 2034 is the correct year when age 67 is reached.
    The report asserts State Pension of £230.21 per week starting age 67 in 2034. The weekly figure of £230.21 is internally consistent within the report (stated in both the canonical model extraction and the Suitability Report [P12]). However, the comparison with evidence reveals three material gaps: (1) No State Pension forecast is on file. [P13] records that the client promised to send a 'state pension forecast screenshot', indicating this critical corroborating document was not yet available when the suitability report was prepared or at the time of the last meeting summary. The absence of an official forecast means the £230.21 figure is unsupported by the primary evidence source. (2) The self-employment income of £45,000 stated in the report contradicts the £42,000 gross annual salary documented in [P14] Meeting Summary with Jack. This is a material discrepancy of £3,000 (6.7%). The report's basis for the £45,000 figure is not explained and may reflect outdated information or a different employment arrangement not documented in the retrieved passages. (3) The report extracts an 'Other' income category but assigns no figure. The evidence shows Tony contributes £600/month per [P14] or £800/month per [P9], creating internal inconsistency in the file. If 'Other' refers to Tony's contribution, the report should have stated the amount; if not, the category should be explained. (4) State Pension age of 67 is confirmed in [P1]. The commencement year 2034 is consistent with age 67 if the client was born in 1967, but no birth date is visible in the evidence to confirm this independently. The guard on State Pension forecasts notes that a forecast may quote a weekly figure that must be annualised at 52 for annual comparison, but this guard does not excuse the absence of the forecast itself. The affordability of the recommendation depends on the State Pension commencing as projected; without a forecast on file, this assertion is unsupported. The £45,000 income figure used in cashflow and affordability may be materially overstated if the true income is £42,000, affecting the overall suitability assessment.

      - Report retirement income objective of £25,000-£30,000 unclear as to basis (gross/net, household/personal); evidence (P5) discusses £1,900/month pension income plus £20,000 part-time work income = £42,800 gross, but personal net after Tony's contribution unclear
      - Evidence (P2, P5) indicates inheritance complicated by legal proceedings and may require £20,000 legal contribution; report does not note this contingency or impact on £350,000 assertion
    The requirement tests whether affordability is fully supported and consistent. Multiple material discrepancies emerge. First, the inheritance amount: the report asserts £350,000 on a specific date (2027-03-24), but evidence suggests £150,000 is more realistic given Melanie is entitled to a quarter share of a property valued at £1.4-£1.6M. P5 explicitly notes legal complications requiring potential £20,000 contribution, and P1 states 'at least £150,000' while noting timeline remains uncertain. The report's £350,000 figure is not clearly supported and carries contingent liability. Second, the retirement date and income trajectory: the report models full employment cessation at age 60 (2031-03-24) with -£45,000 income impact, but evidence (P5) indicates Melanie intends to return to part-time work in January 2027 at ~£20,000/year and plans to remain employed until retirement. This materially changes the cashflow profile: instead of a cliff-edge income loss in 2031, there is partial continuation of employment income. Third, cessation of employee benefits: the report records £0 financial impact from loss of pension contributions in 2026-03-24, but evidence (P2) states Melanie 'has stopped working' and 'lost employment income', and P18 notes she 'will no longer be entitled to employee benefits such as pension contributions'. The report does not quantify the impact of the cessation of employer pension contributions that are currently embedded in the monthly budget. Fourth, emergency fund: the fact find (P2) documents an emergency fund of £7,000 against a required £24,000, creating a £17,000 shortfall. While P5 discusses a proposed £40,000 tax-free lump sum withdrawal (£20,000 for emergency funds and legal fees, £20,000 for liabilities), the suitability report does not formally address this withdrawal, does not confirm it is being recommended, and does not show how it is funded or sequenced with the pension consolidation. Fifth, mortgage overpayment: evidence inconsistently describes overpayment as £250/month (P1, P8) and £750/month (P6). The report's mortgage repayment date of 2033-03-24 (7 years) depends on which figure is correct; with £250 overpayment the timeline extends; with £750 it tightens. Sixth, expenditure basis: the report states £2,986 monthly; the fact find shows £2,836.23 calculated (£150 difference). The ambiguity note identifies uncertainty whether the report's later statement of £2,000 personal net in retirement is net of State Pension or a different basis. This inconsistency undermines the affordability assessment. Seventh, retirement income objective: stated as £25,000-£30,000 but the basis (household gross, personal net, with/without Tony's contribution) is ambiguous and not clarified against evidence. P19 notes that 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement even following a fall in the market', but this statement relies on the £350,000 inheritance figure which is not firmly supported. The evidence does support that Melanie has committed to returning to part-time work and has expressed flexibility to work beyond 60 if needed, which strengthens capacity; however, the report's treatment of future needs as occurring in discrete annual jumps (2026 contribution cessation, 2031 retirement, 2033 mortgage clearance, 2034 State Pension) does not capture the overlapping income from part-time work or the contingency of inheritance legal proceedings. The access timeframe of 10-15 working days for pension withdrawals is stated but not related to any specific future need or liquidity event. The report does not address whether the recommended funds can be accessed on this schedule if urgent need arises. The extraction's internal inconsistency note flags that retirement expenditure is stated as £2,000 net per month but current is £2,986, creating ambiguity about what net means and whether this £2,000 is achievable or modelled. Overall, the affordability assertion is supported in general principle—there is income from multiple sources and some flexibility—but it is not fully supported in detail. The planned needs are stated but not all are quantified or evidenced, the inheritance is overstated, the employment trajectory is not aligned with evidence, and the emergency fund gap is acknowledged but not addressed in the recommendations. The report's capacity for loss statement (P19) that 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient' depends critically on an unsupported £350,000 inheritance figure.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). Report asserts Aviva platform charge is 0.19% vs Quilter 0.27%; these figures are not corroborated in any retrieved passage, and the report itself contains internal inconsistency flagged in expectedButAbsent (EA2 existing annual charge of 0.98% includes advice charge 0.50% and fund charge 0.58%, not platform charge alone at 0.40%). Report claims total charges of 0.20% annually across combined funds; this figure is unsupported by evidence. Report rationale 'wider access to investment options compared to Nest (which offers only 6 funds)' is stated but no evidence of Nest fund options or a comparison document is retrieved. 5 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five provider selection rationales: (1) Lower costs vs Quilter (0.19% platform charges vs 0.27%, saving £208 per annum); (2) Active monitoring capability; (3) Greater investment choice and wider range of funds; (4) Online tools for performance tracking; (5) Flexible retirement options. For True Potential switch, it claims: platform charges 0.21% vs 0.40% (saving 0.19% or £88.68); fund charges 0.58% vs 0.20% (saving £177.35). Investment strategy rationale includes: risk alignment with profile 7, diversification across asset classes, blend of active/passive management, simplicity, competitive charges, peer performance, governance via CIP quarterly review, and multi-asset fund design. Five alternatives considered: (1) leave scheme untouched, (2) switch funds within existing scheme, (3) switch to another existing pension, (4) switch to employer scheme, (5) set up new plan. Report states these were evaluated and Option 5 (new Aviva plan) selected. Aviva financial strength: A (Stable) by S&P, £407bn AUM.
    File:     Evidence confirms Aviva platform offers 6,900+ funds [P7], is FTSE 100-listed Aviva plc [P7], rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM [P7]. Existing arrangements table [P10] shows True Potential platform 0.40% + 0.50% advice + 0.58% fund charges (total 1.48%); Nest risk level 8. Performance data [P2, P4, P6] shows recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) tracked closely over 12 months to Feb 2026. Death benefit comparison [P5] shows Aviva provides 97% of existing plans' death benefits (£65,907 vs £67,945). Retirement projections [P9] show lump sum at age 87 higher under Aviva (£36,600 vs £33,600 existing). Aviva Terms & Conditions [P8, P12, P14, P16] confirm online access, flexible withdrawals, model portfolio options. True Potential fund performance data [P22, P23] shows Allianz Balanced fund returned 17.7% (1 year), 39.4% (3 year), 38.8% (5 year). Nest Higher Risk fund composition detailed [P20, P21] showing diversified holdings across equities, bonds, alternatives. No document retrieved showing the five alternatives were actually researched, compared or documented with reasons for rejection.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Quilter platform charges 0.27%; comparison document does not corroborate this figure for Quilter
      - Report claims saving of £208 per annum on Quilter switch based on 'estimated fund value' but no Quilter fund value or basis stated in evidence
      - Report states True Potential platform 0.40% but also states total True Potential charges as 0.98% (0.40% platform + 0.50% advice + 0.58% fund); the £88.68 saving on platform only (0.19%) does not account for ongoing 0.50% advice charge which continues under new plan [canonical model extraction notes this discrepancy]
      - Report states fund charges saving of £177.35 (0.58% vs 0.20%) on True Potential funds but no independent verification of Aviva fund charges at 0.20% provided in evidence
      - Report recommends switching from Nest (risk level 8) stating 'current fund is not in line with agreed risk level 7' yet report also states recommended funds have 'risk rating 7' but does not show Nest actually underperformed: Nest Higher Risk fund [P20, P21] shows 44% exposure to UBS Climate Aware Global Developed Equities and diversified alternatives, while recommended portfolio restricted to two funds (HSBC Dynamic, Vanguard 80% Equity)
      - Report asserts five alternatives were considered and evaluated, but no document evidence provided showing comparative analysis, research or reasons for rejection of alternatives (Option 1-4); only assertion that Option 5 was selected
      - Report states charges are 'competitive' [canonical model] without evidence of competitive benchmarking against other providers on platform
      - Report states funds 'have performed well in comparison with their peers' [canonical model] but performance comparison document [P2, P4, P6] shows only weighted average performance of proposed funds, not peer ranking or percentile data
      - Report describes multi-asset fund rationale as 'professionally managed, diversified' but recommended solution is two equally-weighted funds (50% HSBC, 50% Vanguard), not a managed multi-asset fund
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Comparative research documents showing analysis of the five stated alternatives (leave scheme, switch funds within scheme, switch to another pension, switch to employer scheme), Reasons for rejection of each alternative with supporting analysis, Quilter platform charges corroboration and Quilter fund value at time of recommendation, Independent verification of Aviva ongoing fund charges at stated 0.20%, Peer performance benchmarking data for recommended funds to support 'performed well in comparison with their peers' claim, Competitive charges benchmarking against other providers at similar risk profile, Documentation showing how multi-asset strategy claim relates to two-fund portfolio structure, Evidence addressing whether ongoing 0.50% advice charge under Aviva reduces net benefit versus Quilter switch, Basis for 'estimated fund value' used in Quilter savings calculation
    The report contains client-specific language linking recommendations to objectives (OBJ2, OBJ3, OBJ5) and personalised rationales tied to client circumstances. However, critical gaps undermine the suitability justification: (1) Provider comparison is incomplete. The report asserts lower costs for Aviva vs Quilter (0.19% platform) and vs True Potential (0.19% platform + 0.20% fund charges = 0.39% total vs current 0.98%), but these figures rest on bases not established in evidence. Quilter charges 0.27% is stated without corroboration; Aviva fund charges 0.20% are stated without independent verification. The True Potential comparison conflates platform-only (0.40%) with total recurring (0.98% including continuing 0.50% advice), creating ambiguity about whether ongoing advice charges under the new arrangement were factored into the switch rationale. (2) Alternatives analysis is asserted but not evidenced. The report states five options were considered but provides no document showing comparative research, analysis table, rejection reasons, or why each alternative was unsuitable. This is a missing component: the 'consideration of relevant alternatives' specified in the requirement is claimed but not demonstrated. (3) Risk profile justification is inconsistent. The report recommends switching from Nest (risk 8) because 'not in line with agreed risk level' (7), yet the existing Nest Higher Risk fund shows sophisticated diversification (44% climate-aware equities, private credit, infrastructure, commodities, emerging markets [P20, P21]) vs the simpler two-fund recommended solution. No performance comparison between Nest and proposed funds is presented to justify the switch on risk grounds, only a risk-level mismatch. (4) Investment strategy rationale includes generic claims ('diversification', 'professional management', 'competitive charges', 'peer performance') marked in the canonical model as 'isClientSpecific false' or 'Assumed' modality. These provide weak justification without supporting evidence. The multi-asset fund description does not match the two-fund structure (HSBC 50%, Vanguard 50%), suggesting the rationale was drafted generically. (5) The charge savings calculations are presented without disclosure of all ongoing costs. The £88.68 platform saving and £177.35 fund saving are highlighted, but the continuing 0.50% advice charge under Aviva is not subtracted from net benefit in the comparison narrative. Under the guard against absolute marketing claims vs. comparative ones, the report distinguishes itself on 'greater investment choice' (6,900 funds vs Quilter/True Potential limits) and 'active monitoring', which are legitimate provider differentiators if comparative. However, cost claims rely on stated charges not independently verified in the evidence. (6) Vulnerability overlay: No evidence of vulnerability assessment in the file; therefore no material impact on this assessment identified. (7) Death benefit and retirement projection comparisons are provided ([P5, P9]), showing 97% coverage and higher PCLS (£36,600 vs £33,600), which are client-specific justifications, but these do not offset the gaps in alternative analysis and charge transparency.
