  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The canonical model states that 17 recommendations are present in the report. Of these, 10 are flagged as client-specific (true) and 7 are flagged as not client-specific (false). The true cases are at indices: 0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 9, 14, 15, 16. The false cases are at indices: 4, 7, 8, 11, 12, 13. The report asserts rationale for each recommendation linked to client objectives and circumstances.
    File:     The evidence shows Melanie Anness is a post-divorce client managing pension transfers following recent divorce from Paul Annis. She has an accounting background and prior experience in currency trading, earning £42,000 annually at Bridgeport Interiors Limited. Meetings on 16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, 18/03/2026, and 21/07/2026 are documented. The meeting summaries describe her situation as post-divorce financial management and pension transfer administrative processes. Risk profile was assessed as level 7. Investment preferences were not explicitly discussed in early meetings, with the focus on administrative pension transfer aspects. The evidence indicates objectives were identified in the fact find but specific retirement income targets or fund allocation preferences are not fully detailed in the passages provided.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report flags 7 of 17 recommendations as not client-specific (false at indices 4, 7, 8, 11, 12, 13), indicating these lack personalised language or individual circumstances framing. The evidence does not contain the full text of the suitability report recommendations themselves to verify which recommendations are generic versus personalised.
      - The meeting summaries indicate 'Objectives' were marked as 'Not discussed' in early meetings (P7), yet the report asserts recommendations grounded in objectives. This creates a gap between the timing of objective-setting and recommendation delivery.
      - The evidence shows investment preferences were 'not explicitly discussed' early in the process (P6), yet recommendations are stated to be client-specific where they include investment strategy justification.
      - No document in the evidence pack contains the actual suitability report or its recommendations section, only the canonical model extraction and meeting summaries. The comparison cannot directly assess whether the report's rationale text is personalised or generic.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Full text of the suitability report recommendations section, including the rationale narratives for each of the 17 recommendations, is not present in the evidence pack.     The check requires assessment of whether the suitability report contains clear, client-specific explanations of why recommendations meet the client's needs, including consideration of alternatives. The canonical model reports 17 recommendations, of which 10 (58.8%) are flagged as client-specific and 7 (41.2%) are flagged as not client-specific. This internally inconsistent set of flags—where more than one-third of recommendations lack personalisation—itself raises concern about completeness. However, the core comparison cannot be performed because the suitability report text is not in the evidence pack. The canonical model's 'isClientSpecific' array is an extraction result, not evidence that the report meets the requirement. The meeting summaries establish that Melanie's circumstances are post-divorce, accounting-background, risk-profile 7, and focused on pension transfers. Early meetings show objectives were not explicitly discussed (P7) and investment preferences were not discussed (P6). A later meeting (P3, 18/03/2026) references 'presenting recommendations concerning your pension funds' and 'tailor the recommendations to your specific needs', and the 21/07/2026 fact find (P4) is described as 'Adhoc client meeting for TFC and regular income withdrawal'. This suggests objectives and preferences were eventually gathered, but the evidence does not contain the recommendations themselves or their rationales. The extraction report's ambiguities note states that '/needsAnalysis/objectives' contains retirement income of '£25,000 to £30,000 in retirement' but it is unclear whether this is household, personal, gross or net. This ambiguity in the objectives themselves undermines any claim that recommendations are cleanly linked to clear, unambiguous client goals. The guard 'Standard regulatory wording must be present and is not evidence of impersonal advice' does not apply here because no recommendation text is available to assess. Without the report's actual recommendation narratives, it is impossible to determine whether the 7 non-client-specific recommendations (40% of the total) are generic boilerplate or whether personalisation is present across all 10 flagged as specific. A finding that more than one-third of recommendations are marked as not client-specific, combined with missing objective clarity and absence of the recommendations themselves from the evidence, indicates the requirement cannot be verified as met.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report presents five options: Option 1 - Leave existing pension untouched; Option 2 - Leave existing pension in place and switch investment funds; Option 3 - Switch into another existing arrangement; Option 4 - Switch into employer's pension arrangement; Option 5 - Set up new pension plan (RECOMMENDED). For the recommended option, the report states: moving to Aviva platform for three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest). For Quilter retention alternative, rejection reasons cited: (1) Aviva has lower platform charges 0.19% vs Quilter 0.27%, saving £208 per annum; (2) Client expressed no preference to leave funds with Quilter; (3) Adviser can actively monitor and adjust investments. For Nest retention, rejection reasons: (1) Current Nest Higher Risk Fund not in line with agreed risk level of 7; Aviva offers wider range (6 funds vs wider access); (2) Nest is simple auto-enrolment scheme, client wishes sophisticated approach with professional management; (3) Aviva provides more flexible retirement options including flexi-access drawdown. For investment pathways alternative, rejection reasons: (1) Client requires more tailored solution than generic four solutions; (2) Does not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately; (3) Requires regularly reviewed and adjusted solution as part of ongoing advice; (4) Has not performed as well as recommended investment strategy.
    File:     Evidence supports the existence of five options presented in the report [P4]. Performance data [P2][P3] shows: Risk 7 HSBC Global/Vanguard LS 80 (recommended) - 5yr return 45.91%, volatility 8.63; Nest Higher Risk Fund - 5yr return 47.84%, volatility 8.50; True Potential - 5yr return 38.53%, volatility 8.45; benchmark UT Mixed 40-85% - 5yr return 27.43%, volatility 7.48. Report acknowledges [P3]: 'The recommended funds have outperformed your existing True Potential funds and underperformed against the Nest fund. This is to be expected given the level of risk for each plan, with the Nest funds with a higher equity content achieving greater growth.' Death benefit comparison [P23][P24] shows total existing death benefits £67,945 vs Aviva £65,907 (97% of existing). Critical yield analysis [P12][P24] for Nest shows rate of return required from Aviva 1.19% to 1.26% higher than existing scheme across growth scenarios. Fund projections [P14][P24] show inflation-adjusted fund at age 87: existing schemes £121,807 (at 2.94% growth) vs Aviva £106,000 (12.42% reduction). Investment pathways rejection [P22] confirms four reasons stated in report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Nest Higher Risk Fund 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' but evidence shows Nest achieved 47.84% 5-year return vs recommended 45.91%, outperforming the recommendation
      - Report states switching to Aviva enables 'active adviser monitoring and adjustment' as advantage over Quilter but provides no evidence of what monitoring/adjustment capability difference exists between platforms
      - Report rejects Nest on basis of 'wider range of investment options' but provides no quantitative comparison of actual fund options available or investment flexibility differences
      - Report cites £208 annual platform charge saving (0.19% vs 0.27%) but critical yield analysis [P12][P24] shows Aviva requires 1.19%-1.26% higher annual return on Nest funds to match existing scheme at retirement, far exceeding stated charge savings
      - Report projects £121,807 at retirement for existing arrangements but £106,000 for Aviva at 2.94% growth rate (12.42% reduction), yet rationale emphasizes suitability and alignment without addressing this material projected shortfall
      - Report states client 'expressed no preference to leave funds with Quilter' as rejection reason but this is client preference, not a rationale for why Aviva recommendation is suitable for stated objectives
      - Death benefit shortfall of £2,038 (97% of existing) is noted in analysis but not reflected in rationale for recommendation or discussion of whether this matters to client's goals
      -  Report states investment pathways 'has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy' but no specific performance comparison provided for this asset class as alternative
    The report presents alternatives and rejection reasons, meeting the structural requirement. However, material inconsistencies undermine the client-specificity of the rationale. (1) Risk alignment claim: The report rejects Nest because 'current fund is not in line with agreed risk level of 7' and Nest fund is risk 8, yet the report's own performance data [P3] demonstrates Nest achieved 47.84% 5-year return vs recommended fund's 45.91% - outperforming the recommendation by 1.93 percentage points. The report acknowledges this disparity [P3] ('This is to be expected given the level of risk for each plan, with the Nest funds with a higher equity content achieving greater growth') but does not reconcile this acknowledgment with the rejection rationale. If the Nest fund's higher risk and superior performance are 'to be expected,' the rejection on risk misalignment grounds appears internally contradicted unless the client's stated risk tolerance of 7 is a genuine constraint - but this is not explained as binding. (2) Cost comparison gap: Report cites £208 annual platform charge saving (0.27% vs 0.19%) as primary Quilter rejection reason. However, critical yield analysis [P12] shows Aviva requires 0.09% higher annual return on Nest alone to match existing scheme - and 1.19%-1.26% higher return when considering all arrangements under various growth scenarios [P24]. The £208 saving represents only 0.08% annual benefit, far outweighed by the 1.19%-1.26% critical yield gap. This fundamental cost-benefit relationship is not presented to the client or addressed in the rationale. (3) Fund range claim: Report states Nest offers 'only 6 funds' and Aviva offers 'wider range,' but no specific count or comparison of investment options under each platform is provided in the evidence. The claim lacks quantification. (4) Projected fund shortfall: Critical yield analysis [P14] shows existing arrangements project to £121,807 at age 87 (2.94% growth) vs Aviva £106,000 (12.42% reduction). This material 12.42% projected shortfall is not addressed in the recommendation rationale. The report does not explain why accepting this reduction is suitable for stated objectives. (5) Death benefit: Existing total £67,945 vs Aviva £65,907 represents £2,038 shortfall (3% reduction). While disclosed in projection tables, this is not discussed in the rationale as a trade-off consideration. (6) Client preference on Quilter: Reporting that client 'expressed no preference to leave funds with Quilter' is a statement of client preference, not a suitability rationale explaining why Aviva meets objectives. (7) Investment pathway rejection: Report cites underperformance of investment pathways as rejection reason but provides no specific fund name, return data, or time period for the comparison. The evidence [P22] confirms the four rejection reasons stated but does not substantiate the performance claim. (8) Adviser monitoring claim: Report states moving to Aviva enables 'active adviser monitoring and adjustment' vs remaining with Quilter, but no evidence compares actual monitoring or adjustment capabilities between platforms. The claim lacks substantiation. Overall, the report satisfies the structural requirement (alternatives are presented with stated reasons) but fails to deliver client-specific economic justification. The recommendation's cost-benefit analysis is incomplete: charge savings of £208 are offset by critical yield shortfalls of 1.19%-1.26% and projected fund reductions of 12.42%, yet this trade-off is not explained to the client. The risk-alignment rationale conflicts with the report's own acknowledgment that the rejected Nest fund achieved superior returns. The rationale appears generic across all three arrangements rather than tailored to each arrangement's specific case for switching or retaining.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Three existing arrangements are identified: (1) Quilter Pension Sharing Order, advised action FullTransfer, rationale: lower overall cost structure (0.19% platform charges vs 0.27%, saving £208 per annum) and ongoing monitoring capability. (2) True Potential, advised action FullTransfer, rationale: lower platform charges (0.21% vs 0.40%, saving £88.68) and lower fund charges (0.20% vs 0.58%, saving £177.35), plus ongoing monitoring and wider investment options. (3) Nest, advised action FullTransfer, rationale: current fund not in line with agreed risk level (risk 8 vs agreed risk 7), plus wider range of investment options and ongoing monitoring. All three rationales cite 'isClientSpecific: true' and reference client objectives.
    File:     The evidence documents include pension switch analysis reports (Annes M SAP documents) comparing existing and proposed Aviva arrangements. The switch analysis shows: (1) Quilter: transfer value £260,000, existing annual recurring charge derivable from the 0.27% stated in report. (2) True Potential: transfer value £46,672.80, platform charge 0.40% stated in existing arrangements, but later stated as 0.98% (including 0.50% advice charge and 0.58% fund charges). (3) Nest: transfer value £21,272.64, platform charge 0.30%, fund charge not separately itemised in main evidence, Nest Higher Risk Fund listed with risk level 8. The switch analysis projects outcomes at retirement age 87 showing reduction in yield for each arrangement if transferred to Aviva. Death benefit comparisons show proposed Aviva provides 97% of existing plans' death benefits. No alternative funds or providers within existing plans are discussed as considered options.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states True Potential platform charge as 0.40%, but derived figures and switch analysis context show total ongoing charge of 0.98% including advice. The 0.40% appears to be platform-only, not the full charge basis used in recommendation.
      - Report rationale for True Potential cites 'platform charges with Aviva are 0.21% compared to the True Potential platform charges of 0.40%, this represents a saving of 0.19% or £88.68' — but the switch analysis shows True Potential's current annual recurring charge as 0.98%, not 0.40%, creating a material basis mismatch.
      - Report rationale for Nest cites 'current fund is not in line with the agreed risk level' and recommends switch to achieve risk 7 alignment. However, evidence shows Nest Higher Risk Fund has risk level 8, and recommended Aviva funds have weighted average ongoing cost of 0.2154% with risk rating not explicitly stated as 7 in the evidence provided.
      - No evidence of alternative funds within Nest (e.g., Nest Lower Risk Fund) being considered and rejected. Rationale states 'Whilst we could look at the Lower risk fund within this plan' but provides no analysis of why this in-plan alternative was not recommended.
      - No evidence of alternative providers or wrappers being compared. The rationale cites 'wider range of investment options' as a benefit but does not compare Quilter, True Potential, or Nest against other receiving schemes besides Aviva.
      - Report states Quilter rationale includes 'we have negotiated a reduced platform charges of 0.19%, compared to the Quilter charges of 0.27%' — the receiving scheme charges (0.19%) are not verified in the evidence as a negotiated reduction specific to this client.
    Each arrangement carries a stated recommended outcome (FullTransfer) and a named rationale. However, material gaps and inconsistencies weaken the client-specificity and evidence base. (1) Quilter: Rationale cites lower costs (0.27% vs 0.19%) and monitoring. The cost comparison is stated but the 0.19% negotiated rate is not evidenced; the £208 saving derivation rests on transfer value £67,945.44, which sums True Potential and Nest only, not Quilter (£260,000). This appears to be a calculation error applied to the wrong arrangement, or a basis inconsistency. (2) True Potential: The rationale quotes '0.40%' platform charge and '0.58%' fund charges, calculating £88.68 and £177.35 savings. However, the switch analysis shows True Potential's current annual recurring charge as 0.98% including 0.50% ongoing advice. The report's rationale does not clarify that the 0.40% excludes the ongoing advice charge already being paid, creating ambiguity about whether the comparison fairly presents the true cost difference. The savings figures (£88.68, £177.35) derive from the narrower 0.40% and 0.58% basis, not the 0.98% total. (3) Nest: Rationale states current fund 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' (risk 8 vs agreed 7) and recommends switch to access wider options. The evidence confirms Nest fund is risk 8. However, the rationale does not cite analysis of Nest's own Lower Risk Fund (which would have risk 6 per True Potential comparison, closer to agreed 7 than risk 8). The rationale mentions 'Whilst we could look at the Lower risk fund within this plan' as a counterfactual but provides no analysis or reasons why in-plan repositioning was rejected. The 'wider range of investment options' and 'ongoing monitoring' rationales are generic and present in all three existing arrangement rationales, suggesting template language rather than distinct client-specific differentiation. All three cite the same monitoring benefit identically. The evidence does show switch analysis with critical yield calculations for each arrangement, indicating return targets have been assessed, but the rationale does not explain why those projected returns justify the switch despite reduction-in-yield figures (Quilter -14.75%, True Potential -14.75%, Nest -31.35% at 2.94% growth assumption per switch analysis). The recommendation does not explain whether or how the projected outcomes support suitability given these reductions. The vulnerability overlay does not appear relevant here: no special support needs or communication requirements are evidenced in [P24] (Fact Find) that would materially affect the assessment of whether the stated rationales are client-specific.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report makes three recommendations: (1) Consolidate existing pensions into a new Aviva Platform plan to receive the Pension Sharing Order from Quilter and consolidate True Potential and Nest pensions — marked as 'Recommended' with adviser implementing and intended outcome to 'Consolidate pension arrangements into a single platform with lower costs, better investment alignment with agreed risk profile, and improved retirement flexibility'; (2) Invest consolidated funds in 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc and 50% Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity — marked as 'Recommended' with adviser implementing and intended outcome to 'Align pension fund investments with your agreed risk profile of 7 and growth objectives to ensure funds build in preparation for retirement at age 60 with target annual income of £25,000 to £30,000'; (3) Complete nomination of beneficiaries form — marked as 'Recommended' with client responsible and intended outcome to 'Ensure pension death benefits are distributed according to your wishes and maximize tax efficiency of inheritance for beneficiaries'. Implementation timescale stated as '10-15 working days' for the pension transfer process. The report also discloses 'out of market risk' and confirms client comfort with access timescale.
    File:     Meeting summaries from 16 January 2026 confirm discussion of pension transfer requirements and risk profile assessment at level 7. Notes indicate procedural requirements for transferring pension through Quilter using paper-based system, requirement for letter of authority signed by ex-husband Paul Annis, and that Paul's pension funds were 'currently held in cash'. Meeting on 30 March 2026 is referenced for presentation of recommendations via Teams. Aviva Pension Terms & Conditions confirm that changes to income drawdown payments must be requested at least 10 working days in advance, and that the adviser must handle such requests. No evidence in the file specifies whether the consolidation or investment changes are adviser-recommended versus client-directed, nor does the file evidence who specifically will execute each step beyond generic adviser involvement. The Fact Find notes state 'No change to underlying portfolio' for Nest steps with tax-free cash withdrawal, creating potential ambiguity about whether the report's recommendation to move Nest funds contradicts an earlier decision. Aviva T&Cs describe administrative processes but do not corroborate the specific implementation steps, timescale, or responsibility allocation stated in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states consolidation action with adviser implementing; file evidence does not explicitly confirm adviser will implement the Quilter pension sharing order process or consolidation, only that specific paper-based procedures and letter of authority are required
      - Report states investment recommendation as adviser-implemented; file evidence shows risk profile agreed at level 7 but does not confirm adviser will execute the fund selection and allocation
      - Report recommends nomination of beneficiaries form completion with client responsibility; Aviva T&Cs describe death benefit processes but do not evidence that client has been provided the form or confirmation of responsibility allocation
      - Fact Find notes state 'No change to underlying portfolio' for Nest; report recommends moving Nest funds to new Aviva platform with different allocations — unclear whether this contradicts an earlier client instruction or represents a change in advice
      - Report specifies '10-15 working days' for completion; file evidence confirms this is Aviva administrative process timing but does not evidence whether this was communicated to client or confirmed as acceptable
      - Report states adviser will implement nomination form; Aviva T&Cs indicate form 'will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun' but do not confirm adviser responsibility to provide or follow up on completion
    The report clearly states that all three recommendations are 'Recommended' by the adviser, that implementation responsibility falls to the adviser for recommendations 1 and 2, and to the client for recommendation 3. The intended outcomes are specified for each. However, the file evidence does not corroborate whether these statements reflect client instructions or adviser recommendations. The Fact Find reference to 'No change to underlying portfolio' for Nest creates ambiguity: it is unclear whether this was a binding instruction the client gave, or whether it was superseded by the later advice to consolidate. The report's out-of-market risk disclosure and access timescale note are generic or client-specific statements made on behalf of the adviser, but the file evidence does not confirm these were communicated after 30 March 2026 when recommendations were presented. The Aviva T&Cs confirm that changes to income arrangements require adviser action at least 10 working days in advance, and that forms will be provided post-transfer, but this is standard provision, not specific evidence that this dialogue occurred or that the client has agreed to proceed. The report states outcomes clearly but the file does not evidence whether the client actually accepted the recommendations or instructed the changes. This is a silence on consent, not a contradiction of the statement of what is recommended or by whom; the report's claims stand as asserted.

    Report:   The report presents five options for pension consolidation. Option 1: Leave existing pension scheme untouched (not recommended).     Discrepancies:
      - Report presents five pension consolidation options with four marked not recommended and one recommended, but file evidence does not record client discussion of these alternatives or explanation of why Options 1-4 were rejected
      - Report claims lower costs with Aviva at 0.19% versus Quilter 0.27% saving £208 per annum, but extracted data shows internal inconsistency: True Potential platform charge stated as 0.40% in arrangements table versus 0.98% (including advice 0.50% and fund charges 0.58%) in switch assessment, creating ambiguity about baseline charges
      - Report recommends specific fund allocation (50% HSBC / 50% Vanguard) reflecting risk profile 7, but states existing Nest fund (risk 8) is 'not in line with agreed risk level', yet historical performance data shows Nest 45.91% vs recommended funds 47.84% over 5 years, suggesting performance may not support the rationale
      - Report rationale on investment flexibility cites access to wider options than Nest (6 funds), but no evidence in file shows this alternative was presented to or discussed with the client
      - Report cites client preference to leave Quilter as rationale, with quote 'You mentioned during our meeting that you had no ties or preference to leave the funds with Quilter', but P1-P5 meeting summaries do not record this statement or this preference being discussed
      - Meeting evidence P5 explicitly states 'Your investment preferences were not explicitly discussed during the meeting, as the primary focus was on the administrative aspects of your pension transfer', contradicting the premise that client-specific investment strategy was developed through discussion
      - Report provides boilerplate rationale items marked isClientSpecific: false for tax efficiency and diversification, mixed with client-specific rationale, without distinguishing which elements flow from client circumstances versus generic principles
    The report presents a well-structured options framework with five alternatives, clearly designating Option 5 (new Aviva pension plan) as recommended. The presentsUnresolvedOptions field correctly records false, indicating no unresolved options remain. However, the guards require checking whether this resolution is supported by evidence in the file. The file contains meeting summaries from multiple dates (16/01/2026, 20/02/2026, 18/03/2026, and 21/07/2026) but none records explicit discussion of Options 1-4 or reasons they were rejected. P5 explicitly states investment preferences were not discussed in initial meetings due to focus on administration. While the report provides client-specific rationale (client preference to leave Quilter, client knowledge and experience), the key supporting evidence—the client's agreement to the alternatives framework and stated reasons for rejection—is absent from the file. The cost comparison rationale references 0.19% vs 0.27%, but the extracted data reveals internal inconsistency in how True Potential charges are stated (0.40% platform vs 0.98% including advice), creating uncertainty about whether the baseline comparison is accurate. The risk profile alignment rationale (risk 7) is internally supported but the comparison to existing Nest arrangement (risk 8) introduces a discrepancy: the report justifies moving away from risk 8 to risk 7, yet Nest's historical performance (45.91%) is cited as underperforming the recommendation (47.84%), suggesting performance rather than risk profile alignment may be the actual driver. The report's boilerplate elements (tax efficiency, diversification) lack client-specific grounding. Critically, while options are presented and one is marked recommended, the evidence showing the client accepted this framework and agreed with the reasons for rejection is not on file. The meeting summaries focus on administrative matters and fact-find rather than recommendations discussion until the 30th March meeting referenced in P3, but no summary of that recommendations meeting is provided in the evidence pack.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts three recommendations: (1) Consolidate existing arrangements into Aviva Pension, to be arranged by Adviser; (2) NewPlan (unspecified action type), arranged by Adviser; (3) NonProductAction (nomination of beneficiaries), to be completed by Client. Expected timescale for implementation is stated as 'After pension transfer process begins' for individual recommendations, and '10-15 working days' for the overall solution. The report discloses out-of-market risk, access timescale of 10-15 working days (client-specific, with reference to client's liquid cash), and requires completion of nomination of beneficiaries form 'after the transfer process is begun'. The report states the client 'are comfortable with the concept' of the 10-15 working day access timescale.
    File:     Evidence confirms Aviva's T&Cs specify that for lump sum investments, funds are purchased when payment is treated as cleared funds, generally within 3-4 business days depending on payment method [P1]. Aviva's KFD confirms it is a self-invested personal pension (SIPP) and references receipt of 'details of the number of units/shares we buy for you and the price we pay' for lump sum payments [P7]. The meeting summary dated 30th March confirms the presentation of recommendations was scheduled, with action items identified for client to provide pension forecast and locate pension sharing annexe [P2, P3]. No evidence in the file specifies which adviser will execute the consolidation, the exact date the transfer will commence, the process by which the NewPlan recommendation will be implemented, or confirmation of arrangements for the nomination form to be provided.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states expected timescale as '10-15 working days' for overall solution implementation, but individual recommendation timescales are vague ('After pension transfer process begins'), leaving when each discrete action occurs unclear
      - The report recommends 'NewPlan' as an action but does not specify what this plan is, when it will be established, or by whom
      - The report states nomination of beneficiaries form 'will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun' but does not specify who will provide it or confirm a concrete date
      - The report attributes client comfort with 10-15 day access timescale to possession of 'liquid cash', but the file contains no asset breakdown confirming liquid cash holdings or amount available
      - No evidence confirms the named adviser firm or individual responsible for executing the consolidation and new plan recommendations
    The report contains a general timescale (10-15 working days) attributed to Aviva's provider service standard, which satisfies the requirement for a stated timescale under the guard provided [P1, P7]. However, the report fails to clearly delineate action, timing and responsibility for each of the three discrete recommendations made. The consolidation action is attributed to 'Adviser' but no individual adviser name or firm identifier is provided; Aviva's T&Cs confirm the platform can process transfers once cleared funds are received [P1], but the report does not confirm when the transfer instruction will be issued or by whom specifically. The 'NewPlan' recommendation is undefined—no product, provider, wrapper or implementation method is stated. The nomination of beneficiaries is correctly identified as a client responsibility, but the timing is contingent ('after the transfer process is begun'), and the report does not confirm who will send the form or on what date. The client's stated comfort with the 10-15 day access window is predicated on possession of liquid cash, but no evidence in the file itemises the client's cash holdings or confirms this assumption. The extraction metadata flags an ambiguity regarding expenditure and retirement income figures, and separately notes internal inconsistency in charge calculations, but these do not directly bear on the action-timing-responsibility triad for this specific requirement. The meeting summary confirms a recommendations presentation was scheduled for 30 March 2026 [P2], and action items were agreed [P3], but does not confirm execution of those items or detail the implementation instructions for each recommendation.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Report states: (1) Client has a valid Will in place but it needs to be updated [P9]. (2) Client should set up and register Power of Attorney for financial affairs/health and welfare [P9].     File:     File records: (1) Client is actively engaged in updating estate planning following divorce [P1]. (2) Client is in the process of revising will to reflect current circumstances [P1]. (3) Client is appointing two daughters as power of attorneys [P1]. (4) Client is involving a solicitor to ensure legal aspects are properly addressed [P1]. (5) Client is aware of importance of comprehensive plan and is taking proactive steps to safeguard estate [P1]. (6) Certified Final Order [P3] confirms divorce dissolution and automatic revocation of former spouse appointments under Wills Act 1837 and Children Act 1989, but does not record current will or power of attorney status.     Discrepancies:
      - Report states client 'should set up and register Power of Attorney' [P9] but file records client is already 'appointing two daughters as power of attorneys' [P1], indicating Power of Attorney is being established, not absent
      - Report marks beneficiary nomination as 'Client' responsibility with no adviser involvement stated, but does not specify timeline, form provider, or confirmation mechanism beyond 'will be provided after transfer process begins'
      - File indicates will update is in progress via solicitor [P1] but report states will 'needs to be updated' without clarifying whether update is already underway or future action required
      - Report does not name the responsible party for ensuring beneficiary nomination form is provided or tracked, despite marking the action as client responsibility
    The requirement asks whether ancillary recommendations are stated with an owner and consistent with file records. Three ancillary recommendations appear in the report: (1) Will review, (2) Power of Attorney setup, and (3) Beneficiary nomination. Comparison is possible because the file contains substantive estate planning evidence. Guard applies: 'An adviser recommending the client set up a Power of Attorney is a different claim from the client already having one.' The file [P1] records the client is already in the process of appointing daughters as power of attorneys with solicitor involvement, not that the client needs to set up power of attorney. The report [P9] states it 'would be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney', which is phrased as a forward recommendation but file shows this action is already underway. The will recommendation is consistent: report states will 'needs to be updated' and file confirms client 'is in the process of revising will' [P1]. Beneficiary nomination is recommended with 'Client' as responsible party; the report states the form 'will be provided after the transfer process is begun' but does not name who provides it or confirm it will be tracked. The canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted=false, which aligns with file evidence that power of attorney is being appointed but not yet granted at the report date. Will status in canonical model (hasWill=true, isWillUpToDate=false) is consistent with file evidence. No inconsistency exists between report and file on substance, but the report does not acknowledge that power of attorney setup is already underway via solicitor—it presents the recommendation as if it were a new action rather than ongoing. The beneficiary nomination recommendation lacks specification of who ensures the form is provided and how completion is monitored, creating a process gap rather than a consistency failure.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states Platform charge 0.19% and Investment Management 0.20% totalling 0.39% for recommended plan. Aviva documentation shows fund OCF/TER of 0.19% and 0.20% respectively, but does not separately state a 0.19% platform charge as a distinct layer—the charges appear to be fund charges, not platform plus fund charges. The decomposition of 0.39% into Platform 0.19% and Investment Management 0.20% cannot be reconciled from the Aviva charges documentation, which treats these as fund OCF figures, not platform + fund split. Report's initial adviser fee of 3.00% (£9,838.36) is calculated on transfer values of £327,945.44. Verification: 3% × £327,945.44 = £9,838.36. This is arithmetically correct. However, the canonical model notes identify that £9,838.36 ÷ £67,945.44 (transfer value of two arrangements only) = 14.49%, creating ambiguity about which base was used. The report calculates 3% on the full £327,945.44 current value, not the £67,945.44 being transferred. The basis discrepancy is unresolved in the evidence. Aviva illustration confirms initial adviser charge 3.00% = £9,838.36 on £327,945.44 total cash transfer, and ongoing adviser charge 1.00% payable monthly (£265.09 estimated). Report states ongoing adviser fee is 1.00% pa. These align. 19 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following cost layers for the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension: Platform 0.19% ongoing, Investment Management 0.20% ongoing, for a Total Charge of 0.39% pa. In monetary terms, this equates to £623.10 platform charges and £655.89 investment management charges, totalling £1,278.99 annually on the fund value. Initial adviser fee is 3.00% (£9,838.36). Ongoing adviser fee is 1.00% pa (£265.09 per month estimated). The report states that transaction costs of underlying funds are excluded because they are variable and subject to change based on market conditions and trading activity, and notes these are already accounted for in fund performance figures presented net of all charges. Product and platform charges are disclosed.
    File:     Fund factsheets (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc) show OCF/TER of 0.20% and 0.19% respectively. Aviva pension charges documentation shows HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc TER/OCF of 0.19% and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity Ongoing charge of 0.20%, with no initial charges. Aviva illustration shows initial adviser charge of 3.00% equalling £9,838.36 deducted from cash transfer payments totalling £327,945.44, and ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% payable monthly. The Aviva pension illustration shows Platform charge (Aviva) but does not separately itemise a platform charge figure; it shows Investment Management charges for the funds themselves. Fund charges data shows weighted average fund charges across True Potential existing arrangements but no platform charge is specified for True Potential (stated as 0.40% in existing arrangements, but this includes adviser charge 0.50% not yet incurred, creating ambiguity in the basis).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Platform charge 0.19% and Investment Management 0.20% totalling 0.39% for recommended plan. Aviva documentation shows fund OCF/TER of 0.19% and 0.20% respectively, but does not separately state a 0.19% platform charge as a distinct layer—the charges appear to be fund charges, not platform plus fund charges. The decomposition of 0.39% into Platform 0.19% and Investment Management 0.20% cannot be reconciled from the Aviva charges documentation, which treats these as fund OCF figures, not platform + fund split.
      - Report's initial adviser fee of 3.00% (£9,838.36) is calculated on transfer values of £327,945.44. Verification: 3% × £327,945.44 = £9,838.36. This is arithmetically correct. However, the canonical model notes identify that £9,838.36 ÷ £67,945.44 (transfer value of two arrangements only) = 14.49%, creating ambiguity about which base was used. The report calculates 3% on the full £327,945.44 current value, not the £67,945.44 being transferred. The basis discrepancy is unresolved in the evidence.
      - Aviva illustration confirms initial adviser charge 3.00% = £9,838.36 on £327,945.44 total cash transfer, and ongoing adviser charge 1.00% payable monthly (£265.09 estimated). Report states ongoing adviser fee is 1.00% pa. These align.
      - Transaction costs are stated as excluded 'because they are variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions and trading activity.' The report discloses this exclusion with reasons. The fund factsheets (Vanguard, HSBC) and Aviva KFD state that ongoing charges figures 'exclude portfolio transaction costs.' This is consistent disclosure of a material exclusion with reasons provided.
    The requirement tests whether all material cost layers are disclosed and consistent. The report discloses six material cost components: (1) Platform charge 0.19% pa, (2) Investment Management charge 0.20% pa, (3) Initial adviser fee 3.00%, (4) Ongoing adviser fee 1.00% pa, and (5) an explicit exclusion of transaction costs with stated reasons. All are present in the report's cost disclosure section. The Aviva pension charges document and fund factsheets corroborate the fund OCF figures of 0.19% (HSBC) and 0.20% (Vanguard). The Aviva illustration corroborates the adviser fees (3.00% initial = £9,838.36, and 1.00% ongoing). However, a material ambiguity arises in the composition of the 0.39% total charge. The report presents this as Platform 0.19% + Investment Management 0.20% = 0.39% pa. The Aviva charges document shows the HSBC fund at 0.19% OCF and Vanguard fund at 0.20% OCF, but does not separately identify a 0.19% 'platform charge' as a distinct layer. The Aviva illustration references 'Aviva charge' but directs readers to separate Key Features Documents for details, stating only 'For details of your Aviva charges, please refer to your Key features illustration(s), provided separately.' The KFD itself states ongoing charges of 0.19% and 0.20% but labels these as fund charges (OCF), not platform fees. The report's decomposition treats the 0.39% as a two-layer stack (Platform + Investment Management), but the evidence suggests these may be fund OCF figures only, with any platform charge either embedded, separately accounted, or not itemised in the passages provided. This creates a basis ambiguity: is the report stating Platform fee 0.19% separately, or conflating fund management charges with platform infrastructure? The Aviva T&Cs reference platform fees in context of discretionary model charges and DIM arrangements, but do not quantify a baseline platform fee of 0.19% in the passages retrieved. The initial advice fee of 3.00% = £9,838.36 is arithmetically sound when applied to the £327,945.44 total current value across all three existing arrangements. However, the canonical model ambiguity flags that this may have been calculated on a different base (£67,945.44 transfer value only = 14.49%), suggesting potential confusion in the original report about which assets the fee applies to. The illustration clarifies the fee is deducted from 'cash transfer payments' totalling £327,945.44, suggesting it is applied to total assets held, not transfer value alone. Transaction costs are properly disclosed as excluded, with reasons, consistent with fund factsheet language. The cumulative effect of charges is stated in monetary terms (£1,278.99 pa) and the illustration provides projected values showing the impact of charges over time. Aggregation is present. The comparison can be performed because the fund charges are corroborated. The ambiguity about platform charge decomposition does not prevent comparison—it reveals that the basis of the report's cost split is not fully supported by the evidence provided. This is a disclosure clarity issue: the report claims a 0.19% platform charge but the evidence does not isolate such a charge as a separate platform fee; it shows fund OCF figures only.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, calculated as £9,838.36 on current transfer values of £67,945.44. It also states an ongoing fee of 1% per annum of fund value, projected as £3,181.08 for the first year. These fees are stated to be paid directly by the pension scheme on transfer (initial) and automatically taken from funds invested (ongoing).
    File:     The TFAS Wealth Client Agreement [P14] states: 'Initial Service - There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on with a minimum of £1,350 fee. Based on the value of your funds, our fee will be £9,839.36'. The Client Agreement [P20] states for ongoing services: 'we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states initial fee as £9,838.36; the Client Agreement states £9,839.36 (difference of £1.00, approximately 0.01% variance).
      - The suitability report projects first-year ongoing fee as £3,181.08; this figure does not appear in the Client Agreement or any supporting document. The Client Agreement establishes the rate (1%) and mechanism (automatic deduction) but not the monetary projection.
      - The suitability report does not explicitly state the minimum fee floor of £1,350 for the initial service mentioned in the Client Agreement, though the stated fee (3% of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36 before any adjustment, or as calculated £9,838-£9,839) far exceeds this minimum.
    The percentage rates match exactly: both the report and agreement specify 3% initial and 1% ongoing. The initial monetary fee differs by £1.00 (£9,838.36 vs £9,839.36), a rounding difference of 0.01% that falls within normal variance for currency rounding at this scale and does not signal a substantive discrepancy. The Client Agreement specifies this fee applies to 'the value of the funds that we advise on' with a minimum of £1,350. The stated transfer value is £67,945.44, so 3% = £2,038.36 (minimum applies). However, the report's stated monetary amount of £9,838.36 is neither 3% of £67,945.44 nor any recognisable multiple. The extraction metadata notes this inconsistency: '3% of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The figure £9,838.36 appears to be 14.49% of the transfer value.' This arithmetic impossibility was flagged in the extraction's internal inconsistencies. The report's fee basis and percentage are consistent with the agreement, but the monetary amount stated cannot be reconciled with the stated transfer value and percentage. The ongoing fee rate (1%) matches the agreement; the monetary projection (£3,181.08) cannot be verified against any document and does not reconcile to the stated fund value. On the basis of fund value alone after deduction of initial fee, 1% of approximately £58,107 (£67,945.44 minus initial fee) would yield approximately £581, not £3,181. The projection may reflect a different valuation basis (post-inheritance, gross of all existing charges, or a cumulative total) not disclosed in the report. The mechanism (automatic deduction from funds) matches the agreement in both cases.
      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement.md [A] (P14)
        "Initial Service - There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on with a minimum of £1,350 fee. Based on the value of your funds, our fee will be £9,839.36"
      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement.md [A] (P20)
        "we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested"
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I] (P4)
        "we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is 9,838.36 with current transfer values"
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I] (P21)
        "Initial adviser charge of 3% which is taken from the new solution, reducing the initial investment accordingly. An ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%"

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures of 1.5%, 1.5%, and 1.6% for Low, Mid, and High growth scenarios respectively. Critical Yield figures are stated as 0.41%, 3.37%, and 6.32% for the same scenarios. The effect of switching is stated as -12.42% across all scenarios. The report claims the recommended Aviva scheme offers lower overall costs: platform charges of 0.21% (vs True Potential 0.40%, saving 0.19% or £88.68) and ongoing fund charges of 0.20% (vs existing 0.58%, saving £177.35). Annual recurring charges for the recommended plan are stated as 0.39% (0.19% platform + 0.20% investment management), totalling £1,278.99. The report states these figures are 'based on the amount being invested into the plan' and 'assume no investment growth'.
    File:     The Aviva pension illustration [P1] shows projected fund values at three growth rates with charges applied, but does not isolate RIY or Critical Yield figures. The Aviva KFD [P12] and T&C [P5], [P6], [P9], [P13], [P16] describe platform mechanics and charges structure but do not state specific RIY or Critical Yield percentages. The SAP documents [P20], [P21], [P22], [P24] show switching analysis with different RIY and Critical Yield figures: for the full combined transfer (all three schemes), RIY is stated as 1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6% and Critical Yield as 0.74%, 3.7%, 6.66% at 0%, 2.94%, 5.88% growth rates respectively. For True Potential alone [P21], Critical Yield figures are 0.5%, 3.46%, 6.41%. The effect of switching is shown as -14.75% for True Potential and -20.75% for all schemes combined. Platform charges are confirmed as 0.19% (not 0.21%) in [P20]. Fund charges for recommended funds are 0.2100% and 0.2207% (weighted average 0.2154%) per [P20]. Vanguard and HSBC fund charges are 0.19% per [P15]. The fact sheet [P19] lists ongoing charges of 0.2% but does not reconcile to specific RIY or Critical Yield. The report in [P18] states annual recurring charges of 0.39% total (0.19% platform + 0.20% investment management), matching the canonical model.
    Discrepancies:
      - Critical Yield 0.41% stated in report for Low growth but SAP shows 0.5% (+0.5%) for True Potential alone at 0% growth and 0.74% (+0.74%) for all schemes combined at 0% growth.
      - Critical Yield 3.37% stated in report for Mid growth but SAP shows 3.46% (+0.52%) for True Potential at 2.94% growth and 3.7% (+0.76%) for all schemes at 2.94% growth.
      - Critical Yield 6.32% stated in report for High growth but SAP shows 6.41% (+0.53%) for True Potential at 5.88% growth and 6.66% (+0.78%) for all schemes at 5.88% growth.
      - Effect of switching stated as -12.42% in report but SAP shows -14.75% for True Potential alone and -20.75% for all schemes combined.
      - Platform charge stated as 0.21% in report text but confirmed as 0.19% in both [P18] annual recurring charges table and [P20] SAP document.
      - Report claims savings of £88.68 (0.19% on £46,672.45 = £88.68) but this appears to apply only to True Potential platform differential; total impact unclear as Quilter and Nest are also being transferred.
      - RIY figures 1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6% in report appear to match SAP but only for all-schemes combined analysis; basis not clearly specified in report.
      - Cumulative cost impact presented in isolation by fund type (platform, fund management) but not as single aggregate figure showing total annual cost reduction or cost as percentage of transferred value.
    The report presents Critical Yield and effect-of-switching figures that do not match the SAP switching analysis documents, which are the supporting evidence for the recommendation. The SAP documents [P21], [P22] show that when applied to True Potential alone, Critical Yield is 0.5%, 3.46%, 6.41% (not 0.41%, 3.37%, 6.32%) and effect of switching is -14.75% (not -12.42%). When applied to all three schemes, Critical Yield is 0.74%, 3.7%, 6.66% and effect is -20.75%. The report does not disclose which scope has been used. RIY figures 1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6% in the report match the combined-schemes SAP RIY row [P22], but the report states these under 'Recommended' heading without clearly indicating they apply to all arrangements being transferred, not just the Aviva recommendation. The platform charge is stated as 0.21% in [P10] narrative but the actual charge confirmed in the annual recurring charges table [P18] and SAP [P20] is 0.19%. The £88.68 saving quoted applies to the 0.19% differential on True Potential alone, yet the report does not clarify whether this saving applies to the full transferred amount (£67,945.44) or only to True Potential (£46,672.80). The fund charges cited as 0.20% appear in [P18] as a rounded figure but [P20] shows actual weighted average of 0.2154%. The report discloses the annual recurring charges of 0.39% and monetary value of £1,278.99 [P18], which arithmetically reconciles: 0.39% × £327,945.15 ≈ £1,278.99 (using total of all current values). However, the report does not present a cumulative cost illustration showing the combined effect of platform, fund and adviser charges over time, or contrasting this with the existing arrangement costs. The switching analysis tables in the SAP [P21], [P22], [P24] show projected fund values under different scenarios but these are not replicated or reconciled in the main suitability report. The claim of 'lower overall cost structure' [P10] is supported in principle by the platform differential (0.19% vs 0.40%) and fund differential (0.2154% vs 0.58%), but the quantified benefit statements refer only to True Potential, not to Quilter (£260,000) and Nest (£21,272.64), which together represent 68% of the assets being transferred. No evidence is provided that consolidation onto Aviva reduces costs for those arrangements. The critical yield increases (0.5% to 3.46% to 6.41% for True Potential, or 0.74% to 3.7% to 6.66% for all schemes) indicate that the Aviva recommendation requires higher investment returns to match existing scheme outcomes, contrary to the narrative that switching improves value. This is not contradicted by any guard: growth rate assumptions are disclosed and RIY figures are consistent with switching-cost impact, but the mismatch between report figures and supporting SAP figures is genuine.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' comparing existing and recommended charges on a per-arrangement basis. For EA2 (True Potential), it states an existing annual charge of 0.98% (£457.39), with an impact of -0.59% (savings of £275.37). For EA3 (Nest), it states an existing annual charge of 0.30% (£63.82), with an impact of +0.09% (cost increase of £19.15). The report also states platform charges for EA1 (Quilter) as 0.27% (conditional), and for EA2 it discloses platform charges 0.40%, ongoing advice charge 0.50%, and fund charges 0.58%, totalling 1.48%. The recommended Aviva plan shows total annual recurring charges of 0.39% (£1,278.99), comprising platform 0.19% and investment management 0.20%. The report claims the Aviva recommendation provides 'lower overall cost structure' and 'lower costs' with explicit savings calculations.
    File:     Evidence from provider illustrations (Annes M SAP) shows the Nest arrangement has an annual management charge of 0.3% (P1, P4). True Potential platform charges are stated as 0.40% with ongoing advice charge 0.50% and weighted average fund management charges 0.58% (P12). The Quilter conditional platform charge is stated as 0.27% (P5, P11). The Aviva recommended plan shows: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio Ongoing Cost 0.2100%, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity Fund Ongoing Cost 0.2207%, Weighted Average 0.2154% (P1). The report's own extracted charges table shows True Potential with platform 0.40%, advice 0.50%, fund 0.58% totalling 1.48% (P12), and separately in the comparison states 0.98% 'excluding ongoing advice charge' (P7). The Aviva illustration shows annual recurring charges of 0.39% total: platform 0.19% (£623.10) and investment management 0.20% (£655.89) (P7).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states EA2 (True Potential) existing charge as 0.98% 'excluding ongoing advice charge' in the comparison table, but the arrangements table separately itemises platform 0.40%, advice 0.50%, and fund 0.58% totalling 1.48%. The 0.98% figure is unexplained and does not reconcile to either platform-only (0.40%) or the stated components.
      - The comparison basis for True Potential is stated as 'of fund value pa (excluding ongoing advice charge)', creating ambiguity: is the 0.98% platform plus fund only (0.40% + 0.58% = 0.98%), or does it include something else? The extraction footnote suggests 0.98% in the comparison is platform 0.40% plus advice 0.50% plus fund 0.58% minus something, yet this arithmetic does not yield 0.98%.
      - Arithmetic check on EA2: 0.98% of £46,672.80 (True Potential current value per extraction) = £457.39. This matches the stated monetary amount. However, the basis stated is 'excluding ongoing advice charge', yet 0.50% is part of the itemised charges. If 0.50% advice is excluded, then 0.40% + 0.58% = 0.98%, which matches. But the report then claims a -0.59% impact (savings), which when applied to the Aviva 0.39% charge suggests the comparison is: 0.39% - 0.98% = -0.59%. This implies the 0.98% comparison includes the 0.50% advice charge despite the stated basis excluding it.
      - The impact amount for EA2 is -£275.37, stated as basis 'of fund value pa (savings)'. Checking: £457.39 - (0.39% × £46,672.80) = £457.39 - £182.02 = £275.37. This calculation uses the 0.98% as the full comparison figure, including what the basis says is excluded (advice charge).
      - Report states for the recommended Aviva plan: 'Annual recurring charges' of 0.39% (£1,278.99) with platform 0.19% and investment management 0.20%. However, P1 shows the recommended fund charges are 0.2154% (weighted average of 0.2100% and 0.2207%), not 0.20%. The platform charge of 0.19% is a negotiated reduction from standard (P1 states 'Decrease in Annual Management Charge of 0.08%' from 0.25% standard, yielding 0.17% base, plus fund charges would total c. 0.2% platform at the phrasing used in the report, though the illustration shows 0.19% platform explicitly).
      - Inconsistency within the report itself: For True Potential in the existing arrangements table, it states 0.40% platform, 0.50% advice, 0.58% fund = 1.48% total. In the comparison table for the same EA2, it states 0.98% existing charge 'excluding ongoing advice charge'. If advice is excluded, it should be 0.40% + 0.58% = 0.98% (which arithmetically works), but the extraction provides two contradictory bases for the same plan.
      - The report's description of the impact for Nest states '+0.09%' as a 'cost increase', yet the calculation shows the Nest plan at 0.30%, and if moving to Aviva at 0.39%, the difference is 0.39% - 0.30% = 0.09%. However, the report earlier justified switching FROM Nest by stating the Nest 'risk level' is 8 and the agreed risk is 7, not by cost. The cost increase is presented without explanation of offsetting benefits specific to the Nest switch.
    The comparison is technically performed and monetary figures can be arithmetically verified against fund values. However, there is a material inconsistency in how the True Potential charge is presented. In the existing arrangements section, True Potential is itemised as 0.40% platform + 0.50% advice + 0.58% fund = 1.48%. In the comparison table, the same plan EA2 is stated as 0.98% 'excluding ongoing advice charge'. The statement 'excluding ongoing advice charge' creates the impression that the ongoing advice charge (0.50%) is removed from the comparison, which would yield 0.40% + 0.58% = 0.98%. This is arithmetically correct. However, the impact calculation (0.98% - 0.39% = 0.59% saving) then includes the effect of removing the 0.50% advice charge when switching, which contradicts the stated basis of the comparison. The guard permits 'Comparisons on different bases (with and without adviser fees) are both legitimate', but the report must clearly state which basis each comparison uses. Here the basis is ambiguous: it says 'excluding ongoing advice charge' but the impact calculation implicitly removes the 0.50% from the existing total, treating it as part of what is being saved. This is not a stated finding — it is an internal contradiction about whether the advice charge is being excluded from the comparison or included in the savings calculation. For Nest, the cost increases by 0.09% when switching to Aviva, but the report does not explain why this cost increase is acceptable. The Nest switch rationale (P11, P12) focuses on risk misalignment (Nest at risk 8 vs agreed risk 7), not cost benefit. The cost-benefit analysis is therefore incomplete: the report shows Nest customers will pay 0.09% more annually (£19.15) but does not quantify offsetting benefits or explain whether the risk correction justifies the cost. The Quilter switch shows a saving of 0.08% platform charges (0.27% to 0.19%), claimed as £208 per annum based on estimated fund value. The estimated fund value is approximately £260,000 (per the extraction), so 0.08% × £260,000 = £208, which reconciles. For True Potential, the report claims both platform savings (0.40% to 0.19% = 0.21% saving, or £88.68 based on fund value, implying fund value of £422,000, but the current value is £46,672.80) and fund charge savings (0.58% to 0.20%, = 0.38% saving, or £177.35 based on fund value £46,672.80, which reconciles). These two savings are presented separately (P10) but the comparison table combines them into a single impact, creating potential for double-counting or confusion. The evidence shows that provider illustrations use different bases: the SAP illustrations show fund charges net of all costs (e.g., P1 shows Aviva weighted average fund ongoing cost 0.2154%), while the report rounds this to 0.20% in its summary. This is a minor rounding difference (0.2154% vs 0.20%) and is typical of reports, but it should be disclosed if material to conclusions. Overall, the comparison is performed and figures are largely arithmetically sound when checked against plan values, but the basis of the comparison — particularly what is and is not included in the 'existing charge' for True Potential — is stated ambiguously, and the cost increase for Nest is not explained or justified against stated benefits.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts multiple cost benefits and value propositions: (1) Lower platform charges for Quilter switch: 0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 per annum; (2) Lower platform charges for True Potential switch: Aviva 0.21% vs True Potential 0.40%, saving 0.19% or £88.68; (3) Lower fund charges: Aviva 0.20% vs existing 0.58%, saving £177.35 per annum; (4) Benefits include active monitoring, greater investment choice (wider range vs 6 funds in existing plan), online access to platform and reports, and flexible retirement options. The report claims recommended funds have 'strong risk-adjusted performance within its category' and states the recommended funds 'remain aligned with your changing needs.' An initial advice fee of 3% is stated, calculated as £9,838.36 on transfer values of £67,945.44.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) True Potential platform charges documented at 0.40% (passage P7, existing arrangements table). (2) Aviva platform charges stated as 0.21% in recommended arrangement. (3) Nest Higher Risk Fund performance over 5 years: 47.84% cumulative return (passages P2, P11). (4) Recommended Risk 7 funds performance (HSBC/Vanguard) over 5 years: 45.91% cumulative return (passages P2, P11). (5) True Potential fund performance over 5 years: 38.53% cumulative return (passages P2, P11). (6) Aviva Multi-asset Plus IV 2 performance (at 19/03/2026): 5-year return 46.39%; HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic: 49.61%; Vanguard LifeStrategy 80%: 47.28% (passage P8). (7) Fact Find shows monthly household expenditure £2,836.23; report later states personal expenditure in retirement £2,000 net per month (passages P22, P19). (8) Initial advice fee: calculated as 3% would equal £2,038.36 on £67,945.44 transfer value, not £9,838.36 as stated (derived figure mismatch).
    Discrepancies:
      - Initial advice fee arithmetic: Report states 3% of £67,945.44 = £9,838.36, but 3% of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36. The stated amount £9,838.36 represents 14.49% of transfer value, not 3%.
      - Performance comparison basis unclear: Report states recommended funds (Risk 7 HSBC/Vanguard) at 45.91% cumulative 5-year return underperformed Nest (47.84%) and outperformed True Potential (38.53%), yet claims funds offer 'strong risk-adjusted performance' without providing risk-adjusted metrics or volatility data to support this claim.
      - Cost saving claims and performance tension: Report claims fund charge saving of £177.35 annual (0.58% to 0.20%) and platform saving of £88.68 (0.40% to 0.21%), yet the higher-cost existing True Potential fund (0.40% platform + estimated fund charges) returned 38.53% over 5 years while the lower-cost recommended Aviva arrangement (0.21% platform + 0.20% fund charges) returned lower performance (45.91% for the recommended fund mix vs 47.84% for existing Nest; specific Aviva fund performance at comparable date shows Aviva Multi-asset Plus 46.39%, HSBC 49.61%, Vanguard 47.28%).
      - Cumulative impact of charges not aggregated: Report discloses platform charges (0.21% vs 0.40%), fund charges (0.20% vs 0.58%), and initial advice fee (stated as 3% but calculated incorrectly), but does not provide an all-in aggregated annual charge or cumulative cost impact over the projection period to retirement age 87.
      - Critical Yield/Required Outperformance not clearly linked to costs: The derived figure 'required outperformance: £19.15 at 1.23% implies fund value £1,556.91' does not match any arrangement value, suggesting the cost impact analysis may be incomplete or incorrectly calculated.
      - Basis of expenditure assumption ambiguous: Report uses £2,000 net monthly retirement expenditure in cashflow (passage P19) but Fact Find documents £2,836.23 household expenditure (passage P22), creating uncertainty about whether cost benefit comparisons are on consistent basis.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Breakdown of initial advice fee structure: Report states 3% but calculation does not reconcile; unclear if this includes other fees (transfer, setup, ongoing adviser charges) or if 3% applies only to part of the transfer value., Aggregated all-in annual charge for recommended arrangement: Individual platform (0.21%) and fund charges (0.20%) are stated but no total ongoing annual charge is provided; ongoing adviser charge mentioned but not quantified as recurring percentage or amount., Baseline charge for existing True Potential arrangement: While 0.40% platform charge is stated, the fund charge component (0.58%) is stated separately; unclear if True Potential 0.40% platform includes any adviser charge or is platform only, and whether this is on comparable basis to Aviva's quoted charges., Risk-adjusted performance metrics: Report claims 'strong risk-adjusted performance' for recommended funds but provides no Sharpe ratio, Information ratio, or volatility comparison to support this; raw performance figures show underperformance vs Nest., Transfer and transaction costs: Report does not disclose whether there are exit charges on existing arrangements, transfer fees to new provider, or any other switching costs that would affect net benefit of proposed charge savings., Projected cumulative cost impact: No illustration provided showing total charges (initial and ongoing) as percentage of fund value or monetary amount over projection period to age 87, despite extensive cashflow modelling in report.
    The requirement asks whether all material charges are disclosed and whether benefits claimed for higher costs are supported and consistent with evidence. Several material issues arise: (1) ARITHMETIC FAILURE: The initial advice fee of 3% is arithmetically impossible. 3% of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. This is a 7.8x overstatement and represents 14.49% rather than 3%. This is a material charge error and falls into 'highest' severity under the guidance (charge figure arithmetically impossible). (2) AGGREGATION ABSENT: The report discloses individual charge components (platform 0.21% for Aviva vs 0.40% for True Potential; fund charges 0.20% vs 0.58%) but nowhere states an all-in annual charge or cumulative cost impact. This is 'High' severity (no aggregated all-in cost). (3) PERFORMANCE INCONSISTENCY: The report claims cost benefits from switching but the performance data contradict the value proposition. Nest (higher cost, risk 8) returned 47.84% over 5 years; recommended funds (lower cost, risk 7 HSBC/Vanguard) returned 45.91%. The Aviva funds show similar or lower performance (Aviva Multi-asset Plus 46.39% at 19/03/2026). The report acknowledges this ('underperformed against the Nest fund') but frames it as acceptable due to risk profile difference. However, it then claims cost savings without showing that the savings offset the performance gap or justify the cost increase relative to alternatives. (4) CHARGE BASIS INCONSISTENCY: True Potential platform charge stated as 0.40% in existing arrangements section, but the switching analysis states True Potential charges as 0.98% (0.50% advice + 0.58% fund). The report does not clearly specify which True Potential charges apply to existing vs future, or explain why the comparison uses different bases. This creates ambiguity about whether the 0.19% 'saving' is comparing like with like. (5) BENEFITS UNSUPPORTED: The stated benefits (active monitoring, greater choice, online access, flexibility) are generic platform and service features not specific to this client or genuinely unique to Aviva. The report provides no evidence that these benefits are material to this client or that they could not be obtained within existing arrangements. The report does not cite any constraint on the True Potential or Nest platforms that prevents these services. (6) MISSING AGGREGATED ANALYSIS: Despite producing multiple cashflow projections (passages P12-P14 show projected fund values to age 87), the report does not show the total impact of initial fee (£9,838.36 stated) plus ongoing charges over the full period, nor does it reconcile the cost impact with the performance assumptions used in the projections. (7) VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: The Fact Find (P24) notes the client has stopped working, has emergency fund shortfall of £17,000, and is living off depleted savings (£7,000, needs £24,000). This financial vulnerability is material to a 14.49% initial fee (or even a 3% fee): a client in tight financial circumstances is particularly sensitive to high upfront costs, and the report does not explain how this fee burden aligns with her stated need to preserve emergency reserves and replace lost employment income. The report notes her plan to withdraw £40,000 as tax-free cash to cover legal fees and pay off liabilities; a fee that consumes 9,838/67,945 = 14.5% of transfer value materially affects this capacity.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states on page 21 that 'this means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by 1.23% per annum to age 89'. This figure represents the required outperformance as a percentage of fund value per annum to justify the cost difference between the existing and recommended arrangements.
    File:     No supporting evidence document provides an independent assessment of whether costs are excessive relative to benefits, or corroborates the required outperformance figure. The requirement is assessed from the report alone and does not rely on external corroborating passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The required outperformance figure of 1.23% pa is stated without explicit disclosure of the underlying cost comparison that produced it. The canonical model shows an 'annual charge difference' of £19.15 at 0.09%, which implies a fund value of £21,277.78 (Nest's current value), yet the report's required outperformance of 1.23% pa implies a fund value of only £1,556.91 when calculated backwards (£19.15 ÷ 1.23% = £1,556.91). These two implied fund values do not reconcile.
      - The report does not disclose the absolute cost difference in money terms between the existing and recommended arrangements, nor does it break down the component charges (platform, fund, adviser fees) for comparison.
      - The internal inconsistency in the extracted canonical model shows platform charges for True Potential stated as 0.40% in the existing arrangements table but 0.98% (including 0.50% ongoing advice charge and 0.58% fund charges) in the 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment'. The report does not clarify whether the ongoing adviser fee of 0.50% pa is disclosed to the client and is part of the justified cost.
      - The initial advice fee is stated as 3% but calculated as £9,838.36 on transfer values of £67,945.44, which equals 14.49%, not 3%. This arithmetic discrepancy means the actual total cost impact is not correctly stated.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The basis and calculation method for the required outperformance figure of 1.23% pa is not transparent. The report does not state which charges are included in 'current plan' costs and which in 'proposed plan' costs., The absolute annual cost difference in money terms (e.g., £XX per annum) between existing and recommended arrangements is not disclosed., The total all-in cost as a percentage of fund value for both existing and recommended arrangements is not aggregated and compared., The cost impact of the initial advice fee (correct percentage and amount) is not clearly stated or integrated into the outperformance requirement., The ongoing adviser fee structure and how it differs between arrangements (if at all) is not clearly disclosed., Cumulative cost impact over the investment term to age 89 is not illustrated.
    The requirement tests whether costs are excessive relative to benefits. The report states a required outperformance of 1.23% pa but does not provide sufficient transparency to validate this figure or assess whether it represents good value. The extraction revealed an internal inconsistency: the True Potential arrangement's ongoing costs are stated as 0.40% (platform only) but elsewhere as 0.98% (platform plus ongoing adviser charge). The report does not clarify whether the ongoing adviser charge is reflected in the cost comparison. The initial advice fee shows an arithmetic error: stated as 3% but actually 14.49% of the transfer value (£9,838.36 on £67,945.44). This means the true total cost to the client is not accurately disclosed. The required outperformance calculation cannot be verified from the information provided: backwards calculation of the 1.23% figure yields an implied fund value of £1,556.91, which matches neither the Nest value (£21,277.78) nor any arrangement total, suggesting the calculation methodology is not transparent. Without a clear, itemised cost comparison (initial fees, ongoing annual percentage charges, platform charges, fund charges, adviser fees for both existing and proposed arrangements), and without cumulative cost impact over term, the report does not adequately justify why the recommended arrangement's costs are not excessive. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerabilities are recorded in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.
