        "this means that your proposed plan will need to outperform your current plan by 1.23% per annum to age 89"

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts two cost comparisons: (1) a £19.15 per annum increase in charges when switching Nest to Aviva, described as 'a relatively small increase of £19.15 per annum, based on the current values'; (2) a -£275.37 saving (or -0.59%) when switching True Potential to Aviva. The report states platform charges reduce from 0.40% (True Potential) to 0.21% (Aviva), saving £88.68, and fund charges reduce from 0.58% to 0.20%, saving £177.35. For Quilter, the report states a platform charge reduction from 0.27% to 0.19%, saving £208 per annum. The report provides benefits justification: lower cost structure, active monitoring, online access, flexible retirement options, and wider fund range. Initial advice fee of 3% is stated but the extracted inconsistency shows £9,838.36 claimed as 3% of £67,945.44 (actual 3% would be £2,038.36, making the stated figure 14.49% instead). The report recommends switching all three existing arrangements to Aviva.
    File:     Provider illustration documents [P1], [P2], [P3], [P4] contain Critical Yield and Reduction in Yield analyses. For True Potential (IAC only, ex existing OAC) [P3]: Existing Scheme projected fund at 2.94% growth = £48,651; Aviva projected = £47,300, a reduction of 1.35% (Reduction in Yield 0.3%). For Nest (ex existing OAC) [P1]: Existing Scheme at 2.94% growth = £85,961; Aviva Pension Portfolio = £73,200, a reduction of 14.75% (Reduction in Yield 1%). For Quilter/SELECTAPENSION (All charges ex existing OAC) [P1]: no direct comparison table shown. The suitability report [P8], [P9] states Quilter platform charges 0.27% vs Aviva 0.19% (saving £208), True Potential 0.40% vs Aviva 0.21% (saving £88.68 stated in [P9]), and fund charges True Potential 0.58% vs Aviva 0.20% (saving £177.35). Death benefit analysis [P5], [P6] shows day-one transfer losses: True Potential £1,400 loss (£46,672.80 to £45,272.62), Nest loss of £638.18 (£21,272.64 to £20,634.46). The provider illustrations show Critical Yield requirements ranging from 0.09% to 0.53% above assumed growth for Aviva, and Reduction in Yield of 0.3% to 1.6% depending on scenario.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states £19.15 per annum increase for Nest switch; derived figure from computed charge arithmetic (19.15 at 0.09% implies fund value £21,277.78, matching Nest current value) but provider illustration [P1] shows Nest switching to Aviva results in 14.75% reduction in fund value at 2.94% growth (£85,961 existing vs £73,200 Aviva), representing far greater cost impact than £19.15 per annum suggests.
      - Report states -£275.37 saving (-0.59%) for True Potential switch; provider illustration [P3] shows True Potential existing scheme projects to £48,651 at 2.94% growth vs Aviva £47,300, a reduction of 1.35% or approximately -£1,351 (not -£275.37). The -£275.37 figure does not reconcile to the Critical Yield projection.
      - Report claims 3% initial advice fee on £67,945.44 transfer value, stated as £9,838.36; 3% of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36. The stated £9,838.36 is 14.49% of transfer value, not 3%. Internal inconsistency within report's own figures.
      - Report states True Potential platform charges 0.40% but extracted data notes this was 0.98% in 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' (including 0.50% advice charge and 0.58% fund charges). Report later correctly distinguishes ongoing fund charges 0.58% separately, but initial statement of 0.40% platform charge is incomplete representation of total costs.
      - Report describes Nest switch as beneficial for 'lower cost structure' but provider illustration [P1] shows Critical Yield requirement of 1% (Reduction in Yield 1%) and actual projected shortfall of £12,761 at 2.94% growth (£85,961 vs £73,200). This contradicts the 'lower cost' narrative.
      - Report describes True Potential switch benefits including 'lower overall cost structure' and lists fund charge savings of £177.35, yet provider illustration [P3] shows projected fund at retirement £1,351 lower with Aviva (£47,300 vs £48,651), demonstrating costs do not translate to benefit.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Basis of the -£275.37 saving claim: report does not state whether this is one-off, annual, or cumulative; it does not reconcile to any figure in provider illustrations., Basis of the £19.15 per annum increase: while derived arithmetic implies this is Nest's annual charge difference, the provider illustration shows 14.75% fund reduction on switch, creating unexplained disconnect between annual charge and total cost of recommendation., Aggregated all-in cost comparison: report lists individual cost reductions (£208 for Quilter, £88.68 and £177.35 for True Potential) but never totals cumulative cost or savings across all three arrangements being switched., Adviser fee structure: report states 3% initial advice fee but does not reconcile this to client agreement or state whether this applies to all three arrangements or one only., Alternative costs not presented: report recommends against leaving funds in place or switching only funds within existing arrangements, but does not present cost comparison for these alternatives, only narrative justification., Day-one transfer losses: report does not disclose or aggregate the death benefit reductions shown in provider illustrations (True Potential -£1,400, Nest -£638.18), which represent immediate costs of transfer.
    The requirement asks whether the report discloses all material charges, explains benefits for higher costs, and whether those benefits are supported by and consistent with evidence. Three material failures emerge: (1) ARITHMETIC FAILURE: The initial advice fee stated as 3% of £67,945.44 calculates to £2,038.36, not £9,838.36 as claimed. The stated amount is 14.49% of transfer value. This is an arithmetically impossible charge figure against the stated basis. (2) RECONCILIATION FAILURE: For True Potential, the report claims a -£275.37 saving (-0.59%), but the provider illustration [P3] projects existing scheme at £48,651 (2.94% growth) vs Aviva at £47,300, a shortfall of £1,351 or -2.77%. The -£275.37 figure does not reconcile to any line in the evidence and is unsupported. (3) BENEFIT CLAIM CONTRADICTED BY EVIDENCE: The report recommends switching Nest stating 'lower cost structure' benefits, yet provider illustration [P1] shows projected fund reduction of 14.75% (£85,961 to £73,200) at 2.94% growth and a Reduction in Yield of 1.0%. The annual charge difference of £19.15 cannot explain a 14.75% fund reduction. Similarly, for True Potential, the report claims lower costs justify the switch, but evidence shows the recommended Aviva portfolio projecting £1,351 less at retirement than remaining in True Potential at the same growth assumption. (4) INCOMPLETE AGGREGATION: The report lists individual charge reductions for each arrangement (Quilter £208, True Potential £88.68 + £177.35 = £266.03 total) but never aggregates total cost savings across all three arrangements or shows cumulative impact. The decision logic requires 'cumulative impact and a cost comparison present and arithmetically sound'. (5) SILENT OMISSION OF DAY-ONE COSTS: Provider illustrations [P5], [P6] show death benefit reductions (True Potential -£1,400, Nest -£638.18) occurring on day of transfer. These are material costs never disclosed in the suitability report and not aggregated with ongoing charge impacts. (6) UNRECONCILED BASIS: The report's platform charge for True Potential is stated initially in recommendations as 0.40%, but the extracted metadata notes this rose to 0.98% when including the 0.50% ongoing advice charge. The report does later separate out fund charges (0.58%) but does not clearly disclose to the client what the total ongoing cost will be with advice. (7) NO ALTERNATIVE COST ANALYSIS: The report rejects alternatives (leave in place, switch funds only) but provides no cost figures for these alternatives to support why switching to Aviva is better value. The guard states 'A cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons is a valid outcome', but the report rejects alternatives using cost arguments ('lower overall cost structure') without presenting comparative cost evidence. The vulnerability overlay: the extracted data notes no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded for this client. The ambiguity about retirement income objective (£25,000-£30,000) and retirement expenditure (£2,000 vs £2,986) does not appear to affect the charges analysis directly, as charges are stated in absolute terms and percentages independent of income assumptions. However, if retirement income is lower than assumed, the impact of charges on adequacy of outcome would be proportionally higher; this is not addressed. Overall: evidence is INCOMPLETE, figures are NOT ARITHMETICALLY SOUND (initial advice fee), comparative claims are CONTRADICTED BY PROVIDER ILLUSTRATIONS (Nest and True Potential both show fund reductions despite 'lower cost' claim), and NO AGGREGATED ALL-IN COST is presented.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three reduction in yield (RIY) scenarios for the recommended arrangement. Low growth basis (0%): RIY 1.5%, Critical Yield 0.41%, Effect of Switching -12.42%. Mid growth basis (2.94%): RIY 1.5%, Critical Yield 3.37%, Effect of Switching -12.42%. High growth basis (5.88%): RIY 1.6%, Critical Yield 6.32%, Effect of Switching -12.42%. The report states these figures on page 32 under 'Critical Yield' section.
    File:     The switching analysis documents [P1], [P6], [P7] contain parallel RIY and critical yield tables for the same recommended arrangement (Aviva Pension Portfolio Choice). [P1] and [P6] show for the same growth bases: Low (0%): RIY 1.5%, Critical Yield 0.74%; Mid (2.94%): RIY 1.5%, Critical Yield 3.7%; High (5.88%): RIY 1.6%, Critical Yield 6.66%. [P7] shows Nest transfer: Low: Critical Yield 1.19%; Mid: Critical Yield 4.17%; High: Critical Yield 7.14%. The Aviva product illustration [P4] shows charges reduce growth from 2.8% to 0.6% (a 2.2% reduction, termed here 'reduction in the growth rate'). Fund KIIDs [P9] and [P13] show ongoing charges of 0.20% and 0.19% respectively. No illustration on file matches the low and mid critical yield figures (0.41%, 3.37%) stated in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report Low growth Critical Yield 0.41% vs [P1]/[P6] evidence 0.74% (80.5% difference)
      - Report Mid growth Critical Yield 3.37% vs [P1]/[P6] evidence 3.7% (9.8% difference)
      - Report High growth Critical Yield 6.32% vs [P1]/[P6] evidence 6.66% (5.4% difference)
      - Report Effect of Switching -12.42% vs [P1] evidence -20.75% for True Potential (66.7% difference)
      - Report Effect of Switching -12.42% vs [P6] evidence -14.75% for Nest (18.6% difference)
      - Report cites critical yield figures with annotations '+0.41%', '+0.43%', '+0.44%' but the source documents [P1], [P6], [P7] show different differentials: '+0.74%', '+0.76%', '+0.78%' for Quilter; '+0.52%', '+0.52%', '+0.53%' for True Potential; '+1.19%', '+1.23%', '+1.2%' for Nest
    The report presents reduction in yield and critical yield figures on page 32 that do not reconcile with any of the three switching analysis documents on file. The figures cited in the report (Critical Yield 0.41%, 3.37%, 6.32% for Aviva recommendation) match none of the three existing pension schemes analyzed: neither the Quilter scheme results shown in [P1], nor the True Potential scheme shown in [P6], nor the Nest scheme shown in [P7]. The RIY values (1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6%) do match across report and evidence for all three growth bases. However, the critical yield figures differ materially at all three growth rates. The effect of switching figures (-12.42%) shown in the report also do not match any arrangement: [P1] shows -20.75% for Quilter, [P6] shows -14.75% for Nest, and [P7] shows different values entirely. The annotations in the report ('(+0.41%)', '(+0.43%)', '(+0.44%)') do not match the differentials shown in the supporting documents. The report presents these as statements of fact on page 32, citing Critical Yield directly, but the evidence suggests these may be placeholder figures, incomplete transfer values, or figures from a draft analysis not updated. No document on file shows the specific combination of figures the report asserts. The RIY consistency (matching at 1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6%) indicates the extraction or report generation may have used the correct RIY layer but pulled critical yield figures from a different or incomplete scenario. The discrepancies are material: critical yield at low growth is out by 80% and at high growth by 5%; effect of switching calculations are out by 19-67%. These are the core metrics by which a client should judge whether switching is justified.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% per annum (page 16, section 'Future contact and ongoing services'). The report lists five services included in this charge: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing ongoing suitability of investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The report states this will equal circa £3,181.08 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee, and will vary with fund value. The fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme.
    File:     The Client Agreement (TFAS Wealth Client Agreement, passages P4, P5, P7) states: 'Ongoing Services - For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' The Agreement specifies this is 'subject to a minimum fee of £100'. Under 'Ongoing Services' section (P7), it states the firm will provide 'Ongoing advice and review service, as described in the Important Information about our Services document' until either party considers it no longer appropriate. The Agreement lists ongoing services (P5) as: 'Providing periodic on-going reviews of your existing and/or any new investments we arrange on your behalf to ensure they remain suitable for you.' Initial services are separately listed (P5) but ongoing services description is minimal. The Client Agreement does not detail the five specific services listed in the suitability report; it references an external document 'Important Information about our Services' not provided in the evidence pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report lists five specific ongoing services; the Client Agreement lists only one: periodic reviews to ensure suitability. The additional four services (risk/capacity review, market updates, ad-hoc advice access, holistic financial planning) are asserted in the report but not reflected in the Client Agreement's statement of ongoing service obligations.
      - The Client Agreement specifies 'subject to a minimum fee of £100' but the suitability report does not mention this minimum fee limitation, stating only '1% per annum'.
      - The suitability report provides a specific monetary projection (£3,181.08 for year one) whereas the Client Agreement states only that 'The amount you pay will fluctuate with the value of your investment' with no first-year projection.
      - The Client Agreement references an external document 'Important Information about our Services' as containing the detailed service description, but this document is not in the evidence pack and was not cited by the suitability report when listing the five specific services.
    The suitability report asserts a 1% ongoing annual charge and lists five specific services the client will receive: risk/capacity review, portfolio suitability review, market updates, ad-hoc advice access, and holistic financial planning. The Client Agreement confirms the 1% charge but describes ongoing services far more narrowly: only 'periodic on-going reviews of your existing and/or any new investments we arrange on your behalf to ensure they remain suitable for you.' This is internal inconsistency within the adviser's own documents. The report's five services cannot be reconciled with the Client Agreement's single service statement. The Client Agreement also includes a 'subject to a minimum fee of £100' qualification absent from the suitability report. The report does not alert the client to this floor, which could be material if the portfolio value is small relative to the charge calculation. The Client Agreement references 'Important Information about our Services' as the authoritative source for ongoing service detail, but this document is not provided. Where the suitability report and Client Agreement diverge on the scope of services to be provided for a stated charge, the client receives conflicting representations of what they are paying for. The absence of the controlling reference document prevents verification that the report's five-service list aligns with the firm's formal service commitment. Under COBS 6.1 and 9.2.2R, the charge and its supporting service must be clearly disclosed and consistent across communications to the client. This is a disclosure and consistency concern: the two primary documents on file do not align on what ongoing services are included in the 1% charge, and neither cites the same controlling definition.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven material benefits to justify the recommended switches: (1) Lower overall cost structure for Aviva vs Quilter (0.19% platform charge vs 0.27%, saving £208 pa); (2) Active monitoring and adjustment capability; (3) Greater choice—wider range of investment options than existing plans (which offer only 6 funds); (4) Online platform access for fund performance monitoring and charge transparency; (5) More flexible retirement options and tax-efficient withdrawal strategies; (6) Lower platform charges for Aviva vs True Potential (0.21% vs 0.40%, saving £88.68 pa); (7) Lower fund charges for Aviva (0.20% vs True Potential's 0.58%, saving £177.35 pa). The report states 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' and that benefits justify the switch. The report also states 'By moving to the recommended scheme, I will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust your investments as required'. No initial advice fee is reconciled against these claimed savings in the narrative.
    File:     Aviva T&Cs [P1] confirm that Pension Portfolio offers 'a range of insured Funds' and 'all the collective investment Funds' with adviser ability to move between options as needs change, and access to 'a range of income options' at retirement. Key Features Document [P6] and T&Cs [P1] confirm online access and flexible retirement options. True Potential factsheets [P19, P20] and Nest fund factsheets [P5, P14, P16, P17] are in evidence but neither True Potential nor Nest materials explicitly disclose their fund menu sizes. Quilter materials are absent from the evidence pack. HSBC and Vanguard KIIDs [P4, P11, P13] confirm fund objectives and investment approach but do not address platform or retirement flexibility. The switching analysis documents [P3, P10] show charge comparisons but do not itemise True Potential's full fund range or confirm whether Nest and True Potential offer fewer funds than claimed. The charge figures cited in the report (0.19%, 0.40%, 0.58%, 0.21%, 0.20%) are supported in the switching analysis [P10]: Aviva Annual Management Charge 0.25% with 0.08% decrease = 0.17% (close to 0.19% stated); True Potential platform 0.40% appears in switching analysis [P2, P3]; Nest platform charge 0.3% appears in switching analysis; fund charges of 0.2154% weighted average for Aviva and 0.58% for True Potential align with stated figures [P10]. However, the report does not disclose the initial advice fee (£9,838.36 or 14.49% on transfer value, per canonical model inconsistency) in the narrative cost comparison, nor does it show this fee offset against the claimed ongoing savings. The claimed savings are calculated on current values and do not account for the initial fee drag or payback period.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'lower overall cost structure' and savings of £208–£177 pa, but does not disclose or reconcile the initial advice fee of £9,838.36 against these claimed savings in the cost comparison narrative.
      - The report states Nest 'offers only 6 funds' but evidence confirms Nest's growth funds include at least the Higher Risk Fund with complex underlying holdings across multiple asset classes [P5, P14, P16]; no evidence confirms Nest's total fund count or that 6-fund constraint is accurate or meaningful compared to Aviva's offering.
      - The report states True Potential offers fewer investment options, but True Potential factsheets [P19, P20] are not explicit about fund count; the comparison does not quantify True Potential's fund range or explain why Aviva's offering is materially broader.
      - The report cites platform charge savings of £88.68 pa (0.19% difference on £46,672 current value) but does not disclose or account for the £1,400 'day one effect of transfer' cost shown in the switching analysis [P3], which represents a capital loss on switch.
      - The report claims flexible retirement options as a benefit but does not evidence that True Potential or Nest lack equivalent flexibility; Aviva T&Cs [P1, P6] and KFD [P6] confirm Aviva's flexibility, but comparable evidence for existing providers is absent from the file.
      - The report asserts 'active monitoring and adjustment capability' as a benefit but provides no evidence that True Potential or Nest restrict adviser adjustments or monitoring; this benefit may reflect a change in service proposition (ongoing adviser charge) rather than a platform or scheme feature unavailable under existing arrangements.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quilter scheme charge structure and fund range—the report claims Aviva is cheaper than Quilter (0.19% vs 0.27%) but Quilter documentation is not in the evidence pack, so the comparison cannot be verified., Quantification of fund count for Nest (report states 6, but only one fund factsheet provided), True Potential (fund count not specified in available factsheets), and Aviva (fund count not specified in Key Features or T&Cs)., Evidence that True Potential and Nest restrict adviser monitoring or adjustment capability, or that their retirement options are materially less flexible than Aviva's., Initial advice fee disclosure and payback analysis—the report does not disclose the £9,838.36 initial fee in the narrative cost justification or calculate the net benefit after fee amortisation., True Potential and Nest's service and monitoring proposition—whether ongoing adviser charges were present under existing arrangements and, if so, on what basis the 'active monitoring' benefit justifies incremental cost., Day-one transfer costs (£1,400 for True Potential, £638 for Nest [P3, P10]) and their offset against claimed ongoing savings—the payback periods are not calculated.
    The requirement asks whether benefits are supported by and consistent with evidence where higher costs arise. The comparison cannot be performed in full because: (1) Quilter evidence is absent, preventing verification of the claimed 0.19% vs 0.27% saving. (2) The initial advice fee of £9,838.36 is not reconciled in the narrative; it is a higher cost not explained or offset against claimed benefits in the cost justification section. The canonical model shows this fee is 14.49% of transfer value, not the stated 3%, but the report's narrative does not disclose the fee at all. (3) The report claims lower ongoing costs but does not calculate or disclose the net benefit timeline; day-one transfer losses of £638–£1,400 are documented in switching analyses [P3, P10] but not addressed in the narrative cost comparison. (4) The 'active monitoring' and 'flexible retirement' benefits are stated but evidence does not distinguish them from features already present in True Potential and Nest; Aviva T&Cs and KFD [P1, P6] confirm these features exist at Aviva, but comparative evidence for existing schemes is absent or insufficient. (5) Fund choice is claimed as a benefit (Nest stated as '6 funds' vs Aviva's wider range), but the report does not quantify Aviva's offering or evidence the 6-fund constraint for Nest; only one Nest fund factsheet is in the pack [P5, P14, P16], which lists extensive underlying holdings but does not establish total fund count or confirm the fund menu is smaller than alternatives. (6) The charge figures themselves (0.19%, 0.40%, 0.58%, 0.21%, 0.20%) are supported in the switching analyses [P10] and reconcile with stated transfer values, so those calculations are arithmetically sound on their stated basis. (7) However, the bases differ: the report cites savings on current values (£88.68 on £46,672 True Potential fund; £177.35 on £46,672) but does not disclose or amortise the £9,838.36 initial fee against these savings. On a £46,672 fund, £88.68 pa represents 0.19% annual benefit; the fee of £9,838.36 would require 111 years of that benefit to recover. This is a material non-disclosure of cost impact in the value narrative. Under the false-positive guard 'Consolidation convenience is a real benefit but not a financial one', the 'active monitoring' benefit may reflect a service change rather than a financial advantage, and should be weighted as stated rather than as a return. However, the guard does not excuse failure to disclose the initial fee in the cost justification or to reconcile it against savings. (8) The report's narrative focuses on benefits (choice, flexibility, lower platform/fund charges) and does not address the £9,838.36 initial fee or calculate net payback. This is a disclosure shortfall: a material cost layer is silently omitted from the cost comparison narrative, even though it is calculated in the canonical model. (9) Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that materially affect this assessment. The requirement stands on the consistency and completeness of cost and benefit disclosure.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states that the proposed Aviva plan requires outperformance of 1.23% per annum to age 89 compared to the current Nest plan, based on an annual growth rate of 2.94%. The report discloses Reduction in Yield (RIY) figures of 1.5%, 1.5%, and 1.6% across different growth scenarios. The report projects a fund value of £106,000 in real terms at age 89 under 2.94% growth. The report presents Critical Yield rates of 0.41%, 3.37%, and 6.32% (+0.41%, +0.43%, +0.44%) for the Aviva plan under the three growth assumptions. Fund charges are stated as 0.19% and 0.20% with a total of 0.20%. The report asserts that adviser remuneration is via policy (initial and ongoing), resulting in a comparison showing the Aviva plan is more expensive than the True Potential plan (allowing 0.01% underperformance) but more expensive than the Nest plan (requiring 1.23% outperformance).
    File:     Provider illustrations [P1, P6, P7, P12] present detailed switching analysis showing multiple cost scenarios. In the scenario excluding existing OAC [P1], RIY figures match at 1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6%, and projected fund values at 2.94% growth are £73,200 (Nest analysis) and £106,000 (True Potential analysis, matching report). Critical Yield rates in P1 for Nest switching show 0.5% (+0.5%), 3.46% (+0.52%), 6.41% (+0.53%), not matching the report's 0.41%, 3.37%, 6.32%. In the scenario including existing OAC [P12], Critical Yield rates for Nest show 0.41% (+0.41%), 3.37% (+0.43%), 6.32% (+0.44%), matching the report exactly. Fund charges in P15 show HSBC 0.19% and Vanguard 0.20% (matching report), with weighted average 0.20%. Day-one transfer effects show Nest value reduction from £21,272.64 to £20,634.46 (-£638). The illustrations consistently show that switching to Aviva results in lower projected fund values across all growth scenarios (£73,200 vs £85,961 at 2.94% for Nest; £106,000 vs £134,612 at 2.94% for True Potential aggregate) despite the outperformance requirement being disclosed.
    Discrepancies:
      - Critical Yield figures in report (0.41%, 3.37%, 6.32%) match P12 (including existing OAC) but do not match P1 (excluding existing OAC, which shows 0.5%, 3.46%, 6.41%) — report does not disclose which cost scenario these figures derive from or that alternative scenarios exist
      - Report projects £106,000 at age 89 at 2.94% growth in real terms, but does not reconcile this against the day-one loss of £638 on the Nest transfer or explain whether this is sufficient to offset the initial charge impact
      - Report states 1.5% RIY but multiple illustrations show this applied across different charging bases (with/without existing adviser charges), creating ambiguity about which charging structure the 1.23% outperformance requirement is based upon
      - Report discloses required outperformance of 1.23% pa vs Nest but the provider illustrations show equivalent figures only when including existing OAC (0.41% vs True Potential); the comparison is asymmetric and does not clarify why True Potential (0.01% underperformance permitted) and Nest (1.23% outperformance required) are treated differently despite both being existing arrangements
      - Fund charges stated in report as 0.20% total, but fund level breakdown (0.19% and 0.20%) implies weighted average of 0.2050%, not exactly 0.20% — immaterial but indicates rounding or aggregation not fully explained
      - Report does not disclose the RIY methodology or what charges are included in the 1.5% figure, nor does it explain the distinction between 1.5% RIY and 0.09% variance in the charge impact between scenarios (RIY 1.5% vs effect of 0.09% difference)
    The comparison can be performed because the required outperformance (1.23% pa) is disclosed alongside growth assumptions and projected fund values. However, the report's disclosure is materially incomplete and creates discrepancies in interpretation. First, the Critical Yield figures (0.41%, 3.37%, 6.32%) are taken from illustrations that include existing adviser ongoing charges (P12), but the report does not state this explicitly or disclose that alternative calculations excluding these charges exist (P1 shows 0.5%, 3.46%, 6.41%). This is a presentation inconsistency: the report relies on one cost scenario without acknowledging the basis. Second, the 1.23% outperformance requirement is stated as necessary to match the Nest plan at 2.94% growth, yet the provider illustration (P1, excluding existing OAC) shows this comparison at the day-one transfer date only, not on a full portfolio basis over the accumulation period. The report does not address the day-one loss (£638 on Nest transfer) or explain whether the projected £106,000 is net of all charges or whether the 1.23% threshold already accounts for this loss. Third, the RIY figures (1.5%, 1.5%, 1.6%) are presented as applying to the 'Reduction in Yield' calculation but the report does not state whether these are being applied to the existing arrangement value, the recommended arrangement value, or the difference. The arithmetic check confirms that a 1.23% annual outperformance requirement is stated in P15 and illustrated in P1, but P12's inclusion of existing adviser charges produces the Critical Yield rates the report relies on without disclosure of this choice. Fourth, the report permits True Potential to underperform by 0.01% but requires Nest to outperform by 1.23% — a material asymmetry unexplained in the report. This suggests different adviser charge treatments across the two existing arrangements, but the report does not disclose this. The guard regarding 'required outperformance under about 0.5% per annum is within the noise of assumptions' does not apply here because 1.23% is materially above that threshold and is the linchpin of the suitability reasoning. The report states the Aviva plan is 'more expensive than your current Nest plan' but does not quantify the cumulative cost impact over the projection period, only the annual rate of outperformance needed. The projected values themselves show Aviva as lower (£106,000 vs £134,612.81 for True Potential aggregate at 2.94% on an all-arrangements basis), confirming higher relative costs reduce absolute outcomes. The report's explanation of benefits in exchange for costs is absent: no explanation is given for why a plan requiring 1.23% annual outperformance is recommended, nor are the benefits of consolidation, fund choice, or platform features weighed against this cost penalty. The charge comparison lacks aggregation and reconciliation: the report does not disclose total ongoing charges (fund + platform + advice) as a single figure, nor does it show cumulative cost over the projection term.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report states charges increase from 0.30% to 0.39% (0.09% increase); the comparison documents show True Potential current charge is 0.98% including ongoing advice charge, not 0.30% platform charge alone [P16]. Report presents the charge increase as 'relatively small' at £19.15; this figure applies only to the Nest fund (£21,272.64 at 0.09%), not to the overall transfer value of £67,945.44. Report states recommended funds underperformed against Nest (45.91% vs 47.84%); the underlying basis for this comparison is not explained—it is unclear whether these are the specific recommended funds or broader fund category comparisons. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies four disadvantages: (1) GreaterFlexibilityAdvantage - describes the receiving Aviva scheme as providing more flexible retirement options and active monitoring; (2) ChargeIncrease - states annual charges increase from 0.30% to 0.39%, a £19.15 increase per annum based on current values; (3) MarketTiming - warns that if the stock market rises between realising existing funds and purchasing new funds, the client could be financially disadvantaged; (4) HigherCharges - states the Aviva plan is 0.09% more expensive than the current Nest plan, £19.15 additional cost per annum; (5) WeakerPerformance - notes recommended funds have underperformed against Nest Higher Risk Fund (45.91% versus 47.84% cumulative performance); (6) BalancedApproach - characterises the recommended funds as more balanced and in line with risk tolerance but having lagged behind higher-risk Nest funds. The report also lists three limitations: (1) CashflowLimitation - projections do not consider potential long-term care costs in later life; (2) ProjectionLimitation - projections are based on assumptions and small variances can result in marked differences; (3) ExcludedCosts - charges exclude transaction costs of underlying funds which are variable. The report states no guarantee that returns will exceed existing arrangements [P15].
    File:     Product literature (Aviva KFD) identifies multiple material disadvantages and risks: transferring from Defined Benefit or older schemes may result in loss of valuable rights or guaranteed income, and growth is dependent on future investment returns which can go down as well as up [P2]. Income drawdown risks include that income may impact means-tested benefits, investments must grow to compensate for withdrawals, high income withdrawals will deplete the fund and leave less for dependants or annuity purchase, and tax position may not match exactly [P3]. Market timing risks are identified: the price of investments may fall or rise between instruction and transaction [P3]. Overseas investment risks include exchange rate exposure [P3]. Property investment risks include illiquidity and valuation uncertainty [P3]. Small company investments may take longer to sell [P3]. The KFD notes that the plan is not a stakeholder plan and does not meet government minimum standards, and stakeholder schemes may meet requirements at least as well [P1]. Comparison documents (SAP) show the Nest fund (risk 8) has outperformed the recommended Aviva funds over 5 years (47.84% cumulative versus the recommended portfolio's implicit lower performance), and switching carries a material reduction in yield: at the medium growth assumption (2.94%), the critical yield required from Aviva is 4.17% compared to the existing scheme's growth assumption, a difference of 1.23% [P16]. The comparison documents also show a day-one capital loss on the switch: transferring £46,672.80 from True Potential results in only £45,272.62 invested in Aviva, a loss of £1,400 [P16]. The projected fund value shows a -31.35% reduction in the fund if moved to Aviva at all growth rate assumptions [P16]. Tax treatment disclosure [P12] states that tax statements are based on current law and subject to change.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states charges increase from 0.30% to 0.39% (0.09% increase); the comparison documents show True Potential current charge is 0.98% including ongoing advice charge, not 0.30% platform charge alone [P16]
      - Report presents the charge increase as 'relatively small' at £19.15; this figure applies only to the Nest fund (£21,272.64 at 0.09%), not to the overall transfer value of £67,945.44
      - Report states recommended funds underperformed against Nest (45.91% vs 47.84%); the underlying basis for this comparison is not explained—it is unclear whether these are the specific recommended funds or broader fund category comparisons
      - Report does not disclose the day-one capital loss of £1,400 on transfer from True Potential (£46,672.80 to £45,272.62) identified in the comparison document [P16]
      - Report does not disclose the material reduction in yield: at medium growth assumption (2.94%), Aviva requires 4.17% annual return versus the existing scheme, a difference of 1.23% or £19.15 annually on £1,556.91 implied fund value [P16]
      - Report does not disclose the -31.35% reduction in projected fund values at all growth rate scenarios shown in the comparison [P16]
      - Report characterises the Aviva scheme as providing 'greater flexibility' and 'active monitoring' as advantages; product literature emphasises that this is an advised platform and restrictions apply to non-advised clients [P7], and active monitoring is not a product feature but an adviser service
      - Report does not identify the loss of Defined Benefit protections or guaranteed income that may apply to the Quilter arrangement, though no DB status is explicitly stated for existing plans in the evidence
      - Report does not disclose that Aviva is not a stakeholder plan whereas the KFD states stakeholder schemes may meet client requirements at least as well [P1]
      - Report does not clearly distinguish between platform charges (0.40% True Potential, 0.30% Nest per internal inconsistencies noted) and total ongoing charges including advice; the comparison basis is therefore unclear
    The report presents a mixture of genuine disadvantages and framing issues. The ChargeIncrease and HigherCharges disadvantages are stated but obscure the underlying issue: the 0.30% figure used appears to refer to the Nest fund alone, not the blended charge across all existing arrangements (0.39% blended across all three at £327,946). The comparison is therefore not like-for-like. More materially, the report omits three significant disadvantages evident from the file: (1) the day-one capital loss of £1,400 on the True Potential transfer, which is a direct measurable consumer harm; (2) the critical yield differential of 1.23% at medium growth scenarios, meaning the recommended scheme must outperform by this amount annually to match existing projections—a substantial hurdle not explained to the client; (3) the -31.35% reduction in projected fund values across all scenarios, which is among the most material disadvantages a pension switch can carry and is not mentioned anywhere in the report. The WeakerPerformance disadvantage is presented but immediately followed by BalancedApproach attempting to reframe it positively by reference to 'risk-adjusted performance', without explaining what this means or whether it is trade-off the client has accepted. The report characterises flexibility and active monitoring as client-specific advantages within the disadvantages section, which is a framing choice that prioritises benefits over harms. The product literature risks (income drawdown impact on means-tested benefits, depletion risk on high withdrawals, taxation uncertainty) are generic but material and do not appear in the report. The report does state that transaction costs are excluded from charges and that there is no guarantee returns will exceed existing arrangements, which are valuable limitations. However, the critical gap is that the report never quantifies the financial impact of switching: the client sees neither the £1,400 day-one loss, nor the 1.23% annual yield requirement, nor the -31.35% projected fund reduction. These are precisely the loss scenarios and potential consumer harms the requirement demands be 'fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently' explained. The vulnerability overlay: the client has recently undergone divorce proceedings and is establishing financial independence (per meeting notes [P19]). The absence of quantified switching costs, clear loss scenarios, and explanation of the critical yield differential may materially impair her ability to understand the financial consequences of this recommendation, particularly where she is navigating new financial circumstances. The report's framing of disadvantages mixed with benefits in a single section, without clear demarcation of what she is giving up versus what she is gaining, compounds this risk.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states product-specific risks including: (1) access delay of 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes; (2) cancellation limitation that previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back. Investment-specific risks include: (1) timing risk from stock market movements between realising existing funds and purchasing new funds; (2) inflation risk at 6% scenario leading to asset depletion at age 81; (3) performance risk with no guarantee new investment will outperform existing arrangement; (4) market timing and market risk from stock market fluctuations; (5) liquidity delay of 10-15 working days; (6) capital risk that unit prices and pension fund value can fall with no guarantee of original capital return; (7) past performance not guarantee of future returns; (8) switch between providers does not guarantee greater pension fund.
    File:     Product literature (KIIDS and fact sheets for Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio) discloses: counterparty risk, liquidity risk, credit risk, inflation risk, use of derivatives, investment fund risk, performance risk, emerging market risk, counterparty risk, derivative risk, operational risk, exchange rate risk. Aviva Pension KFD discloses risks including small company investments taking longer to sell, overseas investment currency effects, property investment liquidity, trading practice price fluctuation risk, fixed term product access restrictions, income drawdown risks including impact on means-tested benefits and fund depletion risk. True Potential fund factsheets disclose operational risk, credit risk, exchange rate risk, liquidity risk. The Aviva Pension KFD also states that if transferring from Defined Benefit scheme, valuable rights may be lost and growth is defined by future investment returns which can go down as well as up.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not specifically address counterparty risk, which is disclosed in both Vanguard and HSBC KIIDS as a material risk not fully captured by the Risk and Reward Indicator.
      - The report does not specifically address credit risk, which is disclosed in HSBC KIIDS, True Potential factsheets, and Aviva Pension KFD.
      - The report does not address derivative risk or the use of derivatives, which is disclosed in Vanguard KIIDS as potentially increasing or reducing exposure to underlying assets and resulting in greater fluctuations.
      - The report does not address operational risk, which is disclosed in multiple fund documents (True Potential and HSBC).
      - The report does not address exchange rate risk, which is disclosed in Vanguard KIIDS, HSBC KIIDS, and True Potential factsheets as affecting investments denominated in non-GBP currencies.
      - The report does not address the specific risk of investment in other funds (Investment Fund Risk), disclosed in True Potential and Vanguard literature as involving third-party governance risks.
      - The report does not address emerging market risk, disclosed in Vanguard KIIDS and True Potential factsheets as involving greater political and economic instability risks.
      - The report does not address currency/exchange rate movements specifically, though Vanguard and HSBC literature disclose this as a material risk affecting fund value.
      - The report does not address the risk that the Risk and Reward Indicator is based on historical data and may not be reliable for future risk profile, stated in Vanguard KIIDS.
      - The report does not address potential loss of valuable benefits when transferring from Defined Benefit pensions, disclosed in Aviva Pension KFD.
      - The report does not address the risk that property investments may take time to sell and value depends on valuer's opinion, disclosed in Aviva Pension KFD.
      - The report does not address the risk of means-tested benefits impact from income drawdown, disclosed in Aviva Pension KFD.
      - The report does not address fund concentration risk in specific sectors, countries, or companies, disclosed in Vanguard KIIDS.
      - The report does not address the specific risks of the HSBC fund's active management approach or potential underperformance versus strategic asset allocation.
    The comparison can be performed as product literature is available for both recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio) and the existing Nest arrangement. The report discloses generic investment risks (capital at risk, market timing, past performance) and some product-specific risks (access delay, cancellation limitation, inflation stress test scenario). However, the product literature for the recommended funds and Aviva platform discloses numerous material risks that are not addressed in the report. These include counterparty risk, credit risk, derivative risk, operational risk, exchange rate risk, investment fund risk, emerging market risk, currency risk, property investment liquidity risk, means-tested benefits impact, and the limitation of the Risk and Reward Indicator itself. The report's risk disclosure relies significantly on general boilerplate warnings (capital at risk, past performance, no guarantee of returns) rather than product-specific explanations tied to the client's circumstances or the particular funds recommended. The inflation stress test (6% scenario depleting assets at age 81) is provided as a loss scenario but lacks prominence and is buried in the 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' section rather than integrated into the main risk and disadvantages discussion. The report does not explain how the recommended funds' risk characteristics differ from the existing arrangements being switched from, particularly regarding the shift from Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk level 8) to recommended funds at risk level 7, or how the specific risks of each recommended fund apply to this client's objectives. The critical risk of potential loss of Defined Benefit protections (relevant given the Quilter arrangement is referenced as existing) is not addressed. The guard regarding deferral to product literature applies here: the report states risks are 'detailed in the literature provided' but does not extract, explain or relate them to the client's specific circumstances, leaving material product-specific risks undisclosed in the suitability report itself.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states five categories of tax consequences: (1) Death benefits are tax-free if death occurs before age 75, with beneficiaries able to receive lump sum, drawdown income or purchase dependents annuity; if death occurs after age 75, same options available but taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate. (2) All payments subject to Aviva's discretion; where nominees withdraw before age 75 it is tax-free, after age 75 subject to nominee's marginal rate. (3) Each withdrawal from flexi-access drawdown is taxed at the client's marginal rate of Income Tax. (4) Personal contributions benefit from tax relief — net contribution of £80 becomes £100 gross. (5) Pensions currently outside estate for IHT purposes, but from 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits will be included in taxable estate for IHT, with exceptions for spouse/civil partner transfers, dependents' scheme pensions, and death-in-service benefits. The report also states the client currently has no IHT liability as assets are below the £500,000 allowance, but receipt of the £350,000 inheritance will take the client over the limit, and the adviser will monitor the IHT position at future reviews. The report cites the Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 and states contributions are limited by the Annual Allowance of £60,000 per annum, with high earners having a lower allowance, and Money Purchase Annual Allowance restrictions to £10,000 where flexi-access has been triggered.
    File:     The Aviva Pension KFD [P6] confirms that if the client dies before age 75, benefits are normally paid tax-free to nominated beneficiaries at trustees' discretion (unless a trust deed applies), with the Lump Sum & Death Benefit Allowance limiting tax-free benefits; if beneficiary exceeds this allowance or the lump sum is paid more than two years after death, income tax applies. If death occurs after age 75, the full remaining pension fund can be paid to beneficiaries at trustees' discretion, taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate, with lump sum payments subject to tax charge based on beneficiary's income tax rate or 45% if paid to trust. The Aviva T&Cs [P4] state that lump sum death benefits may be subject to tax charges levied by HMRC, and if commercial property is not sold within two years of death, lump sums are taxable even if death before age 75. [P13] confirms tax relief on contributions and notes the annual allowance set by HMRC, with adviser able to give details of current maximums. The main suitability report [P1] confirms the client currently has no IHT liability (assets below £500,000 allowance), but notes from 6 April 2027 most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits will be included in taxable estate for IHT, with exceptions for spouse/civil partner, dependents' scheme pensions, and death-in-service benefits. The report states the client will monitor IHT position at future reviews. [P8] details the Lump Sum and Death Benefit Allowance of £268,275, Annual Allowance of £60,000 per annum (higher allowances possible with protection, lower for high earners), carry-forward of up to 3 years' unused allowances, and Money Purchase Annual Allowance restriction to £10,000 where flexible access triggered, with no carry-forward allowed.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the Lump Sum Allowance as £268,275 [P8 from report], but [P8] also states this is the 'lump sum and death benefit allowance' which limits tax-free benefits; the report's phrasing 'Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 for people with no form of lifetime allowance protection' in recommendations section [at page 24] could be read as narrower than the full allowance being £268,275.
      - The report states the client has 'no Inheritance Tax liability' currently [P1] with assets below £500,000 allowance, but does not quantify what the allowance is or explain whether the £500,000 figure is a total or an individual allocation; [P1] states the NRB is £325,000 each, RNRB is £175,000 (total potential £1m on second death), but the £500,000 threshold used to determine current liability is not explicitly connected to these figures.
      - The report asserts the client 'will continue to monitor' IHT position at future reviews [P1], but does not state when or how this monitoring will occur, or what triggers a review of the recommendation in light of the inherited £350,000.
      - The report states contributions are limited by Annual Allowance of £60,000 per annum [P8] and that the client 'have not yet triggered the MPAA' and 'do not have a lower annual allowance' [P8], but does not state the client's actual salary or earnings, making it impossible to verify whether the £60,000 cap or a 100% salary test applies.
    The tax consequences stated in the report are substantially consistent with the evidence in the case file. The Aviva T&Cs and KFD corroborate the death benefit taxation rules (tax-free before age 75 unless lump sum exceeds allowance or paid after two years; marginal rate after age 75), the requirement for withdrawal income to be taxed at marginal rate, and the tax relief on contributions. The IHT change from 6 April 2027 is correctly described in both the report and [P1], with appropriate exceptions noted. The Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 is confirmed in [P8]. However, several discrepancies merit examination: First, the report's statement of the £500,000 threshold for current IHT liability is not clearly reconciled with the NRB (£325,000 each) and RNRB (£175,000) figures provided in the same section; the £500,000 appears to be a combined or illustrative figure for the couple but is presented without context. This is a disclosure shortfall rather than an error: the adviser should have clarified whether this was individual, household, or joint planning context. Second, the client's actual income or salary is not disclosed in the extracted model, so the claim that the £60,000 Annual Allowance applies (rather than a reduced allowance for high earners, which is noted as a possibility) cannot be independently verified against client circumstances. This is an omission from the canonical model rather than a contradiction in the file. Third, the report notes future monitoring of IHT position but does not specify triggers, timeline or communication plan; this is a process gap rather than a factual inconsistency with the evidence. Fourth, the report correctly identifies that the client has not triggered the Money Purchase Annual Allowance, which constrains the £10,000 limit to specific circumstances; the evidence supports this conditional framing. The tax relief example (£80 net = £100 gross) is a standard illustration compliant with the 25% basic rate uplift for non-taxpayers and applies correctly at basic rate; no client-specific tax band is stated, which is consistent with the guard that generic legislative explanation need not state personal consequence. The recommendation involves a pension transfer and potentially contributions, triggering the need to disclose tax consequences, which the report does. The consequences stated are consistent with product literature and regulatory framework. No material tax consequence identified in the file is absent from the report, and no consequence stated by the report is contradicted by the evidence.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states that it has completed a stress test based on a market crash occurring at the time the client needed access to funds, showing that if the plan sustained a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8, assets would still last the client's lifetime. The report also conducted a stress test based on high inflation at 6%, showing that assets could be depleted at age 81. The report presents these scenarios under 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' section. The report states that the recommended funds are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk' and discloses that 'unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it.' The report notes 'Past performance is not a guarantee of future returns' and 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement.'
    File:     The Aviva Key Features Document [P3] states that pension values 'can go down as well as up' and clients 'may get back less than has been paid in,' and warns of risks including market volatility, liquidity constraints, currency exposure, and income drawdown risks. The product literature [P1] and [P3] discloses that values can fall, there is no guarantee of returns, and income drawdown may deplete the fund if investment growth does not compensate for withdrawals. The Aviva pension illustration [P2] states 'It is important to understand that all the above figures are to give you an idea of what you could receive. We can't predict how your fund will grow and what it will cost to buy an annuity when you retire.' The KIIDs [P6] present volatility and performance data over historical periods. The report's own stress test analysis [P15] shows that under a 6% inflation scenario, assets could be depleted at age 81, and under a 2007/8-style market crash, assets would last the client's lifetime. The performance comparison [P14] shows that the Nest Higher Risk Fund outperformed the recommended funds over 5 years (47.84% vs 45.91%), demonstrating that the client is giving up higher-performing equity exposure.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report identifies loss scenarios (market crash 2007/8 and 6% inflation stress tests) but presents them without quantified loss percentages or amounts, despite stating 'lossPercentage: null' and 'lossAmount: null' in the canonical model
      - The stress test results are presented qualitatively ('assets would still last your lifetime' and 'could be depleted at age 81') without showing the actual fund value at each scenario outcome
      - The report does not quantify what 'a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8' means in percentage terms or explain the severity of that specific market event for context
      - The report emphasizes that recommended funds remain within agreed risk 7, but does not explicitly highlight that this means accepting lower growth potential than the existing Nest fund (risk 8) which achieved 47.84% vs 45.91% over 5 years—this represents a material disadvantage of reduced growth that is not prominently disclosed
      - Generic risk warnings about drawdown ('unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise') appear in the report but are not connected to the client's specific situation, age 55, extended withdrawal period (to age 100), and relatively modest inheritance-dependent retirement income plan
      - The report does not address tax consequences specific to the recommended solution: clients are moving from existing pensions into a new vehicle with potentially different tax treatment at drawdown, and with the inherited funds creating an IHT exposure from 6 April 2027 onwards—these tax changes are not explained in the loss scenarios
      - The disadvantage of being subject to ongoing advice charges (0.50% stated in switch assessment) is not clearly presented as a cost the client bears for recommendations, compared to the benchmark investment pathway option which has 'potentially lower costs'
    The requirement tests whether loss scenarios and disadvantages are 'fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explained' and 'consistent with supporting evidence.' The report does present two loss scenarios: (1) a 2007/8-style market crash, and (2) a 6% inflation scenario. Both are real projections from the cashflow model. However, there are material gaps in how prominently and clearly these are presented. The market crash scenario is stated qualitatively without quantified loss amounts: the report says 'if your plan sustained a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8 your assets would still last your lifetime' but does not tell the client what that loss looks like in pounds or percentage terms. For context, the 2007/8 crisis saw equity market falls of 40-50%, but the report does not explain this. The inflation scenario is similarly presented as 'your assets could be depleted at age 81' without showing the fund value trajectory or depletion point in absolute terms. These scenarios appear in the Alternative Scenarios section [P15] with no emphasis or front-loading in the executive summary or recommendation. The guard states 'a stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one'—the report does label them as stress tests, satisfying that guard. However, a material disadvantage gap exists: the report recommends switching from a Nest fund (risk 8, 47.84% 5-year return) to recommended funds (risk 7, 45.91% 5-year return) but does not clearly present this as a disadvantage in the loss scenario section or in the recommendation prominence. The client is accepting lower growth to stay within risk tolerance, but this trade-off is buried in performance comparison text [P14], not highlighted in disadvantages. The report also delegates tax consequences to product literature and does not explain the specific tax position of the client moving to a new vehicle with IHT implications from April 2027. The vulnerability overlay does not apply materially here; the client has accounting and currency trading background [P10] and is not identified as vulnerable. The loss scenarios themselves are present and evidence-supported by cashflow modelling, but their presentation—scattered, unquantified, and not prominent—falls short of 'fair, clear, accurate and prominent' disclosure.
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P15)
        "I have completed a stress test based on a market crash occurring at the time you needed access to your funds. This showed that if your plan sustained a loss similar to the crash of 2007/8 your assets would still last your lifetime."
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P15)
        "With the recent cost of living crisis and inflation rates increasing above the government's target rate for inflation (2%), I also conducted a stress test based on a high inflation rate of 6%. The cashflow shows that should this high level of inflation continue at the assumed rate, your assets could be depleted at age 81."
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P14)
        "This shows that the recommended funds have outperformed your existing True Potential funds and underperformed against the Nest fund. This is to be expected given the level of risk for each plan, with the Nest funds with a higher equity content achieving greater growth."
      · Aviva Pension KFD.md [H_ProductDocumentation] (P3)
        "Remember that the value of your pension can go down as well as up and you may get back less than has been paid in. This may not be the case in your previous pension scheme."
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P20)
        "You should remember that unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it."
      · Suitability report 2026.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P20)
        "A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider."
      · Anness, M Aviva pension illustration 24.3.26.md [H_ProductDocumentation] (P2)

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that it has a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' that appears on page 8 and is not confined to an appendix. The canonical model indicates hasDedicatedSection=true, appearsOnlyInAppendix=false, and sectionHeading='Other Considerations of Switching'. The report states in the basis of advice section: 'This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives - whilst at the same time highlighting any potential disadvantages and risks, as well as any other implications in terms of costs, charges and taxation.' The report includes a section on 'Alternative Solutions' explaining why a stakeholder pension was not chosen despite lower charges, and discusses stress tests including inflation scenarios and market crash scenarios. Risk profile is stated as 7 on a 1-10 scale with explanation of Growth investor characteristics. Capacity for loss is stated as 'moderate' based on cashflow analysis and ability to work beyond age 60.
    File:     The evidence shows the report contains multiple risk and disadvantage-related passages distributed throughout. P1 contains definitions of Reduction in Yield, Critical Yield, and Effect of Switching, plus explanations of Personal Pension Plans and Flexi-access Drawdown benefits. P3 provides detailed charge tables and explains that transaction costs are variable and excluded from quoted figures, with disclosure that 'monetary terms shown here may differ from the illustration due to differences in calculation methods.' P4 discusses stress tests (6% inflation and 2007/8 market crash scenarios) and includes caveat that projections are based on assumptions and do not consider long-term care costs. P5 shows volatility analysis comparing existing funds to recommended funds, noting the recommended funds 'underperformed against the Nest fund' and that 'Past performance is not a guarantee of future returns.' P7 details attitude to risk as 7/10 Growth profile and capacity for loss as 'moderate' with qualification 'based on the current information we hold.' The passage identifies that inheritance assumption and employment flexibility provide capacity buffer. P8 contains the recommendation summary. No dedicated section explicitly titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' or equivalent appears in any passage heading provided. The risk and disadvantage content is distributed across multiple sections rather than consolidated in one place with that heading.
    Discrepancies:
      - Canonical model asserts dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 and sectionHeading shows that title, but no passage contains a section with that heading or demonstrates its page location.
      - Report asserts disadvantages and risks are highlighted in basis of advice, but no passage labeled as 'Other Considerations of Switching' section is retrievable in the evidence pack.
      - The canonical extraction claims the section appears on page 8 and is not appendix-only, but the retrieved passages do not show a consolidated section with that title at that location.
    The canonical model asserts the existence and page location of a dedicated section 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 that is not appendix-confined. However, the retrieved evidence passages do not include any section with that heading, and do not demonstrate that a consolidated disadvantages and risks section exists at that location. The evidence does show scattered risk disclosure: definitions of switching mechanics (P1), charge disclosure with caveats about transaction costs (P3), stress tests and limitations on projections (P4), performance comparison with underperformance admission (P5), and risk profile and capacity for loss discussion (P7). The distribution of risk content across multiple sections rather than consolidation in a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' suggests either: (1) the extraction identified a section heading that does not appear in the retrieved passages, or (2) the section exists but is not represented in the evidence pack. The report does address several material risks: inflation risk (6% stress test showing asset depletion at age 81), market crash risk (2007/8 scenario showing fund survival), underperformance of recommended funds versus Nest (5-year: 45.91% vs 47.84%), transaction cost variability, and dependence on working flexibility. However, these are presented across multiple sections rather than consolidated under a heading that would ensure prominence. The rule requires disadvantages and risks to be 'fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently' presented. Distributed disclosure across sections without a consolidated disadvantages heading may satisfy content requirements but raises questions about prominence as defined in COBS. The evidence also shows that some material aspects lack explicit disadvantage framing: the report states the recommended funds 'underperformed against the Nest fund' but frames this as expected due to risk level, rather than presenting the underperformance as a disadvantage of moving from Nest. The report notes the comparison shows 'strong risk-adjusted performance' for the recommended funds, which may obscure the absolute performance shortfall versus the existing higher-risk option. Capacity for loss is qualified as 'moderate' based on current information and cashflow, with the caveat 'based on the current information we hold' — a conditional statement that appropriately flags potential change. The stress test on long-term care costs is acknowledged as absent ('does not consider any potential long-term care costs which could occur in later life'). Overall, the evidence shows material risk content is present but its presentation structure cannot be verified against the claimed dedicated section heading 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8.
