  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for all three existing arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, and Nest). The canonical model extraction identified that /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent from the extracted model. The report provides death benefit comparisons: True Potential to Aviva shows £46,672.80 to £45,272.62 (day one effect -£1,400); Nest to Aviva shows £21,272.64 to £20,634.46. For the consolidated case across all three arrangements, existing total death benefit is £67,944.80 with Aviva providing £65,906.62, stated as 97% of existing. The report recommends transfer to Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) without explicitly addressing whether any safeguarded benefits exist in the existing arrangements or what guarantees, if any, are being foregone.
    File:     The passages confirm transfer charges are nil across providers. P2, P6, P9, P14 all provide day-one death benefit comparisons showing specific losses: True Potential £1,400.18 loss (3%), Nest £638.18 loss (3%), combined 3% reduction noted in P9. P17 (Aviva KFD) states 'We don't make a charge for transfers, although other providers may' but does not explicitly detail what safeguarded benefits or guarantees exist in either the existing or receiving arrangements. P12 and P15 (True Potential fund factsheets) describe investment risks and performance risks but contain no mention of safeguarded benefits, guarantees, protected rights, or annuity protections. P8 (Nest Higher Risk fund) contains no details on safeguarded benefits or guarantees. The passages do not evidence any analysis of whether the existing arrangements contain safeguarded benefits (such as protected pension commencement lumps, guaranteed annuity rates, protected rights, or similar). P9 states 'If the death benefit available after switch is lower than that provided by the existing arrangements, consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall' but does not confirm whether this consideration occurred or was documented.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no transfer charges without evidencing whether switching cost analysis compares total economic cost including any loss of valuable features, not merely transaction fees.
      - The report provides death benefit reductions (3% across all three switches) but does not disclose whether these represent the only valuable feature being lost or whether other safeguarded benefits exist in the existing arrangements.
      - The extraction flagged /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits as absent, indicating the report does not state what safeguarded benefits (if any) exist in Quilter, True Potential, or Nest arrangements being switched from.
      - No evidence appears in the file that the adviser examined or documented whether the existing arrangements contain protected rights, guaranteed annuity rates, enhanced death benefits, protected pension commencement lump sums, or other valuable features under pension protection rules.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Safeguarded benefits analysis: the report does not state whether any of the three existing arrangements contain protected rights (A-Day protection, pre-6 April 2006 accrual), guaranteed annuity rates, enhanced death benefit guarantees, protected pension commencement lump sums, or other valuable features under the Pension Protection Fund regime or contractual terms., Provider documentation from Quilter, True Potential, and Nest confirming presence or absence of safeguarded benefits in those arrangements., Analysis addressing whether the 3% death benefit reduction (£1,400 + £638 = £2,038 across the two arrangements being switched) is material in relation to total fund value and retirement objectives., Explicit consideration and documentation of whether the death benefit reduction constitutes an acceptable trade-off given the suitability of the overall switch, or whether it was flagged as a concern to the client.
    The comparison cannot be performed because the report does not establish what safeguarded benefits, if any, exist in the existing arrangements. The extraction's own metadata confirms /existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent, indicating the suitability report is silent on this dimension. The passages evidence only transfer charges (nil) and day-one death benefit reductions (3% across arrangements, totalling approximately £2,038). While P9 acknowledges that 'consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall' if death benefit declines, there is no evidence in the file that such consideration occurred or was documented. The passages do not confirm whether Quilter, True Potential, or Nest arrangements hold any protected pension rights, guaranteed annuity rates, enhanced guarantees, or other contractually safeguarded benefits. The absence of this analysis is itself the finding: a recommendation to switch three pension arrangements without documented examination of whether valuable features are being foregone does not meet the requirement to address safeguarded benefits and guarantees. The death benefit comparison provided shows loss but not analysis of whether that loss is justified or addressed with the client. The requirement is not satisfied by showing transfer charges are zero; it requires evidence that safeguarded benefits have been identified, their loss quantified, and justified as acceptable to the client's circumstances and objectives.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report considered five options: (1) Leave existing pensions untouched — rejected due to client preference and risk mismatch; (2) Switch funds in-situ — rejected because Nest offers limited fund choice within scheme; (3) Switch into another existing arrangement — considered but not discussed further; (4) Switch into employer scheme — rejected as client is self-employed; (5) Set up new pension plan — recommended as best option. The report states the recommendation provides lower costs (platform 0.21% vs 0.40% at True Potential, saving £88.68; fund charges 0.20% vs 0.58%, saving £177.35), wider fund range, ongoing monitoring, and flexible retirement options. Investment pathways rejected as less personalised than recommended solution.
    File:     Evidence shows: (a) Five alternatives were presented on pages 9 and 13 of the suitability report; (b) Performance comparison [P11, P12] shows Nest Higher Risk Fund achieved 5-year return of 47.84% (risk 8), True Potential 38.53% (risk 6), and recommended Risk 7 portfolio 45.91% — the recommendation underperformed Nest by 1.93 percentage points over 5 years; (c) Death benefit analysis [P23] shows existing arrangements provide £67,945 death benefit versus recommended Aviva £65,907 (97% of existing); (d) Projection analysis [P2, P6] with all charges included shows at 2.94% growth: Existing Schemes £121,807 vs Aviva £106,000 (reduction of 12.42%); (e) True Potential platform charges documented as 0.40% [P1], fund charges as 0.58% [P1]; (f) Nest fund composition [P10, P14] shows allocation to equities and alternative assets; (g) Recommended funds [P16, P18, P19] both rated 5-star by Morningstar; (h) No evidence that Nest scheme limits fund choice or that alternative funds within Nest were evaluated.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Nest Higher Risk Fund 'is not in line with the agreed risk level 7' (risk level 8), yet 5-year performance shows Nest at 47.84% return versus recommended portfolio at 45.91%, outperforming by 1.93 percentage points
      - Report claims new scheme offers 'wider range of investment options' than Nest, but provides no evidence comparing Nest's actual fund range against Aviva's, or that Nest fund choice was evaluated
      - Report states switching justified by cost savings (platform 0.19% saving, fund charge 0.38% saving), but all-charges-included projections [P6] show Aviva underperforming existing by 12.42% at 2.94% growth, contradicting net benefit claim
      - Death benefit reduced from £67,945 to £65,907 (£2,038 shortfall), acknowledged in file but report does not address client's acceptance of this loss or explain why it is acceptable
      - Report recommends fund charges at 0.20% for Aviva recommended portfolio but comparison shows True Potential at 0.58%; however, no analysis of why Aviva fund costs are lower or whether this reflects different fund quality or strategy
      - Report rejects Option 2 (in-situ switch at Nest) on basis of 'limited fund choice' but file contains no evidence of what funds are available within Nest scheme or what alternatives were considered there
      - Performance comparison shows recommended portfolio (Risk 7: 45.91%) underperformed both higher-risk Nest (Risk 8: 47.84%) and lower-risk benchmark (not clearly ranked), yet report describes recommended funds as having 'strong risk-adjusted performance within its category' without supporting calculation
    The comparison can be performed because the file contains performance data, charge data, and projection analyses. However, material discrepancies exist. First, on cost justification: the report asserts switching saves 0.19% platform charge and 0.38% fund charges (£266 combined annually on True Potential alone), but the all-charges-included SAP [P6] projects only 12.42% reduction in fund value at switch, implying charges alone do not justify the recommendation when projections are adjusted for inflation and time value. Second, on performance: the report rejects leaving Nest untouched based on risk mismatch (risk 8 vs agreed 7), but the recommendation (risk 7 at 45.91% over 5 years) underperformed Nest (risk 8 at 47.84%) by 1.93 percentage points. The report acknowledges this in [P12] stating 'underperformed against the Nest fund. This is to be expected given the level of risk for each plan' — but then fails to address whether a lower-cost fund at the same or higher risk would have been preferable, or whether accepting higher risk (Nest's risk 8) might have aligned with client's growth objective better than the recommended risk 7. The report's logic treats risk level adherence as paramount, but does not justify why risk 7 is preferable to risk 8 given client's stated retirement income objective of £25,000-£30,000 and 25+ year horizon. Third, on alternative fund consideration: the report rejects in-situ switching at Nest (Option 2) on grounds of limited choice, but file contains no evidence of what funds Nest actually offers, what alternatives were evaluated, or why switching providers was preferable to rebalancing within the scheme. Fourth, on death benefits: the report acknowledges loss (£2,038 or 3% reduction) but does not document client's informed consent to this loss or analyse whether other providers matched existing death benefit while achieving cost savings. The Critical Yield required [P2] of 3.46% at 2.94% assumed growth shows Aviva must outperform by 0.52% annually to break even on total return; file provides no forward-looking evidence this is achievable. Guard applied: Consolidation/provider change for its own sake is addressed — report cites specific reasons (cost, fund range, flexibility, ongoing monitoring). But evidence does not corroborate all claims equally. The file shows charges will be lower, performance history shows modest underperformance, and projections show 12.42% reduction in fund value when charges are included in all-charges scenarios. The recommendation appears to prioritize risk alignment and cost reduction over performance, but the performance underperformance by 1.93% over 5 years is material and unresolved.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three recommendations with higher costs justified by benefits: (1) Quilter switch: platform charges fall from 0.27% to 0.19% (£208 savings). (2) True Potential switch: platform charges fall from 0.40% to 0.21% (£88.68 savings) and fund charges fall from 0.58% to 0.20% (£177.35 savings). (3) Nest switch: annual charges increase by 0.09% (£19.15 per annum), justified by better risk alignment, wider fund range, professional management, active monitoring, online platform, flexible retirement options, and sophisticated approach. The report states for Nest: 'this small charge increase is justified by the benefits of wider fund range, professional management, improved retirement options, and alignment with your agreed risk profile.'
    File:     The evidence pack contains critical yield analysis and comparison tables. For Nest, the Aviva plan's Critical Yield shows it must outperform the existing plan by 1.23% per annum to age 89 to match value (P21). The charge analysis indicates Aviva is 0.09% more expensive annually (P17, P21). The evidence confirms no transfer charges (P1-P4). Regarding benefits: (1) Fund range—Aviva offers extensive Choice option with multiple asset classes (P10), vs Nest's limited fund offering mentioned in report. (2) Online platform—Aviva T&C confirm platform access and features (P9, P10). (3) Flexible retirement—Aviva T&C confirm flexible withdrawal options (P9). (4) Professional management—report claims active monitoring but passage P10 shows adviser must be involved for changes. (5) Risk alignment—Nest existing is risk 8, recommended is risk 7 per report; Critical Yield of 1.23% outperformance required indicates the charge increase creates a performance hurdle, not a benefit offset. No documented evidence shows the £19.15 annual increase will be recouped by stated benefits.
    Discrepancies:
      - Quilter switch justification incomplete: report claims £208 saving from platform charge reduction (0.27% to 0.19%) but does not disclose whether other charges apply or whether total charges fall. The comparison bases are not equivalent—platform charge alone is compared without stating whether investment fund charges differ.
      - True Potential switch: report claims £88.68 saving from platform charge reduction and £177.35 from fund charge reduction, totalling £266.03 annual saving. However, the report later states (P21) that 'the Aviva plan is more expensive than your current True Potential plan' based on 2.94% growth to age 89. This directly contradicts the claimed savings narrative; the Critical Yield analysis shows Aviva can underperform by only 0.01% per annum, implying costs are nearly equal or marginally higher when advice fees are included.
      - Nest switch cost-benefit contradiction: report claims 0.09% increase (£19.15) is 'justified' by benefits, but provides no quantification of those benefits against the cost. The Critical Yield of 1.23% outperformance required means the fund must grow 1.23% faster annually to offset the charge increase plus advice fees, yet no evidence shows the stated benefits (wider fund range, professional management, monitoring) deliver that level of alpha. The fund range benefit is stated generically; no client-specific fund requirement is documented in the evidence (no mention in Fact Find P20 of needing specific investment types unavailable on Nest).
      - True Potential switch: report states fund charges are 0.58% in existing vs 0.20% in recommended, representing £177.35 saving. However, the report's own Pension Switch Charges table (P17) shows True Potential existing annual recurring charge as 0.98% (platform 0.40% + investment management charge + advice charge 0.50%), not 0.58% fund charges alone. The comparison conflates fund AMC (0.58%) with total ongoing charge (0.98%). Without clarifying which charges are being compared, the benefit claim is unsupported.
    The requirement tests whether higher costs are justified by documented benefits. Three distinct findings emerge: (1) Quilter (£208 saving): This shows cost reduction, not higher cost justified by benefits. Not applicable to this requirement's limb. (2) True Potential (claimed £266.03 saving): The report claims combined savings but later contradicts itself via Critical Yield analysis (P21), which shows Aviva 'more expensive' when advice fees included, with only 0.01% tolerance for underperformance. The stated fund charge savings (£177.35) rest on comparing 0.58% to 0.20%, but the report's own charge table (P17) treats True Potential as having a total recurring charge of 0.98% (including advice), rendering the fund-only comparison misleading. This is a data inconsistency within the report's own figures. (3) Nest (£19.15 increase justified by benefits): The report claims benefits (wider range, monitoring, management, flexibility, platform) justify a 0.09% charge increase. However: The stated benefits are generic marketing claims unsupported by client-specific evidence. The Fact Find (P20) shows no documented client requirement for 'wider fund range' or specialist funds Nest lacks. The Aviva T&C (P10) show adviser involvement is required for rebalancing and changes, so 'active monitoring' is not a plan feature but a service to be paid for separately. The Critical Yield (1.23% per annum required outperformance to age 89) indicates the charge increase creates a material performance hurdle; no evidence documents that the cited benefits will overcome it. The benefits analysis is qualitative assertion without quantitative support. The report does state five alternative options were considered (P19), satisfying the alternatives requirement, but does not explain why the 'Lower risk fund within Nest' option was discounted, merely noting 'the new scheme offers access to a wider range.' Under the guard, 'better fund choice needs a fund count or a named client requirement'—neither is present in the evidence. The Nest comparison is therefore weakly justified: higher costs are asserted as acceptable based on unquantified, generic benefits without evidence they will be realised or measured against cost.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three main rationales for switching: (1) Lower costs—Aviva platform charges 0.19% vs Quilter 0.27% (£208 annual saving); True Potential platform 0.40% vs Aviva 0.21% (£88.68 saving); Nest fund charges 0.58% vs Aviva 0.20% (£177.35 saving); (2) Wider investment options and greater flexibility, allowing tailored strategy aligned to agreed risk profile of 7; (3) Ongoing monitoring and active adjustment by adviser; (4) Client preference—no ties to Quilter; (5) Comprehensive review conducted of charges, investment performance, fund range and retirement options; (6) Alternatives considered (5 options listed); (7) Benefits justified despite higher total costs when advice fees included—Aviva can underperform True Potential by 0.01% p.a. or must outperform Nest by 1.23% p.a. to match at age 89.
    File:     Evidence from passages shows: (1) Risk profile confirmed as level 7 (P3, P5, P7); (2) Meeting notes record administrative focus on pension transfer post-divorce, with discussion of risk profile assessment but NOT explicit discussion of investment preferences (P2, P5, P6); (3) Recommendation presentation scheduled for 30 March 2026 (P3, P7); (4) Performance data for proposed funds provided (P4: HSBC and Vanguard monthly returns Mar 2025–Feb 2026); (5) Critical Yield analysis in SAP documents (P12, P13, P17) showing required outperformance rates; (6) Charges comparison tables in SAP (P11, P12, P13) showing platform, fund and ongoing charges; (7) Death benefit comparison (P11); (8) No provider documentation from Quilter, True Potential or Nest confirming their feature sets in file; (9) Aviva KFD (P19) describes investment options available (insured funds, unit trusts, OEICs, equities, etc.) but does not compare to existing providers' ranges; (10) No document records explicit consideration of the 5 listed alternatives or reasons for discounting them; (11) Meeting summaries show risk profile discussed but no evidence of client stating 'no ties to Quilter'—report claims this was stated but file records show meeting was primarily administrative (P2, P5, P6); (12) No document corroborates that 'full review' of existing schemes' features was conducted or conclusions reached on relative merits.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states platform charge saving of £208 p.a. on Quilter basis (0.27% to 0.19%) but Aviva KFD does not specify platform charges separately; SAP document P18 states 'platform charges with Aviva are 0.21%' not 0.19%, contradicting the stated 0.19% in main recommendation
      - Report states True Potential platform charge is 0.40%, and Aviva is 0.21%, representing 0.19% saving or £88.68; SAP document P18 does corroborate these figures
      - Report claims Nest existing fund charges 0.58% vs Aviva 0.20%; SAP (P12) shows Nest charges as 0.30% not 0.58%; the 0.58% figure appears to be True Potential's charge, not Nest's
      - Report asserts client preference to leave Quilter was stated ('You mentioned during our meeting that you had no ties or preference to leave the funds with Quilter'); meeting notes (P2, P5, P6, P7) record administrative discussions and risk profile confirmation but do not contain any passage recording this client statement
      - Report states comprehensive review was conducted of 'charges, investment performance, accessible fund range, and retirement options available'; file contains no document recording analysis of Quilter's fund range, retirement options or performance comparison vs Aviva or Nest
      - Report lists 5 alternatives considered; no document in file records consideration of these alternatives or reasons for discounting Options 1–4
      - Report rationale references 'investment performance' comparison; SAP contains only forward projections at 0%, 2.94%, 5.88% growth, and one month of historical data (P4); no historical performance comparison between existing and proposed funds is present
      - Report claims ability to 'actively monitor and adjust' as benefit of switch; no adviser monitoring plan or service specification is provided in file
    The report identifies three main replacement rationales: lower costs, wider fund range/flexibility, and ongoing adviser oversight. Evidence partly corroborates and partly contradicts. (1) Charges: True Potential to Aviva saving is partially evidenced—SAP P18 confirms 0.40% vs 0.21%, yielding £88.68. However, the Quilter rationale contains an internal discrepancy: the report states 0.19% saving (0.27% to 0.19%), but P18 (Suitability report itself, [I] category) states Aviva platform charges as 0.21%, not 0.19%. This is a self-contradiction within the report. The Nest charges rationale is contradicted: report claims Nest charges 0.58% when SAP P12 shows 0.30% for Nest. The 0.58% figure appears misattributed—it is True Potential's charge. This is a material error affecting the credibility of the switching rationale for Nest. (2) Fund range: Report claims Nest offers only 6 funds and Aviva offers wider access; P19 lists Aviva's investment universe (insured funds, unit trusts, OEICs, equities, etc.) but no document confirms Nest's fund count or compares the two providers' ranges. The claim is unsupported by evidence. (3) Investment performance: Report references existing performance in context of alignment to risk profile; SAP P4 provides one month of recent performance data (Mar 2025–Feb 2026) for proposed funds. No historical performance data for existing schemes' funds is in file. Report provides no evidence that existing funds have underperformed or that proposed funds will perform better. (4) Client preference: Report quotes client as saying 'You mentioned during our meeting that you had no ties or preference to leave the funds with Quilter.' Meeting notes (P2, P5, P6, P7) focus on administrative processes, pension sharing post-divorce, and risk profile confirmation. None record this statement. The quote is asserted by the report but not evidenced in the file. Under rules, a claim the report makes for itself without corroboration is unsupported. (5) Comprehensive review: Report asserts a full review was conducted of existing schemes regarding charges, performance, fund range and retirement options. No document records this review or its findings. The alternatives framework (5 options) is listed but no document shows analysis of why each was rejected. (6) Critical Yield/justification of higher costs: SAP P24 and P13 provide Critical Yield analysis showing Aviva can underperform True Potential by 0.01% p.a. or must outperform Nest by 1.23% p.a. to match at age 89. This partly addresses the cost-benefit trade-off but rests on assumptions not detailed in the passages (growth rates, fees, time horizon). The fact that Nest requires 1.23% annual outperformance is material and significant—the fund must substantially outperform to justify the switch, yet the file contains no evidence of superior historical or projected performance of the Aviva funds vs Nest. (7) Vulnerability overlay: Meeting notes record client is post-divorce, currently employed, age 55, transitioning to unemployment. One meeting notes (P9, dated 21/07/2026, later than report date) record client left employment and is considering pension access. No indication of vulnerability characteristics (mental capacity, literacy, support needs) is recorded. No material effect on the replacement rationale assessment is evident from this information. The discrepancies in charges data (Quilter 0.19% vs 0.21%, Nest 0.30% vs 0.58%) and the absence of evidence for key claims (client preference, alternatives analysis, fund range comparison, performance justification, comprehensive review) indicate the replacement rationale is not fully supported and contains material errors.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that: (1) Quilter pension does not contain any safeguarded benefits; therefore no safeguarded features are lost. (2) Nest pension does not contain any safeguarded benefits; therefore no safeguarded features are lost. (3) True Potential pension does not contain any safeguarded benefits; therefore no safeguarded features are lost. (4) No charges for transfer and/or switching apply to any of the three arrangements (Quilter, True Potential, Nest). (5) An employer contribution is lost on the Nest transfer (employerContributionsLost = true for Nest only). (6) No employer contribution is lost on Quilter or True Potential transfers.
    File:     The case file contains: (P1) Fact Find stating client has no existing final salary or money purchase schemes at time of fact-find completion, with employer scheme shown as 'Not Applicable'. (P3, P8, P9) Nest fund documentation describing the Nest 2040 Retirement Fund as a default fund with growth-phase risk positioning, but no document states whether safeguarded benefits exist or do not exist. (P2, P6, P7, P11-16) True Potential fund factsheets (Allianz Balanced, Goldman Sachs Balanced, SEI Aggressive, Growth-Aligned Aggressive, Schroders Balanced, TrinityBridge Balanced) containing ongoing charges figures (OCF) and fund objectives, but no document confirming presence or absence of safeguarded benefits. (P22) Aviva Pension KFD states 'We don't make a charge for transfers, although other providers may.' This confirms Aviva's own transfer policy but does not confirm the transferring providers' (Quilter, True Potential, Nest) policies. (P20-21) Switching cost analysis document showing death benefit comparison and performance tables but no statement of exit penalties or switching charges for the ceding plans. (P4) Fact Find retirement goals noting client plans to retire at 60, expects £25,000-£30,000 annual pension, and returns to part-time work in 2027. No document confirms employer contributions are live on any plan or whether an employer scheme exists post-2027.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims Nest contains no safeguarded benefits; no document in the file confirms this (absence of evidence is not evidence of absence). (P3, P8, P9) contain Nest fund information but do not state presence or absence of safeguarded benefits or guarantees.
      - Report claims True Potential contains no safeguarded benefits; no document confirms this. (P2, P6, P7, P11-16) contain True Potential fund factsheets but do not address safeguarded benefits or guarantees.
      - Report claims Quilter contains no safeguarded benefits; no document is present for Quilter at all in the file to confirm this claim.
      - Report asserts no charges for transfer and/or switching on all three arrangements; (P22) Aviva KFD only confirms Aviva's own transfer policy ('We don't make a charge for transfers'), not the transferring providers' policies. No document confirms Quilter, True Potential, or Nest have no exit penalties or switching charges.
      - Report states employer contribution is lost on Nest transfer; (P4) Fact Find notes client 'is going to return to work at the start of 2027 with the view to only just do part time' and employer scheme shown as 'Not Applicable'. The timing of the transfer relative to the return-to-work date in 2027 is unclear. No document confirms whether contributions are active at transfer date or whether the client will rejoin an employer scheme.
      - Report states no employer contribution is lost on Quilter or True Potential; (P1, P4) confirm no employer scheme is held at fact-find date, so this is consistent. However, True Potential transfer value £46,672.80 vs. current value £46,672.80 (identical) and Nest transfer value £21,272.64 vs. current value £21,272.64 (identical) suggests only these two are being transferred; Quilter's current value £260,000 has no corresponding transfer value listed, creating ambiguity about whether Quilter is being transferred or retained.
      - The extraction metadata notes that '/existingArrangements/0/safeguardedBenefits' is 'NotPresentInDocument', confirming the report does not state safeguarded benefits for any plan — it only asserts their absence.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Provider documentation from Quilter, True Potential, and Nest confirming whether safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or protected rights exist in the client's specific plans., Written confirmation from Quilter, True Potential, and Nest of exit penalties, transfer charges, or switching costs applicable to the client's arrangements., Clarification of Quilter's status: is the £260,000 current value plan being transferred or retained? No transfer value is stated for Quilter., Confirmation of whether the employer contribution on Nest (if any is active) continues beyond the client's return-to-work date in 2027, or whether it ceases on transfer., Terms and conditions or product literature for Quilter and True Potential plans confirming the presence or absence of valuable features, guarantees, or protected rights.
    The requirement asks whether the recommendation to replace, switch, or transfer is consistent with and supported by evidence. The specific limb [G9.10] addresses 'material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation.' The report asserts that no safeguarded benefits are lost on any of the three plans and that no transfer charges apply. However, the comparison cannot be performed because: (1) No provider documentation exists in the file confirming that the plans contain no safeguarded benefits. The report infers the absence of safeguarded benefits but provides no corroborating evidence from provider terms, factsheets, or confirmations. (2) No documents confirm transfer charges or exit penalties for the transferring providers. The Aviva KFD confirms Aviva's own transfer policy but not that of Quilter, True Potential, or Nest. (3) Quilter's status is ambiguous: it has a current value of £260,000 but no transfer value is listed, making it unclear whether it is being transferred or retained. (4) The employer contribution claim regarding Nest is unsupported: (P4) shows the employer scheme is 'Not Applicable' at fact-find, and while the report states an employer contribution is lost on Nest, no document confirms whether contributions are active at transfer date or whether an active scheme exists. The extraction metadata confirms that safeguarded benefits fields are 'NotPresentInDocument,' meaning the report did not extract these details from supporting documentation — it asserted their absence. Under the decision logic, 'Material clarification or additional evidence required' arises where 'lost features or guarantees are unaddressed or unevidenced' and 'switching costs are not disclosed.' Here, the loss of safeguarded benefits (claimed as none) is unsupported by any provider documentation. Switching costs are claimed as zero but not evidenced. The guard 'A nil exit penalty confirmed by the provider and left out of the report is not a finding' requires a confirmation of nil; no such confirmation is present. An unmentioned penalty that exists is a finding; here, neither a confirmation of nil nor evidence of penalties is present. The absence of corroborating documentation for all three material claims—no safeguarded benefits, no switching costs, and (for Nest) an employer contribution loss—means the comparison cannot be performed and the claims are unsupported.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). Report recommends 'setting up and registering Power of Attorney' but canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false, creating internal inconsistency about whether PoA is a current arrangement or a future recommendation. Report identifies Tony as 'Informal' third-party support based solely on cohabitation and separate finances, but file contains no evidence of any vulnerability assessment that would establish whether Tony provides or is needed for support, or what the nature of that support is. Report makes recommendations for Will update and Power of Attorney but neither is evidenced by client request, discussion outcome, or vulnerability driver in the file. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report records two support needs: (1) 'You have confirmed you have a valid Will in place, however you have acknowledged that it needs to be updated' under Estate Planning; (2) 'It would also be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney for your financial affairs / health and welfare, to ensure that in the event of your incapacity, your spouse or relative could act on your behalf' under Legal Protection. The report identifies third-party support from Tony (Partner, Informal authority) based on 'You live with your partner Tony but maintain separate financial arrangements.' The canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false. No vulnerability assessment outcome, recorded vulnerabilities, or description of current support arrangements beyond Tony's informal relationship is stated in the report.
    File:     The case file contains: (1) Evidence of the client's background as an accountant with currency trading experience, indicating financial capability [P1, P12]. (2) References to 'recent divorce' and post-divorce financial management [P6, P7]. (3) A meeting summary noting the client's mother 'suffered from Parkinson's and dementia' [P8], establishing a family health history but not a recorded vulnerability affecting the client. (4) Tony is mentioned as the client's partner in meeting notes [P7, P12] with an 'amicable relationship' with ex-husband Paul Annis [P7]. (5) The Fact Find [P21] records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference', indicating no identified communication adjustments needed. (6) The Client Agreement [P23] notes that 'Should you require us to make a reasonable adjustment in providing the advisory services to you, please speak to your financial adviser. Examples of reasonable adjustments may be the provision of a large print format pdf or documents provided in braille,' but the file contains no evidence that the client requested or requires such adjustments. (7) No formal vulnerability assessment, recorded vulnerabilities, or documented support needs beyond the routine legal recommendations appear anywhere in the case file. (8) The Certified PSA [P19] and pension transfer process documentation do not record any third-party authority, Power of Attorney, or support arrangements—only the formal requirement for ex-husband Paul Annis's letter of authority for pension sharing [P20].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report recommends 'setting up and registering Power of Attorney' but canonical model records isPowerOfAttorneyGranted as false, creating internal inconsistency about whether PoA is a current arrangement or a future recommendation.
      - Report identifies Tony as 'Informal' third-party support based solely on cohabitation and separate finances, but file contains no evidence of any vulnerability assessment that would establish whether Tony provides or is needed for support, or what the nature of that support is.
      - Report makes recommendations for Will update and Power of Attorney but neither is evidenced by client request, discussion outcome, or vulnerability driver in the file.
      - Fact Find records 'No Requirement' for accessible format and 'No Preference' for delivery method [P21], but report does not reference these findings or confirm client's communication preferences were discussed.
      - Client's mother's history of Parkinson's and dementia [P8] is recorded in meeting notes but never referenced in the suitability report as a factor in assessing client's own vulnerability, capacity, or comprehension needs.
    The report asserts two forward-looking recommendations (Will update, Power of Attorney) that are presented as prudent planning measures but are not tied to any documented vulnerability, support gap, or client-identified need in the file. These are future-planning recommendations, not evidence of current support arrangements or identified vulnerabilities, consistent with the guard that 'a recommendation to put a power of attorney in place is future planning, not evidence of current third-party support.' The report identifies Tony as providing 'Informal' third-party support solely on the basis of cohabitation ('You live with your partner Tony but maintain separate financial arrangements' [P1]), but the file contains no assessment of what support Tony provides, whether it is needed due to vulnerability, or whether the client has formally authorized Tony to act. This is not evidence of support; it is identification of a cohabiting partner. The Fact Find [P21] confirms the client selected 'No Requirement' for accessible format and 'No Preference' for delivery method, suggesting no identified communication adjustment needs, yet the report does not reference or build on these findings. The client's background as an accountant with currency trading experience [P1, P12] and the statement that 'Your background in accounting and currency trading has equipped you with a solid understanding of financial matters' [P1] indicate capability and comprehension, not vulnerability. The client's mother's history of Parkinson's and dementia [P8] is recorded in meeting context but never assessed as affecting the client's own vulnerability, resilience, or support needs. No vulnerability assessment, recorded vulnerabilities, or documented support arrangements (other than the client agreement's standing offer of reasonable adjustments) appear in the case file. The comparison shows that the report makes recommendations without supporting evidence of identified need, and identifies informal third-party support (Tony) without evidencing assessment of that support's nature or necessity. The absence of a formal vulnerability assessment in a case with recent divorce, family health history context, and significant financial transitions represents a process gap rather than a contradiction, but it means the support needs identified in the report are unsupported by documented assessment.

  [G10.2] Communication preferences and formats
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report (P12, P13, P15, P20) does not contain any explicit statement about the client's communication preferences, accessible format requirements, language needs, or literacy considerations. The canonical model holds no value at the path /vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences, indicating the report is silent on this topic.
    File:     The case file contains communication preference data in the Fact Find (P1): Marketing consent preferences are recorded (mail: yes, email: yes, SMS: no, social media: no, automated calls: no), with consent date 07/01/2026. The Fact Find also records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference'. The Client Agreement (P7) states: 'Communication is integral to any relationship, and we recognise that there are now more ways than ever for us to communicate effectively together. We will liaise with you in a variety of ways, including remote video or telephone meetings, letters, emails, through online communication portals and newsletters. You may at any time choose to change your preferences as to how we communicate with you. Should you require us to make a reasonable adjustment in providing the advisory services to you, please speak to your financial adviser. Examples of reasonable adjustments may be the provision of a large print format pdf or documents provided in braille.' The Meeting Summary (P16) documents a telephone meeting on 21/07/2026 with Morgan Seddon. The Fact Find (P3) confirms 'Type of Meeting: Telephone - Recorded' dated 21/07/2026.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report makes no reference to communication preferences despite preferences being captured in the case file (Fact Find P1). The report does not acknowledge or reference the client's recorded preference for mail and email contact, nor the explicit statement of 'No Requirement' for accessible formats.
      - The report does not document that communication preferences were discussed or agreed, despite the Client Agreement (P7) providing the contractual framework for doing so and stating 'You may at any time choose to change your preferences as to how we communicate with you.'
      - No reference is made in the suitability report to the reasonable adjustments framework described in the Client Agreement, even though the Client Agreement (P7) explicitly invites the client to request such adjustments.
    The check asks whether information in the suitability report relating to communication preferences is fully supported by evidence. The canonical model extraction confirms the report is silent on this topic: /vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences is absent. This is not an extraction failure—the extraction meta-data (expectedButAbsent) does not flag this path as a failure to capture present information. The case file clearly holds communication preference data in the Fact Find (P1), which records marketing contact preferences and accessibility requirements as 'No Requirement'. The Client Agreement (P7) establishes the firm's process for managing communication preferences and reasonable adjustments. The meetings were conducted by telephone (P3, P16), which aligns with the recorded preferences but is not explicitly referenced in the suitability report as an application of those preferences. The report's silence on communication preferences is not in itself a vulnerability concern—a client's standard contact preferences are not vulnerability adjustments and their mere recording does not evidence that vulnerability was assessed (see guard). However, the Fact Find explicitly records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement', meaning a check for accessibility need was performed and a finding of no need was recorded. The suitability report makes no reference to this assessment. Where a systematic assessment is performed and recorded in the file (as here: the Fact Find contains a dedicated field for accessible format requirement), the suitability report should either confirm that no adjustments are needed, or should document any discussion of the client's needs. The absence of any reference in the report to communication preferences, accessibility requirements, or the Client Agreement's framework for reasonable adjustments is a process gap. It is not evidence of a vulnerability that was not assessed; it is evidence that an assessment carried out in the Fact Find was not brought forward into or referenced in the suitability report. This creates a consistency issue: a systematic assessment was performed and documented, but the suitability report does not confirm whether it considered this information or applied it to its recommendations.

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts two adjustments made to support client comprehension: (1) 'You intend to appoint your two adult daughters as your Power of attorney' [stated under DesignatedSupport, page 5, marked as Intended]; (2) 'We have used cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement and to conduct stress testing on your pension projections' [stated under RetirementPlanning, pages 8 and 20, marked as Actual]. The report also records three vulnerability characteristics: recent divorce with Pension Sharing Order (page 5), recent employment status change from employed to self-employed (page 5), and expectation of £350,000 inheritance pending legal proceedings (page 6).
    File:     The case file contains multiple meeting summaries (P7, P9, P14, P18, P21) and a fact find (P5, P8) documenting interactions with Melanie Anness. P4 confirms cashflow analysis was conducted: 'We have also utilised Cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement.' P4 states capacity for loss assessment: 'Based on our conversations and analysis, I have determined that your capacity for loss is moderate, based on the current information we hold. Based on your circumstances alone, your pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement, even following a fall in the market.' P9 and P14 confirm recent divorce: client 'is currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce' and references 'Certified Final Order' (P16, dated 12 November 2025). P7 confirms employment change: client 'at the transitional phase after leaving her job'. P5 records sensitivity to data processing. Meeting summaries (P7, P18, P21) document risk profile assessment at level 7. However, no document in the case file explicitly records that the client intends to appoint daughters as Power of Attorney, nor does any passage evidence that this arrangement was discussed, confirmed, or documented as an adjustment made to support comprehension.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'You intend to appoint your two adult daughters as your Power of attorney' marked as stated on page 5 of the suitability report, but no passage in the case file (including the fact find, meeting summaries, or supporting documents) records this intention, discusses it, or references daughters in a Power of Attorney capacity.
      - Report treats Power of Attorney appointment as an adjustment made to support client comprehension (classified under DesignatedSupport), but no evidence appears that this arrangement was made to support comprehension, or that it was discussed as part of vulnerability or support needs assessment.
    The cashflow analysis adjustment is fully corroborated by P4, which explicitly states 'We have also utilised Cashflow analysis to assess whether you will have sufficient funds for retirement.' This adjustment is supported by the file. The vulnerability characteristics (recent divorce, employment change, bereavement/inheritance) are all documented in the file. The vulnerability indicators trigger this check correctly. However, the Power of Attorney appointment claim stands unsupported. The report's canonical model extracts it from page 5 under 'Your current situation' with the quote 'you intend to appoint them as your Power of attorney', marked as Stated and Intended. Yet no passage in the retrieved evidence (P1-P24) contains this statement or corroborates it. P5 (Fact Find) records data processing consent but does not cover Power of Attorney. The meeting summaries (P7, P9, P14, P18, P21) do not mention this arrangement. The Certified Final Order (P16) does not reference Power of Attorney appointments. The report classifies this as an adjustment made to support client comprehension, but the file holds no evidence that: (1) the client expressed this intention, (2) the adviser discussed it as a support measure, (3) it was agreed, or (4) it relates to supporting comprehension of the financial advice. This is an assertion present in the suitability report that is not corroborated by any document in the case file. The classification of this as a comprehension support adjustment (DesignatedSupport topic) is also not explained by any supporting material. The absence of this evidence in a file containing 64 hits across fact finds, meeting summaries, and supporting legal documents suggests either that this arrangement was not discussed or recorded, or that the suitability report contains information not grounded in the documented advisory process.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies four foreseeable harms with mitigations: (1) market volatility between transfer date and investment could result in financial disadvantage, mitigated by client's liquid cash reserves and comfort with processing delay; (2) no guarantee new provider's funds will outperform, mitigated by cashflow analysis showing sufficient assets even in stress scenarios; (3) high inflation stress test shows assets could deplete by age 81, mitigated by option to continue working or reduce hours, and ONS life expectancy of 87 for 55-year-old female; (4) long-term care costs not factored into projections, mitigated by acknowledgement in cashflow report and suggestion to review insurance separately. The report also records two impacts on recommendation: IHT planning considerations (pensions included in taxable estate from 6 April 2027) and accessibility/transparency through online platform for fund monitoring. The report states capacity for loss is 'moderate' based on cashflow analysis showing pension funds plus inheritance sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after market fall, and that client has option to work beyond age 60 or part-time.
    File:     The case file records: (1) Recent divorce (amicable relationship with ex-husband) with pension sharing order; (2) Recent employment change from employed to self-employed with same company, losing employee benefits; (3) Expected inheritance of £350,000 from mother's property (pending legal proceedings, timeline uncertain); (4) Risk profile assessed as level 7/10 (Growth, adventurous); (5) Capacity for loss questionnaire completed and discussed; (6) Background in accounting and currency trading, medium investment experience, regular fund checking; (7) Estate planning underway - updating will and appointing two daughters as power of attorney; (8) Emergency fund shortfall of £17,000 (£7,000 held vs £24,000 required as 12-month buffer); (9) Currently living off savings which will reduce to circa £4,000 by time pension income is set up, causing discomfort; (10) Personal expenditure in retirement £2,000 net per month (vs £2,237 before retirement); (11) Inheritance timing uncertain due to ongoing legal proceedings; (12) No recorded vulnerability assessment, health conditions, mental capacity concerns, communication adjustments, or support needs recorded in any document.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts capacity for loss is 'moderate' based on cashflow analysis, but file records 'Capacity for Loss Questionnaire' section in risk profile as 'No additional notes were recorded', indicating questionnaire was completed but no notes or substantive assessment recorded
      - Report states client 'is comfortable with the concept of 10-15 working day processing delay' as mitigation for market volatility harm, but no evidence in file of client expressing comfort with this specific timeframe or risk
      - Report cites 'option to continue working beyond age 60 or reduce hours' as mitigation for inflation stress scenario, but file shows client 'expects annual earnings to be around £45,000 gross going forward' and mentions 'may consider part time work beyond this point' - expressed as possibility, not confirmed commitment
      - Report identifies emergency fund shortfall (£17,000 shortfall vs £24,000 required) in context of existing arrangements but does not flag this as a vulnerability indicator or support need despite file showing client feels 'very uncomfortable' about cash position reducing to £4,000
      - Report does not reference or account for employment status change from employed to self-employed with loss of employee benefits, which is material life event affecting resilience
    The report addresses foreseeable harms and investment-related considerations but does not conduct a comprehensive vulnerability overlay aligned with FG21/1 requirements. The file contains several vulnerability drivers that the report does not fully reflect: (1) Recent life event (divorce, employment change) - documented but not analysed as vulnerability factor; (2) Financial resilience concern - emergency fund shortfall and reducing cash position noted in file as causing discomfort, not addressed as support need in report; (3) Inheritance timing uncertainty and legal proceedings - material stressor not discussed as impacting vulnerability or advice delivery; (4) Capacity for loss assessment incomplete - questionnaire completed but 'no additional notes were recorded', suggesting assessment was not substantively documented; (5) The report references 'moderate' capacity for loss based on cashflow sufficiency, but this is capacity measured by asset position rather than personal resilience or comprehension. The guardians apply: Age alone is not vulnerability - client is 55, not elderly - but divorce, employment change, financial stress indicators and inheritance complexity combine to indicate material vulnerability drivers under FG21/1. Vulnerability was not absent and ignored; rather, it was incompletely assessed. The file shows client engagement in estate planning (updating will, appointing daughters as power of attorney) which suggests forward-thinking but may also indicate seeking to arrange affairs amid uncertainty. The report's treatment is defensible on investment suitability grounds (cashflow stress-tested, risk profile agreed, online access provided) but incompletely integrated the vulnerability assessment required by the overlay. The comparison can be performed: evidence exists that shows vulnerability drivers were identified in meetings but not substantively reflected in the report's articulation of how these affect the suitability of the recommendation or the support and communication adjustments needed.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three vulnerability characteristics: (1) 'Recently divorced, hence the Pension Sharing Order' recorded as a life event with assertionStatus 'Stated' and modality 'Actual', stated on page 5 under 'Your current situation'; (2) 'Recently altered employment status, going from employed to self-employed with the same company' recorded as an employment change with assertionStatus 'Stated' and modality 'Actual', stated on page 5; (3) 'Expecting to receive an inheritance of £350,000 from the sale of your late mother's property which is currently pending legal proceedings' recorded as bereavement with assertionStatus 'Stated' and modality 'Intended', stated on page 6. All three are dated 2026-03-24 and the vulnerability is recorded as 'Possible' in category 'LifeEvents'.
    File:     The file contains extensive corroboration of all three characteristics across multiple documents: (1) Divorce: [P8] states 'You are currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce' and 'The relationship between you and your ex-husband, Paul Annis, remains amicable'; [P4] confirms 'You are actively engaged in updating your estate planning following your divorce'; [P6] discusses 'pension sharing orders' and the 'letter of authority' requirement signed by 'your ex-husband, Paul Annis'. (2) Employment change: [P9] records 'Melanie has now stopped working through self employment, she was getting frustrated through work and with disagreements with her boss she decided to cut ties with it all. She will look to return to part time work in the new year...hopefully start part time in the new year with a forecasted wage of £25,000'; [P11] states 'Melanie is in a transitional phase after leaving her job, where her annual income was approximately £45,000'. (3) Inheritance: [P4] states 'you are considering the implications of your potential inheritance from your late mother's property, valued at approximately £1.4 million. Although the timeline for receiving your share is uncertain due to ongoing legal proceedings'; [P9] records 'Potential Inheritance of...£150,000.00...proceedings Estimated to be in 2-3 years time, maybe sooner'; [P12] notes 'her desire to enjoy life after losing her mother, who suffered from Parkinson's and dementia'. The file also records in [P4] that the client 'is in the process of revising your will to reflect your current circumstances' and 'appointing your two daughters as your power of attorneys', indicating support and third-party involvement arrangements are documented. [P19] records that the client may request reasonable adjustments and communication preferences can be changed at any time.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records the inheritance expectation as £350,000, but [P9] records it as £150,000.00, and [P4] values it at 'approximately £1.4 million' (the value of the property, not the client's share). The quantum of inheritance is inconsistent across documents.
      - The report states the inheritance is 'currently pending legal proceedings' with no timeline specified; [P9] estimates 'in 2-3 years time, maybe sooner'; [P4] states timeline is 'uncertain due to ongoing legal proceedings'. The timeline characterisation differs.
      - The report characterises the vulnerability as 'Possible' but records it with modality 'Intended' for the inheritance and 'Actual' for the divorce and employment change. This mixing of modalities within a single vulnerability assessment may indicate inconsistency in how the assessment was conducted.
      - The employment status is described in the report as 'going from employed to self-employed with the same company', but [P9] states 'Melanie has now stopped working through self employment...she decided to cut ties with it all', which indicates she ceased self-employment rather than being currently self-employed. The tense and status are misaligned between report and evidence.
    The three vulnerabilities recorded in the report are all corroborated by evidence in the file. The divorce is confirmed across multiple meeting summaries and fact find documents. The employment change is documented in [P9] and [P11]. The inheritance expectation is documented in [P4] and [P9]. However, material discrepancies exist in the detail: the inheritance value differs significantly (£350,000 in report vs £150,000 in fact find vs £1.4m property value in meeting summary), and the employment status description in the report (currently self-employed) does not match the evidence ([P9] states she has stopped self-employment). The report's characterisation of divorce and employment change as 'Actual' and inheritance as 'Intended' is appropriate given the evidence, though the mixing of assertion statuses suggests the vulnerability assessment may not have been conducted with consistent methodology. The report does not explicitly acknowledge or discuss how these vulnerabilities affect support needs, communication adjustments, or suitability considerations — it records the characteristics but does not articulate how they have shaped the recommendation or what adjustments have been made. The file contains evidence of estate planning activity (will revision, power of attorney appointment to daughters documented in [P4]), and [P19] confirms reasonable adjustments are available, but no specific adjustments or support measures tailored to these vulnerabilities are documented in relation to this particular advice. The file therefore supports that vulnerabilities exist and have been identified, but does not clearly demonstrate that the report's handling of them — in terms of stated support needs, communication preferences, or duty of care measures — is fully supported or consistently applied throughout the advice.
